创造财富

Building Wealth

埃里克：你好，欢迎。自《纳瓦尔宝典》第一次出版，已经过了五年。为了纪念这件事，纳瓦尔和我想为你们做一版特别内容。我们在一起待了一天，录了四个多小时的谈话。现在我们把它分享给你们，更新并扩展书里的那些关键想法。和《纳瓦尔宝典》里的其他一切一样，这个版本也免费提供，除此之外，你还可以在自己常用的平台上听到有声书。这本书已经长成了一种口口相传的现象，被人赠送、被人推荐，最终传到全世界几百万人手里，有 40 种语言。我希望你会帮忙让这些好想法继续传播。嗯，谢谢你们加入。我们开始吧。

Eric: Hello and welcome. It's been 5 years since The Almanack of Naval first came out. And to honor that, Naval and I wanted to do a special edition for you. We spent the day together and recorded over 4 hours of our conversation. Now, we're sharing that here with you, updating and expanding the key ideas in the book. Like everything else in the Almanack of Naval, this version is being made available for free in addition to the audio book available on your favorite platform. This book has grown as a word-of-mouth phenomenon, being gifted and recommended, ultimately reaching millions of people all over the world in 40 languages. I hope you will help these good ideas continue to spread. Uh, thank you for joining us. Let's dive in.

过去几年里，看着你越来越深地进入大卫·多伊奇的思想，至少从我这边看，看到他提出的那些解释，以及你把它们织进你原有的创造财富理论里的那些连接线，还有财富的定义怎样演变、扩展，变得普遍，并且适用——你知道，在文明这个层面上，同一套规则一直向下用到你作为个人所做的决定。

Over the last few years, seeing you go deeper and deeper into David Deutsch's ideas, and at least from my seat, see the explanations that he puts forth and the connecting threads that you draw into your existing theories about building wealth, and how the definition of wealth evolves and expands and becomes universal and applies, you know, at the civilization level, the same rules all the way down to the decisions that you make as an individual.

纳瓦尔：多伊奇对财富的定义比我的更好。我当时对财富的定义，很大程度上被我想赚钱的欲望框住了。所以我当时对财富的定义是：你睡觉时也能替你赚钱的资产。所以我想跳出朝九晚五的陷阱，那种投入和产出绑在一起的陷阱。而且我不想非得辛苦工作。你知道，我以前常说，我希望自己是在生活的每个方面都用最少的功获得最大成功的人。不只是生意上，你知道，甚至包括我的私人关系，甚至包括我日常的幸福感，甚至包括锻炼和工作。我和所有聪明人一样，我懒，对吧？

Naval: Deutsch had a better definition of wealth than I did. My definition of wealth was very focused by my desire to make money. And so my definition of wealth was assets that earn while you sleep. And so I wanted to break out of the 9-to-5 trap, of the input-tied-to-output trap. And I didn't want to have to work hard. You know, I always used to say I want to be the most successful guy for doing the least work in every aspect of my life. Not just in business, but you know, even my personal relationships, even my day-to-day happiness, even my exercise and work. I'm like all smart people, I'm lazy, right?

埃里克：杠杆最高的解法是什么？

Eric: What's the highest-leverage solution?

纳瓦尔：没错。懒是一种杠杆，是一种效率，随你怎么说。而且它说的懒，不是我想坐着什么都不干。只是这样一来我就有时间做别的事。我可以关注自己的健康。我可以花时间和孩子在一起。我可以教育自己。我不只是得当一只埋头干活的工蜂。所以我当时对财富的定义大致是：好，让我们跳出朝九晚五的陷阱。让我的资产在我睡觉时工作。那么哪些资产能在我睡觉时工作？资本是显而易见的一种。你知道，你去投资金钱，但那笔资本实际上在做什么？当你所谓投资资本时，你做的是放弃对未来资产的权利，那些资产是社会因为你过去做过的工作而欠你的——假定那些都是正当挣来的。然后你把这个权利让出去，好让企业能雇人、使用资本设备去建造新东西。

Naval: That's right. Laziness is a form of leverage, efficiency, what have you. And it's not lazy in the sense that I want to sit around and do nothing. It's just that then I have time to do other things. I can focus on my health. I can spend time with my children. I can educate myself. I don't just have to be a drone working away. And so my definition of wealth was around, okay, let's break out of the 9-to-5 trap. Let's have my assets work while I sleep. And what are those assets that can work while I sleep? Well, capital is an obvious one. You know, you invest money, but what is that capital actually doing? When you quote-unquote invest capital, what you're doing is you're giving up the right to future assets that society owed you for having done work in the past, assuming it was all properly earned. And then you're giving that up so that businesses can employ people and use capital machinery to build new things.

嗯，另一种能在你睡觉时赚钱的资产，就是企业的一部分。它字面上可以是一台机器，一台你睡着时还在工作的 3D 打印机。它可以是一台在做计算的计算机。它可以是一簇在计算下一个 AI 模型的 GPU。它可以是一个组织，一群按某种方式运转的人构成的流程，而你偶尔看一眼，确认装配、目标和那些零件是对的，其中一些人在不同时区、不同钟点工作。

Well, another asset that could earn while you sleep is just a piece of a business. It could be literally a machine, a 3D printer working while you're sleeping. It could be a computer doing computation. It could be a GPU cluster computing the next AI model. It could be an organization, a process of people who are operating a certain way, and you kind of check in and make sure the assembly and the goals and all those pieces are correct, and some of them working in different time zones, in different hours.

埃里克：也可以是知识产权。

Eric: Could be intellectual property.

纳瓦尔：可以是知识产权。可以是一段已经出去、还在持续流转的媒体，人们要么为它付钱，要么它给你带来门路、名气，或别的什么。

Naval: It could be intellectual property. It could be a piece of media that's out there that just continues to circulate, and people either pay for it, or it gets you access or notoriety or something else.

埃里克：所以，正是这样。

Eric: So, exactly.

纳瓦尔：所以那就是我当时对财富的定义，而且我觉得，如果你只是想赚钱，这是一个好的实用定义。但是，看，大卫·多伊奇对财富的定义更深，也更哲学。而且它扩展和缩放得非常好，从社会或文明一直到单个的人。他对财富的定义是：你能够造成的那一组物理转化。是 effect，意思是把它带进存在。所以一个例子会是：好，如果我存了很多钱，那我就有财富，因为我可以去买一台机器，那台机器可以简单到是一台在挖沟或盖房的挖掘机，或者我可以雇人去做某件事，诸如此类。所以这里面确实有很大一块是储存起来的资本。但财富更大的部分，仍然是你能够造成的那一组物理转化。而创造物理转化的能力，绝大部分来自技术，来自杠杆化的技术。比如 10 个洞穴人或 10 个旧石器时代的人，不会有和 10 个现代人一样的改变事物的能力。那是因为知识。如果你仔细看，你会意识到知识才是那个大乘数。不是资本。

Naval: So that was my definition of wealth, and I think it's a good practical definition of wealth if you're just trying to make money. See, but David Deutsch's definition of wealth is deeper and more philosophical. And it extends and scales really nicely all the way from a society or civilization down to a single individual. And his definition of wealth is the set of physical transformations that you can affect. Effect, as in bring into existence. So an example of that would be, okay, well if I have a lot of money stored up then I have wealth, because I can go buy a machine, and the machine could be as simple as a backhoe that's digging trenches or building houses, or I can hire people to go do something, or, you know, etc. So there's definitely a huge stored-capital component to it. But the bigger part of wealth is again it's a set of physical transformations that you can affect. And the vast majority of the capability to create physical transformations comes from technology, and it comes from leverage technology. Like 10 cavemen or 10 Paleolithic people wouldn't have the same ability to change things as 10 modern humans do. And that's because of knowledge. If you pay careful attention, you realize that knowledge is the big multiplier. It's not the capital.

所以这是马克思完全错了的一件事。马克思主义彻底失败的许多原因之一——先把那些显而易见的、你知道，错误的激励放在一边——是价值并不在资本里。不在工厂里。在知识里。如果你把埃隆·马斯克从 SpaceX 里拿走，你没法把他的财富捕获下来。它消失了，因为知识就那样消失了。SpaceX 变得不那么值钱。它不是一块拿来切分的饼。它是一群被共同使命聚在一起的非常聪明的人，他们持续地造成改变，而你在资助他们、押注于此。它不是一块金子，你可以切下一角拿走，然后再熔化。顺便说，就连金子本身也没有多少价值。金子没有多少价值。金子是指向实际价值的指针。它只是一种稀缺的金属，你可以用来交换价值。但实际价值不在金子里。价值在于正在做事的人。所以当一个社会获得新知识，它就变得更富有。当一个人获得新知识，他就变得更富有。

So this is one thing where Marx was completely wrong. One of the many reasons why Marxism completely fails—leave aside the obvious, you know, wrong incentives—is that the value is not in the capital. It's not in the factories. It's in the knowledge. If you removed Elon Musk from SpaceX, you can't capture his wealth. It disappears, because the knowledge just disappears. SpaceX is less valuable. It is not a pie to be divided up. It is a group of very intelligent people held together by a common mission who are continually affecting change, and you're funding them and betting on that. It's not a piece of gold that you can slice off a chunk and take it and then melt it down. Even that, by the way, doesn't have much value. Gold doesn't have much value. Gold is a pointer to actual value. It's just a scarce metal you can use to trade value. But the actual value is not in the gold. The value is in the people doing things. So as a society gains new knowledge, it becomes wealthier. As an individual gains new knowledge, they become wealthier.

你知道，是的，比方说，按常规标准我是富有的，但我的赚钱能力也处在有生以来最高的时候。为什么？因为我有极多的知识，而且人们知道我有知识，所以因此我能在非常大的层面上造成改变。我因为什么出名——因为做我的那些创业公司？不，并不真是。我最引以为傲的是什么——创业公司？我不知道。创业公司来了又走，你知道。我为此自豪，是因为我曾经作为一个在场上的人，真正做成过一些事。你知道，我似乎因此受欢迎的，只是把事情想清楚，然后用某种方式说出来，而这就是知识，对吧？而这把我带回我们先前谈的，你知道，永恒的知识和智慧，以及某种现代知识。现代知识，比如关于事物的知识，关于怎样造汽车、计算机、机器人以及诸如此类的知识。那种知识是可传递的。一个人可以发现它，他们可以把它传达给下一个人。

You know, yes, for example, I am wealthy by conventional standards, but my earning power is also the highest it's ever been. Why? Because I have tremendous knowledge, and people have knowledge that I have knowledge, and so because of that, I can affect change at a very big level. What do I get famous for—for doing my startups? No, not really. What am I proudest of—startups? I don't know. Startups come and go, you know. I'm proud of it in the sense that I got to be a man in the arena and I got to actually do something. You know, what I seem to have gotten popular for is just for thinking through and then articulating things in a certain way, and that's knowledge, right? And this brings me back to earlier we were talking about, you know, timeless knowledge and wisdom and kind of modern knowledge. Modern knowledge, like knowledge about things, knowledge how to build cars and computers and robots and all that. That knowledge is transmissible. One person can discover it, they can convey it to the next person.

下一个人然后可以，你知道，把它接过去，复制它、拷贝它。这让每个人都更富有。关于人性的知识，关于什么是好的生活、生命的目的是什么的知识。你知道，就像亚里士多德说的，“Dudemania”——或者我这么说是错的。我不会拉丁语，也不会希腊语。

The next person can then, you know, take it on and duplicate and copy it. It makes everybody wealthier. Knowledge about human nature, knowledge about what is a good life, what is the purpose of life. You know, as Aristotle said, “Dudemania”—or I’m saying that wrong. I don’t speak Latin or Greek.

埃里克：元音太多了。

Eric: It's a lot of vowels.

纳瓦尔：对，元音很多，但它基本上就是，你知道，你怎样过一种幸福的生活？那是一种非常实用的哲学。那种知识、那种智慧很难传达。智慧非常简单。如果智慧可以被传达，我们大家就都会有智慧，事情也就做完了。但它总是必须和个人经验缝在一起，并且必须在听的人内部被重新创造出来。你不能只是复制。它不是一个你可以复制的流程。所以智慧是一种你需要一遍又一遍再一遍听到的东西。而且你需要以一千种不同的方式、在一千种不同的情境里听到它，直到你走过自己的生活。然后你做了某件事，然后它一下子对上了，你就像：哦，这人用这种特别的方式说过那个，而它在这个瞬间和我共鸣。然后你把它想清楚，把它缝进你的世界观。它变成你价值系统的一部分。而问题是，如果你已经以某种方式听过它，要么太早，要么次数太多，它就成了陈词滥调，每个人都会翻白眼，对吧？

Naval: Yeah, it's a lot of vowels, but it's basically like, you know, how do you live a happy life? Like that's a very practical philosophy. That knowledge, that wisdom is very hard to convey. Wisdom is very simple. If wisdom could be communicated, we'd all be wise and we'd all be done. But it always has to be stitched in with personal experience, and it has to be recreated inside the listener. You cannot just copy. It's not a process that you can copy. So wisdom is something that you need to hear over and over and over again. And you need to hear it in a thousand different ways, in a thousand different contexts, until you're going through your own life. And then you do something, and then it clicks, and you're like, oh, this guy said that in this particular way, and that resonates with me in this moment. And then you think it through and you stitch it into your worldview. It becomes part of your value system. And the problem is, if you've already heard it said a certain way either too early or too many times, it's a cliché and everyone's going to roll their eyes, right?

所以，我觉得世界上一些最了不起的智慧就在童谣里，对吧？就像 row, row, row your boat。你去找一段比这更了不起的智慧给我看看。很难。Row, row, row your boat gently down the stream，对吧？Merrily, merrily, merrily, my life is but a dream。那里面有很多智慧。生命像一条河，对吧？它多少在流动。它以某种节奏流动。你确实想划桨。你不想只是坐在那儿。你想温和地划。你不想水花溅得太猛。你想保持一个好态度。快活地，生命是一场梦，因为整件事都会消失，而你也走了，对吧？所以那里面有很多智慧。而且它用一种巧妙的方式被包装起来，靠着押韵穿越时间保留下来，你甚至可以用简单的词把它讲给孩子听。但与此同时，如果我把它当作智慧写在一本书里交给你，你会只是笑，因为它太陈词滥调了。它太简单了。你听得太早了。

So like one of the things I think some of the greatest wisdom in the world is in nursery rhymes, right? Like row, row, row your boat. Like find me a greater piece of wisdom than that. It's very hard. Row, row, row your boat gently down the stream, right? Merrily, merrily, merrily, my life is but a dream. There's a lot of wisdom in that. Life is like a river, right? It is kind of flowing. It's flowing at a certain pace. You do want to row. You don't want to just sit there. You want to be gentle about it. You don't want to splash around too hard. You want to keep a good attitude. Merrily, life is a dream, 'cause the whole thing disappears and you're gone, right? So there's a lot of wisdom in there. And it's packaged in a clever way that it persists across time with rhyming, and you can even communicate it to children in simple words. But at the same time, if I gave that to you as wisdom in a book, you would just laugh, 'cause it's too cliché. It's too simple. You heard it too early.

在某种程度上，是的，我觉得那些所谓纳瓦尔的批评者，最常见的批评就像：哦，这是幸运饼干，或者这是陈词滥调，或者人人都知道。而我的回应是：那就是林迪。就像，是的，陈词滥调就是林迪。我在那里试图做的全部——而且我主要是说给自己听的，所以即便我改自己的推文，因为我觉得换一种说法会比原来的措辞更可能让我记住——就是我想用一种有意思的方式说出某种真实的东西。字面上就是这样。即便那个真理以前被说过，而任何关于人性的真理以前都已经被说过，它按定义就是重复，我也不介意重复它，因为这是一种有意思的方式，而我刚刚用这种方式弄明白了它，它就会粘在我身上。也许那里另外某个人，当我这样说时，也会以一种会粘在他们身上的方式弄明白。更有意思的是，也许他们会回过来说些什么，让我重新评估并扩展我刚刚想到的东西。也许我会遇见某个有意思的人。这就是我发推的原因。

In some ways, yeah, I feel like the Naval detractors, quote-unquote, the most common criticism is like, oh, it's fortune-cookie, or it's a cliché, or everybody knows that. And my response is like, that's Lindy. Like yeah, cliché is Lindy. All I'm trying to do there is—and I'm saying it mostly for myself, so even when I edit my tweets, 'cause I think I'm more likely to remember it in a different way than I originally phrased it—is I'm trying to say something true in an interesting way. That's literally it. And even if the truth has been said before, and any truth about human nature has been said before, it is a repetition by definition, I don't mind repeating it, because it's in an interesting way, and I just figured it out in such a way that it's going to stick with me. And maybe someone else there will figure it out in a way that'll stick with them when I say it this way. And more interestingly, maybe they'll say something back which will cause me to re-evaluate and expand what I just thought. And maybe I'll meet someone interesting. That's why I tweet.

埃里克：对。

Eric: Yeah.

纳瓦尔：所以任何涉及并非全新、全新话题的东西，都会变成陈词滥调。所以如果你只想盯着全新的话题，你还剩下什么？嗯，你剩下的就是当代故事讲述，比如金·卡戴珊早餐吃了什么，或者唐纳德·特朗普今天在和最高法院大法官争什么政策，诸如此类。对我来说，那些东西有娱乐性，但如果我们就只谈这些，那就请离开。就像，我完全不觉得那对我的生活有任何增益。或者我们可以谈新的事情。我们可以谈某个 AI 模型里最新的突破。我们可以谈图像生成的最新方法，或者谈人们建造无人机的最新方式，诸如此类，但只有非常非常少的人有资格谈那些。他们未必会把那些突破都放上 Twitter。你也不是每次都在那儿吸收深度技术信息。我们大多数人也分不清什么是真的、什么不是，什么是炒作、什么是假的。所以我觉得，如果你要讨论任何重要而永恒的事情，你绝对会撞上陈词滥调。

Naval: And so anything having to do with any topic that is not brand brand new is going to be clichéd. So if you want to only stick to brand-new topics, what are you left with? Well, you're left with contemporary storytelling, like what did Kim Kardashian have for breakfast, or what policies Donald Trump is fighting with the Supreme Court justice or whatever over today. To me, that stuff is entertaining, but if that's all we're going to talk about, then please leave. Like, I don't find that additive to my life in any way. Or we can talk about things that are new. We can talk about the latest breakthrough in an AI model. We can talk about the latest way to do image generation, or we can talk about the latest way that, you know, people are building drones or what have you, but only a very, very small number of people are qualified to talk about that. They're not necessarily putting all those breakthroughs on Twitter. Nor are you there to absorb deep technical information each time. Nor can most of us tell apart what's real from what's not, and what's hype and what's fake. So I feel like if you're going to discuss anything important and timeless, you are absolutely going to run into clichés.

埃里克：对。大卫·多伊奇，财富的定义，和创造财富的伦理连在一起。我觉得那是一点——看见知识、财富和合乎伦理的财富创造之间的连接，是一次巨大的开锁，因为我觉得那也许是让人在内化这一串其余想法之前就卡住的最大前因：如果你不相信合乎伦理的财富创造是可能的，你就会拒绝整场游戏。

Eric: Yeah. David Deutsch, the definition of wealth connected with the ethics of wealth creation. I think that was one—seeing the connections between knowledge, wealth, and ethical wealth creation was a huge unlock, 'cause I think that's maybe the biggest precursor that gets people stuck before even internalizing the rest of the ideas in the thread: if you don't believe ethical wealth creation is possible, you will reject the whole game.

纳瓦尔：没错。是的。你会过早地变得苦涩。

Naval: That's right. Yes. You will get bitter too early.

埃里克：对。

Eric: Yeah.

纳瓦尔：而且你知道，人们那样做的时候挺糟的。就像对资本主义常见的批评，全是围绕裙带、或者某种，你知道，美联储印钱，或者给银行家送好处，诸如此类。毫无疑问，当金钱卷入、当财富卷入，不只是一部分努力，而是大部分努力，都会进入抽走、控制、保卫、争夺、切分财富。是大部分。如果你看自然界，寄生的物种数量是那种多少独立存活的物种的六倍，对吧？所以大多数东西都在互相吞吃，试图靠别的东西活着。只有少数像是伸向天上的太阳，你知道，试图存活。所以对人们来说，去抓取某样东西似乎总是比去创造某样东西更容易。而当一个社会最终有太多人专注于切分饼，而不是烤饼，那些社会就会崩溃。变得犬儒很容易。我是说，你看着那些银行救助。你多少会看。但我会争辩，那些没有一样是自由市场资本主义。那完完全全是政府干预。你知道，挑选赢家、挑选输家，这并不是说资本主义意味着每个人都公平竞争。嗯，资本主义是你拿出一套最小结构的规则，那个最小可行的规则，让人们能玩这场公平的游戏，从而许多本来会被导向偷盗财产或互相争斗的能量，被导入创造财产。所以这就是为什么资本主义的基础是尊重私有财产。如果我改良了一块地，或者我创造了某样东西，我可以保有那份改良。是的，你必须想清楚如何处理污染的外部性，以及公共物品，诸如此类。但你知道，基本的自由市场资本主义在相当大的程度上把这些想明白了，并且它造成了历史上财富和人类繁荣的最大增长。

Naval: And you know, it kind of sucks when people do that. Like the common critiques of capitalism are all around cronyism, or sort of, you know, the Federal Reserve money printing, or handing out favors to bankers and so on. There's no question, when money is involved, when wealth is involved, not just a portion of the efforts, but the majority of efforts will go into siphoning, controlling, defending, fighting over, slicing up wealth. The majority. If you look in nature, there are six times as many species that are parasites as are sort of standalone, right? So most things are eating each other and trying to live off of something else. And there's a few that are like reaching for the sun in the sky and, you know, trying to survive. And so it just always seems easier to people to grab something than to create something. And when society ends up with too many people focused on slicing up the pie than baking it, then those societies collapse. And it's easy to be cynical. I mean, you watch the bank bailouts. You kind of watch. But I would argue none of that is free-market capitalism. That is government intervention through and through. You know, picking winners, picking losers, which is not to say that capitalism means everybody plays fair. Well, capitalism is you come up with a minimum structured set of rules, the minimum viable that allows people to play the fair game, where a lot of the energy that would get directed into stealing property or fighting each other gets channeled into creating property. So that's why the basis of capitalism is respect for private property. If I improve a piece of land or if I create something, I can keep that improvement. And yes, you have to figure out how to deal with the externalities of pollution and with public goods and so on and so forth. But you know, basic free-market capitalism figured that out to a pretty large extent, and has been responsible for the greatest increase in wealth and, you know, human flourishing in history.

而且人们忘了，你知道，他们没有花足够的时间去看那些人靠一天一美元生活、或者没有能力保护私有财产的地方。那些社会就会直接熔化成彻头彻尾的战争。

And people forget that, you know, they don't spend enough time looking at places where people are living on a dollar a day or don't have the ability to protect private property. Like those societies just melt down into full-on just warfare.

埃里克：我们不会去想一种资本主义质量分数之类的东西。

Eric: We don't think about like a capitalism quality score.

纳瓦尔：没错。就应该是那样。就像美国现在大概有三分之一或 40% 是社会主义的，因为 GDP 里有那么大一块就是由政府控制的。然后还有州、联邦和地方政府。然后你还有另外 20% 或 30% 被监管抽走，或处在教育、医疗这类基本上准政府运营的部门。所以即便在这里，私人部门也相当小，而且在缩小，问题就像：那一层还能扛多少负荷，才到人们放弃、或者逃走；实际上美国的失败情形是最优秀、最聪明的人不再移民到这里。所以我确实觉得，你可以盯着这一切。你可以盯着这一切的不公平，然后就在那里熔化、崩溃。但与此同时，这是人类历史上创造财富最了不起的时期。被创造的知识比任何时候都多。被创造的资本比任何时候都多。被创造的杠杆比任何时候都多。所以，如果你有中等智力，不怕努力工作，而且灵活，你可以做得极好。而你多少必须把“整场游戏都被操纵了”这个想法从脑子里拿出去。

Naval: That's right. That's what it should be. Like the US is what, like one-third or 40% now socialist, because that's how much of GDP is just controlled by the government. And then there's by state, federal, and local governments. And then you've got another 20 or 30% that is drawn by regulation, or it's in sectors like education, healthcare, which are basically quasi-government-run. So even here the private sector is quite small and shrinking, and it's just like how much of a load can that carry before, you know, people give up or they flee, or actually the failure case for the US is the best and brightest no longer immigrate here. So I do think like you can focus on all that. You can focus on the unfairness of it all, and you can melt down and break down right there. But at the same time, this is the greatest period for wealth creation in human history. There's more knowledge being created. There's more capital being created. There's more leverage being created than any other time. So, if you're moderately intelligent and you're not afraid of working hard and you're flexible, you can do extremely well. And you kind of have to get it outside of your mind that the whole game is rigged.

埃里克：对。

Eric: Yeah.

纳瓦尔：这就像，听着，在生活里，有很多运气。你甚至存在这个事实就是运气。你知道，有很多因素不在你的控制里，但生命很长。人是非常一致的。复利确实管用。而你可以通过纯粹的意志力和努力工作，越过运气升上去。如果你不相信那个，那是的，你就是一个犬儒。而犬儒，他们的信念会自我实现。你会陷在泥里。然而，那些乐观、愿意努力工作、并且愿意越过这一切不伦理或基于运气的性质往前看的人，他们才是唯一有机会的人。至少我的经验是，那些人在长的时间尺度上会做得好。所以那也许是 10 到 30 年。那不会是两年。没有快速致富的方案。你知道，那只是有人在卖给你东西。所以在足够长的时间尺度上，你可以从泥沼里升出来。但这从相信它是可能的开始。如果你不认为它是可能的，那对你来说它就的确不可能。而且我觉得这是你会在伟大创业者身上注意到的一个共同主题：史蒂夫·乔布斯和埃隆·马斯克，你知道，他们承担那些他们认为不可能的任务，因为他们多少环顾四周，说，好，如果别人能做，我为什么不能？所以我觉得那种心态极其重要。

Naval: It's just like, look, in life, there is a lot of luck. The fact that you even exist is luck. You know, there's a lot of factors out of your control, but life is long. People are very consistent. Compounding does work. And you can rise past the luck just through sheer force of will and hard work. And if you don't believe in that, then yes, you're a cynic. And cynics, their beliefs are self-fulfilling. You will be stuck in the mud. However, people who are optimistic, willing to work hard, and willing to look past the unethical or luck-based nature of it all, they're the only ones who have a chance. And at least my experience has been that those people do well on long time scales. So it might be 10 to 30 years. It's not going to be two years. There's no get-rich-quick schemes. You know, that's just someone selling you something. So on a long enough time scale you can rise out of the muck. But it starts with believing that it's possible. If you don't think it's possible then yes it's impossible for you. And I think this is a common theme you will notice across the great entrepreneurs, that Steve Jobs and Elon Musk, you know, they take on what they consider to be impossible tasks because they kind of look around, they say, well, if other people can do it, why can't I? So I think that mindset is super important.

我喜欢多伊奇的另一点，是他对此有一种非常乐观的看法。那不只是一种信念。他确实用好的解释把它组织起来，说明为什么人类进步会发生。他说我们应该乐观，因为我们在英国启蒙运动里发现了这个模型，那是他们的哲学运动，我们基本上弄明白了：我们可以通过对好解释的探求来推进科学和社会，这意味着我们在求真。我们在做猜想。那些猜想对批评开放。每个猜想都可以被批评，然后我们把它们拿到实验前。我们拿理论和理论互相检验。我们找出什么管用，无论什么管用，我们就把它流转、颁布给其他人，然后我们把社会往前推进。而且他已经表明，这就是我们过去几百年一直在运作的方式。这样的时段过去也存在过，也许在意大利文艺复兴期间，也许在希腊那种启蒙年代，也许从工业革命往后。而我们如果失去这个，或把它熄灭，会是愚蠢的——你知道，靠着反理性，或说不存在真理这种东西，或放弃对好解释的探求，靠着禁止批评。那就是，你知道，审查所做的事，或者靠着相信某些人，比如，你知道，大科学总是有答案。所以只要我们不放开这种前进的方法，就有理由乐观。而这种由猜想和批评导向好解释的方法，才是真正的科学方法。

What I like about Deutsch also is that he has a very optimistic view on this. It's not just a belief. He really does structure it in good explanations as to why human progress happens. And he says we should be optimistic because we discovered this model in the British Enlightenment, which was their philosophical movement, where we basically figured out that we can advance science and society through the quest for good explanations, which means that we are truth-seeking. We're making conjectures. Those conjectures are open to criticism. Every conjecture can be criticized, and then we hold them up to experiment. We test theories against each other. We find out what works, and whatever works we circulate and promulgate to other people, and then we advance society forward. And he's shown that that is how we've been operating for the last few hundred years. And time periods like this have existed in the past, maybe during the Italian Renaissance, maybe during the Greek sort of enlightenment age, maybe during the Industrial Revolution onwards. And we would be foolish to lose that or extinguish that, you know, by being anti-rational, or saying there's no such thing as truth, or by giving up the quest for good explanations, by banning criticism. That's, you know, what censorship does, or by believing that certain people, like, you know, big science always has the answers. So there is reason to be optimistic as long as we don't let go of this method of advancing. And this method of conjecture and criticism leading to good explanations is the real scientific method.

你知道，我们小时候被教以科学方法。那并不是它真正的运作方式。那像一个非常理想化的虚构，试管实验室版本。但现实是，在正确的环境里，给定正确的自由，我们在生活的每件事上不断地科学。每个人都在试图改善自己的生活。所以每个在乎健康和体格的人，都是一个小科学家，在跑各种实验：哪种饮食对我管用，哪种锻炼对我管用。而且，你知道，他们不断地提出新假说。他们听到新假说。他们尝试新东西。他们看什么管用、什么不管用。所以他们以自己的方式在做同一件事。而所有求真的系统都这样运作。所以在科学里你可以称之为猜想和批评。在技术和商业里你可以称之为，你知道，创新，以及那些公司倒闭，对吧？公司创造东西，失败的那些就倒闭。在进化里你可以称之为，你知道，突变。基因发生突变。然后是选择：那些没能，你知道，没能过关的，就被从基因库里真正移除。

You know, we get taught the scientific method when we're kids. That's not really how it works. That's like a very idealized fiction, test-tube laboratory version. But the reality is that under the right circumstances, given the right freedoms, we're constantly scientific about everything in our lives. Every person is trying to improve their life. So everyone who cares about being healthy and fit is a little scientist running all the different experiments of which kind of diet works for me, which kind of exercise works for me. And, you know, they're constantly making new hypotheses. They're hearing new hypotheses. They're trying new things. They're seeing what worked and what didn't. So they're doing the same thing in their own way. And all truth-seeking systems work this way. So in science you can call it conjecture and criticism. In technology and business you can call it, you know, innovation and just the companies going out of business, right? Companies create things and the ones that fail go out of business. In evolution you can call it, you know, mutation. The genes mutate. And then selection: the ones that didn't, you know, didn't cut it are literally removed from the gene pool.

但所有求真的工作系统都这样运作，也就是我们做出猜想，要么是像突变里那样的随机猜想，要么是像科学里那样的有意猜想，或通过技术创新或商业创新，然后有一个过滤过程。甚至塔勒布用一种完全不同的方式谈这个。他有一整套“自己也要下注”（skin in the game）的说法。而自己也要下注并不只是，哦，我自己的钱压在里面。自己也要下注是：如果我没有成功，随着时间我会被切出去。如果我承担风险，我就应当承受风险。他认为坏的资本主义是别人承受风险。你知道，那是那些银行流氓做的事。好的资本主义是我承担风险，然后我承受那个风险的后果或回报。所以对塔勒布来说，你能做的最伦理的事就是做一个风险承担者。你去做创业者，你去承受风险。不要把它转嫁给别人或社会。然后这种自己也要下注，并不是来自你曾经有过一些赌注这个事实，而是随着时间，那些糟糕的风险承担者被剔除了，他们要么失去了资本，要么失去了生命，要么失去了名声。而那些好的风险承担者最终获益，然后把他们得到的好处扩散给社会其余的人。所以甚至他“自己也要下注”的说法，也多少和多伊奇的猜想与批评是同构的。

But all truth-seeking work systems work this way, which is we make conjectures, either random conjectures like in mutation or deliberate conjectures like in science or through technology innovation or business innovation, and then there is a filtering process. And even Taleb talks about this in a completely different way. He has this whole skin-in-the-game thing. And skin in the game isn't just merely, oh, I have my own money at stake. Skin in the game is I get cut out over time if I don't succeed. If I take risk, I should bear the risk. That's what he considers bad capitalism, when other people bear a risk. You know, that's what banksters do. Good capitalism is when I take a risk and then I bear the consequences or the rewards of that risk. Which is why, to Taleb, the most ethical thing you can do is be a risk-taker. You be an entrepreneur, you go and you bear risk. Don't pass it off to other people or society. And then the skin in the game arises not from the fact that you had some skin in the game, but that over time the people who were bad risk-takers got weeded out, and they either lost capital, they lost their lives, or they lost their reputations. And the people who were good risk-takers ended up benefiting and then spreading the benefits that they gained to the rest of society. So even his skin-in-the-game phrasing is somewhat isomorphic to Deutsch's conjecture and criticism.

埃里克：对，我觉得这就是为什么问责制是对“创业者承担风险”的一次如此重要的重框，因为问责制把这个烤了进去：你亲自承受上行和下行。你不想为了风险而风险。你想显眼地、直接地、公开地承担一种风险。

Eric: Yeah, I think that's why accountability was such an important reframe from entrepreneurs take risk, because accountability bakes in this: you personally bear the upside and the downside. You don't want risk for risk's sake. You want to conspicuously, directly, and publicly take a risk.

纳瓦尔：对。这就是为什么我觉得巴菲特和芒格对投资银行上市有一点反对，因为投资银行曾经是合伙企业、有限责任公司，个体合伙人会承担风险。有限责任公司对你的保护也就那样。如果合伙人做了坏事，风险可以贯通到他们身上。反过来，一旦你作为一家巨大的、没有面孔的公司上市，你就可以躲在 CEO 后面。你可以待在董事会里，你知道，他们可以承担足以让社会破产的风险。社会必须救助他们，因为他们多少加了杠杆，并把整个金融系统置于风险之中。

Naval: Yeah. This is why I think Buffett and Munger were a little opposed to investment banks going public, because investment banks used to be partnerships, LLCs, where the individual partners would take on risk. LLCs only protect you so much. If the partners do something bad, you can have risk flow through to them. On the flip side, once you go public as a big faceless corporation, you can hide behind the CEO. You can be on the board, and, you know, they can take risks that can bankrupt society. Society has to bail them out because they kind of levered up and they put the whole financial system at risk.

但不知怎的，他们自己仍然拿到报酬，安然脱身，还能保住他们的资产，他们的个人资产。他们没事。所以这就是通过公司责任盾，把风险推给社会。

But somehow they themselves still get paid and walk scot-free and get to keep their assets, their personal assets. They're fine. So it's this pushing risk off into society through a corporate liability shield.

埃里克：我觉得，你知道，巴菲特和芒格指出过那不是一件好事。是的，芒格是一个很棒的——你知道，那些让不伦理的行为变得不可能的人，是文明里一些有效的圣人，对吧？所以他谈复式记账、谈收银机，诸如此类。

Eric: I think, you know, Buffett and Munger pointed out that that was not a good thing. Yeah, Munger is a great—you know, the people who make unethical behavior impossible are some of the effective saints of the civilization, right? So he talks about double-entry bookkeeping and a cash register and so on.

纳瓦尔：对。我是说，问题是“不伦理”——这是一条模糊的线，对吧？不同的人有不同的道德。当你自己不在场上做任何事、不和任何东西搏斗、不建造任何东西时，做道德的人更容易，因为那样你就可以直接骑上道德高马。所以学者和记者可以假装自己是最道德的人，而他们其实什么也不做，对吧？所以反过来你有，比如在 AI 竞赛里，你知道，人们吞下所有受版权保护的内容，然后后来说：哎呀，你知道，抱歉，我把你的作业吃了又吐了出来。所以这是一场困难的谈话。它不像一个放之四海而皆准的油滑答案。

Naval: Yeah. I mean, the problem is unethical—it's a fuzzy line, right? Different people had different morality. It's easier to be moral when you're not in the ring yourself doing anything or fighting anything or building anything, 'cause then you can just get on your moral high horse. So academics and journalists can pretend to be the most moral people when they don't actually do anything, right? So on the flip side you have, like for example in the AI race, you know, people are swallowing up all the copyrighted content, then later saying, oops, you know, sorry, I ate your homework and spit it back out. So this is a difficult conversation. It's not like a one-size-fits-all glib answer.

埃里克：有没有这样一种感觉：围绕我们如何操持资本主义的伦理和道德，就像是进步的瓶颈？

Eric: Is there a sense where ethics and morals around how we conduct capitalism is like the bottleneck of progress?

纳瓦尔：你是在说，伦理或道德是进步的瓶颈吗？

Naval: You're saying, are ethics or morals a bottleneck to progress?

埃里克：对。就像我们思考资本主义有多有效，和有多少人是创造者、有多少人是索取者之间的关系。对。而且有文化，有监管，有这么多输入会进到那里。

Eric: Yeah. As we think about like how effective capitalism is in relation to how many people are makers versus takers. Yeah. And there's culture and there's regulation and there are so many inputs to that.

纳瓦尔：对，有的。我是说，我觉得实际上进步最大的障碍，信不信由你，我觉得是规模，对吧？

Naval: Yeah, there are. I mean, I think actually the biggest impediment to progress, I think, believe it or not, I think it's size, right?

埃里克：什么的规模？

Eric: Of what?

纳瓦尔：机构的规模，国家的规模，群体的规模，因为你拥有的群体越大，你就有越多的群体思维。而且群体不承认错误。群体不寻求真理。群体寻求共识。只有个人，或非常小的团队，才能寻求真理。大群体必须有共识，否则它们就散掉。它们彼此争斗，并且退化。所以群体不改变想法。群体不道歉。你知道，那是暴民。所以我觉得最好的情形来自于：你有一些小群体，他们各自既对收益负责，也对损失负责。他们自己也要下注，并且能够承担风险。所以比如在宗教里，你知道，你想看到当年欧洲有几百种宗教、各自尝试自己那一套的时候，你知道他们来到新世界，然后你多少能看见哪种管用、哪种不管用。在技术和商业里，你想看到许多城邦有不同的政策。你想看到许多公司彼此竞争。所以当人类是互相竞争的小群体时，我们做得最好。

Naval: Size of institutions, size of countries, size of groups, because the larger the group you have, the more groupthink you have. And groups don't admit mistakes. Groups don't search for truth. Groups search for consensus. Only individuals or very small teams of people can search for truth. Large groups have to have consensus or they fall apart. They fight with each other and they degenerate. So groups don't change their minds. Groups don't apologize. You know, it's mobs. And so I think the best situation comes from when you have small groups of people who are each responsible for both the benefits and the losses. They have skin in the game that are able to take risks. So for example in religion, you know, you want to see back in Europe when it was hundreds of religions and they were each trying their own thing, and you know they came to the New World and then you kind of got to see which worked and which didn't. In technology and in business, you want to see lots of city-states with different policies. You want to see lots of companies competing with each other. So humans do best when we're small groups competing.

但这里的问题是，竞争会裂成战争。所以宗教，你知道，当它们彼此相邻时，要为许多流血负责。这些小城邦总是最终打起来，并被合并。这些小生意，你知道，最终被更大的吞掉，或者当其中一个最终掌权时会出现垄断效应。所以一个理想社会真正理解怎样联邦化，怎样创造出许许多多小的、互相竞争的——但不是物理动能意义上的竞争，而是虚拟的竞争——弄清楚怎样驾驭那全部能量，那种竞争能量，去创造了不起的东西。所以美国受益于做联邦主义者，受益于有 50 个州、50 套不同的监管系统，以及 50 个，你知道，在治理层面上跑着的实验，它也受益于资本主义，也就是许多小公司在竞争。但崩溃发生在：你只有少数几家大垄断，或者你有像已经接管了政府的大银行，或者政府在运营教育和医疗，或者你最终变成 50 个州只被允许在小事上竞争，却有那些三字母机构和巨大的联邦税收从华盛顿运营一切。所以那就是系统开始崩溃的时候。所以我觉得最繁荣的系统，是那些你有小国家、有小部落、有小公司以非暴力方式彼此竞争的系统。而整个诀窍是：你怎样让它们各自保持主权，并且不把它变成动能、在物理上互相碾压。

But the problem with this is that competition breaks into warfare. So religions, you know, responsible for a lot of bloodshed when they're next to each other. These little city-states always end up fighting and being consolidated. These little businesses, you know, they end up being swallowed up by larger ones, or there's monopoly effects when one of them ends up in charge. So an ideal society really understands how to federalize, how to create lots and lots of small competing—but not in a physical kinetic competition, but in a virtual competition—figures out how to harness all of that energy, that competitive energy, to create great things. And so the US benefited from being federalist, from having 50 states with 50 different regulatory systems and 50 different, you know, experiments being run on the governance level, and it benefits from capitalism, which is lots of small companies competing. But where it breaks down is when you have a small number of big monopolies, or you have like big banks that have taken over the government, or the government running education and healthcare, or when you end up with all 50 states only being allowed to compete in small things, but you have three-letter agencies and giant federal taxes running everything from DC. So that's when the system starts breaking down. So I think that the most flourishing systems are the ones where you have small countries, you have small tribes, and you have small companies competing against each other in a nonviolent way. And the whole trick is how do you keep them each sovereign, and from not making it kinetic and overrunning each other physically.

对我来说，那会是最道德的系统，因为你没法让我们所有人就什么是道德、什么是伦理达成一致，对吧？那是一件不可能的事。这就是为什么整套 AI 对齐的事是一个笑话。我们连人类都对齐不了。我们要怎样对齐 AI？所有 AI 对齐在实践里真正归结成的，就是那个 AI 的所有者或发明者或创造者，你知道，想给它套上绳子，告诉它该做什么。

To me, that would be the most moral system, because you can't get us all to agree on what's moral and what's ethical, right? That's an impossible thing. That's why the whole AI alignment thing is a joke. We can't even align humans. How are we going to align AIs? All AI alignment really boils down to, in practice, is the owner or inventor or creator of that AI, you know, wants to put it on a leash and tell it what to do.

埃里克：对，当我们只聚焦于我们自己、我们控制的东西、以及我们为自己做的决定时，和人多少对齐是很容易的。可一旦我开始——我觉得在道德和伦理上有必要控制你做什么——现在突然之间，我们就在道德和伦理上有了真正的分歧。

Eric: Yeah, it's very easy to be sort of aligned with people when we have a focus only on what we ourselves and what we control and decisions that we make for ourselves. But as soon as I start to—I think it's morally and ethically necessary to control what you do—now all of a sudden we have a real disagreement about morals and ethics.

纳瓦尔：没错。有激励对齐，也就是，你知道，我可以在同一门生意里给你股权，然后我们就激励对齐了。尽管你仍可能选择忽略那个激励，去做别的事。但其他所有形式的对齐，往往都归结为强制。对吧？也许我们对齐，是因为我们属于同一宗教。我们属于同一部落。我们住在同一屋檐下。我们一起做同一门生意。可当你谈的是对齐那些并不住在隔壁、没有亲缘的陌生人时，我觉得你能对齐他们的唯一办法，就是用棍子打其中一个人直到他们付钱。你知道，他们听——那只是威权。那只是武力。那是暴力。那是支撑一切的战争。就像社会运行在一层薄薄的文明表面之上，但最终在底下，拿枪的人永远主管。有时他们会忘记这一点，一次忘十年或二十年，但要么他们重新接管，要么外面有人进来接管。

Naval: That's right. There's incentive alignment, which is, you know, I can give you equity in the same business and then we're incentive-aligned. Although you may still choose to ignore that incentive and do something different. But really all other forms of alignment tend to boil down to coercion. Right? Maybe we're aligned because we're part of the same religion. We're part of the same tribe. We live in the same household. We're doing the same business together. But the moment you're talking about aligning strangers who don't live next to each other, that don't have kinship, I think the only way you can align them is by clubbing one of them until they pay. You know, they listen—that's just authoritarianism. That's just force. That's violence. That's warfare, which undergirds everything. Like society runs on this thin veneer of civilization, but ultimately underneath the people with the guns are always in charge. And sometimes they forget it for a decade or two decades at a time, but either they take back over or someone from the outside comes in and takes over.

这就是为什么我觉得作为一个社会，我们通过了太多轻浮的法律，人们假装法律不是由拿枪的人在执行。每一条法律，一直到你的停车罚单，都是由某个拿枪的人在执行，因为如果你不付那张停车罚单，他们就会传唤你去法庭。然后如果你不去法庭，他们就会派人去你家。如果你不应答，他们就会发出逮捕令。如果你不去自首，拿枪的人就会来追你。所以归根结底，如果你就这么往下跟，会惊讶于似乎很少有人意识到这一点。但一切都是由拿枪的人执行的。然后那些拿枪的人，你给他们枪，告诉他们去执行法律。当然，这是人性，尤其当那个组织变得更大，他们会开始有偏好地执行。你知道，他们会对敌人执行得稍硬一点，对朋友执行得稍软一点。就像那个南美独裁者有名的话，你知道：对我的朋友，一切都可以；对我的敌人，就用法律，对吧？因为法律不过是最大的那帮人同意的东西。

Which is why I think as a society we have too many frivolous laws being passed where people pretend like the law is not being enforced by people with guns. Every law down to like your parking ticket is enforced by somebody with a gun, because if you don't pay that parking ticket, then they're going to summon you to court. And then if you don't go to court, then they're going to send somebody to your house. And if you don't answer that, then they'll put out a warrant for your arrest. And if you don't turn yourself in, then people with guns will come after you. So, at the end of the day, if you just follow that down, it's amazing how few people seem to realize this. But everything is enforced by people with guns. And then those people with guns, you're giving them guns and telling them to enforce laws. And of course, it's human nature, especially as that organization becomes larger, they will start enforcing it preferentially. You know, they'll enforce it a little bit harder against their enemies and a little bit softer against their friends. It's like that South American dictator who famously said, you know, for my friends, everything; for my enemies, the law, right? Because the law is just whatever the biggest gang agrees upon.

而作为一个社会，我们变得和那个非常脱节。尤其在现代民主里，那些没有任何物理力量、在系统里没有任何利害的人，在投票去控制那些确实有物理力量的人。这会造成一种不稳定的局面。而且你知道我们可能会落到这样一种局面——嗯，我不知道，这其实是一个非常有意思的问题。我们未来会不会有更多城邦？我们会不会有更多小独裁，或由有组织的持械者组成的小亲族去划出一块块小封地？还是我们会僵化成少数几个非常大的国家？在我心里，没有什么比一个全球政府更糟，就像一个单一政府运营一切，因为那是一种放之四海而皆准。

And as a society, we become very disconnected from that. Especially in modern democracies where people who don't have any physical power, any stake in the system, are voting to control people who do have physical power. It creates an unstable situation. And you know we might end up in a situation where—well I don't know, this is actually a very interesting question. Do we end up with more city-states in the future? Do we end up with more small dictatorships or small kinships of organized people with weapons carving out little fiefs? Or do we ossify into a small number of very large states? In my mind there's nothing worse than a global government, like a single government running everything, 'cause it's a one-size-fits-all.

它是一座全景监狱，而且它更可能最终变成，你知道，中国或俄罗斯那种控制，而不是一种民主的——真正民主的共和国。甚至因为民主国家有选出独裁者的习惯，而那是一条单行道。

It's a panopticon, and it's much more likely to end up like, you know, China or Russia kind of control, than a democratic—the truly democratic republic. And even because democracies have a habit of electing dictators, and that's a one-way street.

埃里克：我想看看我们能不能在商业和判断力的语境里玩一玩大卫·多伊奇的一些想法。对。如果好的解释难以改动，好的产品是否也难以改动？

Eric: I want to see if we can play with some of the David Deutsch ideas in a business and judgment context. Right. If good explanations are hard to vary, are good products hard to vary?

纳瓦尔：绝对是。好的产品难以改动。所以难以改动意味着：你没法改那些细节而不让这东西破裂、散掉，或不再是一个好解释。所以你认出它们的办法之一，是回头看那个解释，说：好，它怎么可能是别的样子？没有任何别的东西能嵌合全部事实。就像你把一块拼图放进正确的位置：哦，当然必须是那一块，因为如果它没有这一块、这一部分、这一块凸出来、这边这个洞，就没有别的块能嵌进去。好的产品同样难以改动。所以如果你看 iPhone，比方说，很难在不破坏使它出色的那些东西的前提下改变 iPhone 的特征。事实上，他们推出时的原始形态，我想是在 2007 年，和今天的形态仍然相当相似。它并没有真正改变，对吧？他们钉死了基本形态，就像笔记本电脑的形态一样，对吧？从最初那些日子到现在也还没变回去。所以好的产品非常难以改动。它们确实有一套非常独特的环环相扣的部件，然后让它们大于整体。

Naval: Absolutely. Good products are hard to vary. So hard to vary means that you can't change the details without the thing breaking or falling apart or no longer being a good explanation. So one of the ways you recognize them is you look back at the explanation and you say, well, how could it have been otherwise? There's no other thing which would fit all the facts. It's like when you put a puzzle piece in the right spot in a puzzle: oh, of course it had to be that one, because no other piece will fit if it doesn't have this piece and this part and this part jutting out and this hole over here. So the same way good products are hard to vary. So if you look at an iPhone for example, it's hard to change the characteristics of the iPhone without breaking what makes it great. And in fact, their original form factor when they launched, I think it was in 2007, is still quite similar to the form factor today. It hasn't really changed, right? They nailed the basic form factor, same way with like the laptop form factor, right? It still hasn't changed back from the original days. So good products are very hard to vary. They do have a very unique set of interlocking parts that then allows them to be greater than the whole.

你知道，复杂性理论里有一个概念叫涌现。涌现是当你拿来许许多多不同的部件，把它们放在一起，某种你不曾预料的新东西涌现出来。一种新能力涌现出来。但有意思的一点是，你在下面放在一起的那些部件，你并没有给它们更多自由。你给了它们约束。你实际上用某些方式把它们锁在一起。而通过约束它们能够运转的方式，某种新能力在上面涌现出来。所以是那些约束在上面创造了那种能力。所以这有一点反直觉，对吧？因为你会以为更多自由带来更多自由。不，在这个情形里是约束。同样，如果你看一家公司或一份合同，合同是我们自愿进入的约束，为了在未来去创造某样东西。比如婚姻契约是为了在家里创造孩子。或者像公司是一份合同，去创造一个带有股权的实体，我们事先约定怎样分收益、谁来做工、你怎样得到资本。所以为了能够创造东西，你实际上必须能够约束东西。一旦这些东西被锁在一起，它们就难以改动。你不能拿掉一个组件，也不能改变一个组件而不让这东西散掉。而这就是为什么一个好产品是简单的。它实际上——简单并不是说过分地简单。它不一定意味着“像对五岁孩子解释”那种简单，但它意味着你去掉了那些不必要的部分。

You know, there's this concept in complexity theory called emergence. And emergence is when you take a whole bunch of different parts and you put them together and something new emerges that you did not expect. A new capability emerges. But one of the interesting parts is that the parts that you put together below, you didn't give them more freedom. You gave them constraints. You actually locked them together in certain ways. And by constraining the way in which they could operate, some new capability emerged above. So it was the constraints that created the capability above. And so that's a little counterintuitive, right? 'Cause you would think more freedom leads to more freedom. No, in this case it was constraints. Same way like if you look at a corporation or a contract, a contract is voluntary constraints that we enter into to go create something in the future. Like a marriage contract is to go create children in the household. Or like a company is a contract to go create an equity-bearing entity where we agree in advance how we're going to split up the proceeds and who's going to do the work and how you're going to get the capital. So to be able to create things, you actually have to be able to constrain things. Once the things are locked together, they're hard to vary. You can't remove a component, nor can you change a component without the thing falling apart. And that's why like a good product is simple. It actually—by simple it doesn't mean like inordinately simple. It doesn't mean like ELI5 simple necessarily, but it means that you remove the parts that are unnecessary.

埃里克：优雅。

Eric: Elegant.

纳瓦尔：它是优雅的。是的。所以有一句安托万·德·圣-埃克苏佩里的话，他说，你知道，飞机机翼被完美地设计，不是因为没有什么可再加，而是因为没有什么可再拿掉。对。你不能再拿掉。它是柏拉图式的。它是飞机机翼的柏拉图理念。它难以改动。所以一个好产品同样难以改动。你知道，人们这些天想到的一个难题是：为什么所有东西都在向同一种设计收敛？为什么所有汽车看起来都一样？对吧？那是因为我们现在在做风洞测试，所以我们知道那就像最有效率的模型，而我们想要有效率。所以现在所有汽车看起来都像被风吹过。很难从那种设计里改出去。除非你这样做是为了反讽或为了显眼，比如也许，你知道，埃隆和 Cybertruck，就像我有这么大的力量，我不在乎。但我敢跟你打赌，那东西也经过了风洞测试。它是有棱角的并且经过风洞测试的，相对于那种流线型并且经过风洞测试的。

Naval: It's elegant. Yes. So there was an Antoine de Saint-Exupéry quote where he says, you know, the airplane wing is perfectly designed not because there's nothing left to add, but because there's nothing left to take away. Right. You can't take it away. It is Platonic. It is the Platonic ideal of an airplane wing. It is hard to vary. So a good product is similarly hard to vary. You know, one of the conundrums that people come up with these days is why are all things converging on the same design? Why do all cars look the same? Right? It's because we're doing wind-tunnel testing now, and so we know that that's like the most efficient model, and we want efficient. So all the cars now look windswept. It's hard to vary out of that design. Unless you're doing it to be ironic or to stand out, like maybe, you know, Elon with the Cybertruck, like I got so much power I don't care. But I'll bet you that thing is wind-tunnel tested too. It's angular and wind-tunnel tested as opposed to like streamlined and wind-tunnel tested.

但好产品难以改动，还因为它们封装了知识，而当你获得知识，你把它放进产品。而技术所做的，就是知识的自动化。你弄明白某件事，然后你弄明白怎样把它自动化，然后你弄明白怎样把那种自动化规模化，你再弄明白怎样分发那种规模化的自动化，而那种嵌入的知识难以改动，因为在它底下是一个好解释。一旦你弄明白，哦对——一旦你弄明白怎样制造电动车，一旦你有了关于电池的知识，大多数车走向电动就多少是不可避免的。你也许仍然需要一些越野车或军用车围着汽油转，出于生存能力和救灾的理由，但对普通的日常消费者来说，他们不想开车去加油站，弄到会在他们身上爆炸的臭油，把它倒进这台由零件组成的鲁布·戈德堡机器，然后让受控爆炸推动活塞，而他们，你知道，沿街开下去。五十年后看起来会像你看见那些人骑着蒸汽机的图像，你知道，早期的汽车字面上就是带锅炉和煤的蒸汽机。就像它会看起来像那样，对吧？

But good products are hard to vary because they also encapsulate knowledge, and as you gain knowledge, you put that into the product. And what technology does is it's the automation of knowledge. You figure something out and then you figure out how to automate it and then you figure out how to scale that automation, and you figure out how to distribute that scaled automation, and that embedded knowledge is hard to vary 'cause underneath that is a good explanation. Once you figure out that, oh yeah—once you figure out how to make electric cars, once you have the knowledge for batteries, it's kind of inevitable that most cars will go electric. You may need some off-road vehicles or military vehicles around gasoline for survivability reasons and disaster-relief reasons, but for the average everyday consumer, they don't want to drive to the gas station and get smelly oil that can blow up on them and put it into this Rube Goldberg machine of parts and then have controlled explosions driving pistons while they, you know, go down the street. It's going to look in 50 years like when you see those images of guys riding steam engines, you know, like the early cars are literally steam engines with boilers and coal. Like it's going to look like that, right?

而一旦你弄明白电动车，然后你意识到我们实际上想要电动车。你不只是不必去加油站，活动部件更少，更容易修理，更不脏乱，污染更少。而且也因为如果你想要自动驾驶的车，电动车做得更好，因为一辆车给自己充电，比给自己加油容易得多。而如果你有可更换的电池，也许你可以全天候运转，停机极少。而且它可以更好地资本化，把成本压下来。所以它变成它现在的样子。一旦你看见电动车在运转，你就意识到那是下一代难以改动的产品。所以我觉得所有好产品都有这个特征。从某种意义上，如果你在卖产品的多个版本，你最好有一个真正好的理由。就像一个真正好的理由。

And once you figure out electric cars, then you realize actually we want electric cars. Not only do you not have to go to the gas station, there are less moving parts, it's easier to repair, it's less messy, it's less polluting. But also because if you want self-driving cars, electric cars do better, 'cause it's much easier for a car to recharge itself than it is for it to refuel itself. And if you have swappable batteries, maybe you can operate 24/7 and very little downtime. And it can capitalize better, bring the cost down. So it becomes what it is. Once you see electric cars working, you realize that that is a hard-to-vary product for the next generation. And so I think all good products have this characteristic. In a sense, if you're selling multiple versions of your product, you better have a really good reason for that. Like a really good reason for that.

埃里克：嗯，那就把我们带到另一个：好产品有出人意料的触及范围。

Eric: Well, that gets to the other one, which is good products have a surprising reach.

纳瓦尔：绝对是。伟大的产品，就像好的解释一样，它们可以触及许多你从未想到的应用。你知道，史蒂夫·乔布斯和团队从未围坐着对着 iPhone 或 iPad 说：哦，这对埃克森和做石油工作的公司会很棒，因为他们有一个很酷的应用可以弄清物流、工作流和追踪。不，他们不会走到那一层。或者一个更相关的例子也许是，当 iPhone 出现时，你知道，黑莓在企业里根深蒂固，而史蒂夫·鲍尔默，上帝保佑他，你知道，取笑 iPhone 没有键盘。史蒂夫·鲍尔默做对过许多事，所以我不愿把他单独挑出来。我是说，他为微软和他自己都做得很好，所以也许我不会那么点他的名，但当时有人基本上说：好，黑莓就是键盘。企业永远不会容忍一部没有键盘的手机。而他们没有算进去的是，没有键盘、有多点触控、有一块出色的屏幕和软件键盘，解决了这么多事情。而如今是语音——就像如今，你最不想做的事就是给手机加上键盘。多亏了 AI，一切都在走向基于语音。一旦你造出了那个产品，它就有触及范围，而一旦它伸进每个消费者的口袋，那些消费者就把这些产品带到工作中，他们就会像：我为什么在工作用黑莓、在家用 iPhone，而 iPhone 好这么多？

Naval: Absolutely. The great products, just like good explanations, they can reach to lots of applications that you never thought of. You know, Steve Jobs and crew never sat around with the iPhone or the iPad saying, oh this will be great for, you know, Exxon and companies doing oil work because there's a cool app for them for like figuring out their logistics and workflow and tracking. No, they don't go to that level. Or a more relevant example might be when the iPhone came along, you know, the BlackBerry was entrenched in enterprise, and Steve Ballmer, God bless him, you know, made fun of the iPhone for not having a keyboard. Steve Ballmer did lots of things right, so I hate to pick him out. I mean, he did very well for Microsoft and for himself, so maybe I won't call him out as much, but there were people who basically said, well, the BlackBerry is a keyboard. The enterprise will never tolerate a phone without a keyboard. And what they didn't account for is that there's so many things solved by not having a keyboard, by having the multi-touch, and by having a great screen and software keyboard. And nowadays voice—like nowadays, the last thing you want to do is add a keyboard to a phone. It's all going voice-based thanks to AI. That once you've built that product, it has reach, and once it reaches into every consumer's pocket, well, those consumers bring those products to work and they're like, why am I using a BlackBerry at work and I'm using an iPhone at home and the iPhone is so much better?

而知识就是多伊奇的定义。知识是在环境里保持持久的东西。它在环境里复制自己。那些编码了如何适应环境的正确知识的基因，它们会复制。

And the knowledge is the Deutsch definition. Knowledge is the thing that stays persistent in the environment. It replicates itself in the environment. Genes that encode proper knowledge on how to adapt into the environment, they replicate.

那些基因被传下去。不正确的、虚假的基因，它们不在环境里复制。它们被从基因库里剔除。想法也是一样。如果我给你一个怎样赚钱的想法，或者我甚至给你一条一般原则，或者我给你一件具体的事让你出去做而它失败了，你不会保住那种知识。你不会传播那种知识。所以知识在环境里是持久的。而一旦你创造了知识，一旦多点触控屏幕的知识被创造出来，键盘的知识就过时了，至少在那个语境里。而当人们把 iPhone 从家里带进来、在工作里用它们而不是用黑莓时，黑莓就被 iPhone 取代了。而 IT 的人被投票压过了，因为他们归根结底是在为公司其余的人提供服务，而不是去运营公司。

Those genes get passed down. Genes that are incorrect, that are false, they do not replicate in the environment. They get weeded out of the gene pool. The same is true of ideas. If I give you an idea for how to make money, or if I give you even a general principle, or I give you a specific thing to go out and do and it fails, you're not going to keep that knowledge. You're not going to spread that knowledge. So knowledge is persistent in the environment. And once you've created knowledge, and once the knowledge of multi-touch screens was created, then the knowledge of keyboards was obsolete, at least in that context. And the BlackBerrys got replaced by iPhones when people who brought their iPhones in from home would use them at work instead of using BlackBerrys. And the IT guys got outvoted, 'cause ultimately they're there to provide a service to the rest of the company and not to run the company.

然后技术还有深深的赢者通吃的网络效应。所有技术都有。只是在有些情形里比另一些更明显。像 Facebook 或 WhatsApp 这样的东西有极其看得见的网络效应。苹果也有，因为它们是开发者平台。但甚至像亚马逊这样的东西，你会以为它不会有网络效应。当年我们以为会有无数电子商务商店卷入一场向底部的死亡搏斗。结果不是那样。结果有一个巨大的赢家，就像互联网所做的那样，也就是每样东西都有一个巨大的赢家，然后有一条非常小的玩家组成的长尾，但它摧毁中间。它摧毁平庸的中间，那些靠地理或者，你知道，监管来存活的。它们被超级聚合者和长尾一起吹走。但所有技术都有网络效应，都有规模经济。所以最好的产品能把它的开发摊到最大的用户基数上，因此它把最多的事情想透了。它有最多的支持。它有最多的、最新的前沿技术。

And then also technology has deeply winner-take-all network effects. All technology does. It just is more obvious in some cases than others. Like something like a Facebook or a WhatsApp has an extremely visible network effect. Apple does too because they're developer platforms. But even things like Amazon, which you would have thought would not have a network effect. Back in the day, we thought there'd be zillions of e-commerce stores involved in a death struggle to the bottom. Turns out it's not the case. Turns out there's one huge winner, and just like the internet does, which is it has one huge winner for everything and then has a long tail of very small players, but it destroys the middle. It destroys the mediocre middle, which was relying on geography or, you know, regulations to survive. They get blown away by the mega-aggregator and then by a long tail. But all of technology has network effects and has scale economies. So the best product gets to amortize its development over the largest user base, and so it has the most things thought through. It has the most support. It has the most latest bleeding-edge technology.

不管你有多有钱，你都买不到一部更好的手机。你花多少钱也买不到一部比最新 iPhone、最新安卓手机更好的手机。就像，就是这样，对吧？我们都能用到同一部手机，因为有数千亿美元的研发预算已经进入智能手机供应链，进入当前的智能手机设计。所以如果你有钱，你所能做的只是给它镶上钻石，用一种有点悲哀的方式试图看起来特别，但你其实只是看起来像个傻瓜。或者就像汽车，真正昂贵的汽车并不是更好。它们只是更古怪。我觉得今天这颗星球上大概没有比特斯拉 Model Y 更好的车。那是，你知道，2025 年。就像客观地说，如果你看它做而其他车不做的所有事情，它多少是全面最好的车。你不能花一百万美元去买一辆更好的车。你只能花一百万美元去买一辆更滑稽的车，或一辆更古怪的车。更极端的变量，更极端。然后你只是在展示自己是一个滑稽古怪、试图用信号显示地位的人。而没有比被人看出你在试图买地位更糟的显示地位的方式了。

Doesn't matter how rich you are, you can't buy a better phone. There's no amount of money you can spend to buy a better phone than the latest iPhone, the latest Android phone. Like that's it, right? We all have access to the same phone because there is an R&D budget of hundreds of billions of dollars that has gone into the smartphone supply chain and into the current smartphone designs. So if you're rich, all you can do is you can like encrust it with diamonds in a kind of a sad attempt to look special, but you really just look like an idiot. Or it's like cars, like really expensive cars are not better. They're just weirder. I don't think there's a better car on the planet today than probably the Tesla Model Y. That's, you know, 2025. Like objectively speaking, if you look at all the things that it does that other cars don't do, it's kind of the all-around best car. You can't spend a million dollars to buy a better car. You can just spend a million dollars to buy a goofier car or a weirder car. More extreme variables, more extreme. And then you're just showing yourself as someone who's goofy and weird and trying to signal for status. And there's no worse way to signal status than to be shown as trying to buy status.

建立判断力

Building Judgement

纳瓦尔：我有这样一个理论：作为人类，我们总是在寻找某种完美而持久的东西，因为那和我们拥有的生命相反，我们的生命是不完美、不持久的。我们可以在灵性里寻找它，对上帝的探求。我们可以在科学里寻找它，也就是对那个宏大统一的、完美的万物理论的探求。在艺术和美里也有类似物，在西斯廷教堂里，试图把某种完美封装很长、很长一段时间。所以我觉得我个人想聚焦于那个，而这里有一种挣扎，因为你也想看新的东西。世界总是在移动和前进，尤其在事物和物件、学习、技术和科学的领域。你想看最近的东西。你不想学三十年前的科学。你想学我们今天所知道的最好的科学，技术也一样。而那有实用价值，了解 AI 或自动驾驶或机器人，诸如此类。所以了解那些移动得很快、处在知识前沿的东西总是好的，但与此同时你也想研究永恒的东西，那就是人性。人性不会改变。

Naval: I had this theory that as humans we're always searching for something perfect and permanent, because that's the opposite of the life that we have, which is an imperfect and impermanent life. And we can search for it in spirituality, the quest for God. We can search for it in science, which is the quest for the grand unified perfect theory of everything. There are analogues in art and beauty, in the Sistine Chapel, trying to encapsulate something perfect for a long, long period of time. And so I feel like I personally want to focus on that, and there's a struggle, because you also want to look at new things. The world is always moving and advancing, especially in the domain of things and objects and learning and technology and science. You want to look at the most recent things. You don't want to learn the science from 30 years ago. You want to learn the science best we know today, and the same about technology. And that has practical value, knowing about AI or self-driving cars or robotics or what have you. So it's always good to know about what's moving quickly and is on the forefront of knowledge, but at the same time you also want to study the timeless, and that's human nature. Human nature doesn't change.

所以在那里你想依靠那些古人，你知道，你想读叔本华，你想读，你知道，道德经，诸如此类，因为——或者圣经——因为人不会改变。所以对我的推文我多少有些挣扎，甚至对写作也是，播客、思考、我花的时间——我挣扎的是：我想把很多时间花在永恒的东西上，因为那种知识会贯穿我的余生，但我也想把时间花在应时的东西上，也就是最现代的那些。现在在那里我必须寻找实际上有用的现代东西。实际上是关于事物的现代东西，比如我想了解 AI。我想了解自动驾驶。我想了解机器人。我想了解太空旅行和太空探索。我想了解无人机。但我不想知道，你知道，金·卡戴珊今天在做什么。就像那是无用的知识。事实上，我甚至不需要从她以及她和别人的互动里知道任何关于人性的功课，因为那些功课已经被叔本华或康德或过去某个像那样的人写得更好了，对吧？

So there you want to rely upon the olden people, you know, you want to read Schopenhauer and you want to read, you know, the Tao Te Ching and so on, because—or the Bible—because people don't change. And so with my tweets I kind of struggle with, or even with writing, is podcasting, thinking, time that I spend—what I struggle with is I want to spend a lot of time on the timeless, because that knowledge carries through the rest of my life, but I also want to spend time on the timely, which is the most modern stuff. Now there I have to look for the modern stuff that's actually useful. It's actually the modern stuff about things, like I want to know about AI. I want to know about self-driving cars. I want to know about robotics. I want to know about space travel and space exploration. I want to know about drones. But I don't want to know what, you know, Kim Kardashian is doing today. Like that's useless knowledge. In fact, I don't even need to know any lessons in human nature from her and her interactions with other people, because those lessons have been written down better by Schopenhauer or Kant or someone like that in the past, right?

埃里克：柏拉图。

Eric: Plato.

纳瓦尔：所以当谈到哲学和人性，那是永恒的材料。它值得学。它多少有点陈词滥调，就我们可以谈什么是陈词滥调这个意义上，但它多少是陈词滥调。但与此同时它非常重要，值得吸收，因为这些是最深的指导原则。你知道，哲学多少是关于怎样过一种好生活的研究。我想亚里士多德把那个叫 eudaimonia 或类似的东西——大概这个词我发音不对——但那值得研究。但与此同时，当谈到科学、技术和事物的世界，也值得研究那些超现代的、最近的东西。至少对我们的书、我们的工作、我们的写作来说，我不喜欢把应时的东西放进去，而陷阱是那些和事物如何运作的知识无关、而只是关于人的知识，或只是八卦或新闻的应时东西。我觉得很多人都说过这个，最近是塔勒布：你知道，要看新闻有多值得，只要去读昨天的报纸，对吧？你正在消费的信息的半衰期是多少？它在未来还会相关多久，主要基于它在过去已经相关了多久。

Naval: So when it comes to philosophy and human nature, that's timeless material. It's worth learning. It's somewhat clichéd in the sense that we can get into what cliché is, but it's somewhat clichéd. But at the same time it's very important to absorb, because these are the deepest guiding principles. You know, philosophy is kind of the study of how to live a good life. I think Aristotle called that eudaimonia or something like that—probably saying the word wrong—but that's worth studying. But at the same time it's worth studying the ultramodern, most recent stuff when it comes to the world of science and technology and things. At least for our book, our work, for our writing, I don't like putting timely things in there, and the trap is timely things that have nothing to do with knowledge of how things work, but rather knowledge of people, or just gossip or news. I think a lot of people have said this, Taleb most recently, that you know, to see how worth it news is, just read yesterday's newspaper, right? What's the half-life of the information that you're consuming? How long will it be relevant in the future is predominantly based on how long has it been relevant in the past.

对。这和多伊奇的工作有一点连上，也就是他会说一个好解释——你知道它是好解释的办法之一——是它有触及范围，而且触及跨越空间和时间，所以它适用于许多你不曾预料的事物。它解释那些你会以为在它领域之外的事物，或者那个理论最初被提出来是为了解释一件局部的事，但它几乎总是最终解释一件全局的事。所以比方说，就像地球的轴倾理论，你知道，你也许是为了解释为什么有季节才想出它。但然后当你到南半球，季节会翻转，而你不能改那个。你不能拿走理论里解释北半球季节的那一部分，到了南半球再把它扔掉。那个理论有很深的触及，或很宽的触及。所以最好的理论又深又宽。

Yeah. This ties into a little bit with Deutsch's work, which is he'll say a good explanation—one of the ways you know it is a good explanation—is that it has reach, and reach across space and time, so it applies to lots of things that you didn't expect. It explains things which you would think were out of its domain, or it was originally that theory was postulated to explain a local thing, but it almost always ends up explaining a global thing. So for example, like in the axial-tilt theory of the earth, you know, you might have come up with that to explain why there are seasons. But then the seasons flip when you go to the southern hemisphere, and you can't change that. You can't take the part of the theory that explains seasons in the northern hemisphere and then throw it out when you get to the southern hemisphere. That theory has deep reach or wide reach. So the best theories are deep and wide.

而且你知道，对同一件事的另一个完全不同的角度是杰德·麦肯纳。他有这个——我不知道你有没有读过杰德·麦肯纳，但他多少是这个疯狂的、匿名的开悟家伙，对吧？他写这些真的很好笑的书，从《精神开悟：最该死的事》开始。而且我向那些一点也不神秘、一点也不灵性、却又知道有什么不对、有什么缺失、有什么错了的人强烈推荐它们。就像，你知道，我想是墨菲斯描述矩阵的方式。他说，你心里的一根刺，对吧？你感觉到了吗，尼奥？就像心里的一根刺。

And you know another completely different angle on the same thing is Jed McKenna. He has this—I don't know if you ever read Jed McKenna, but he's kind of this crazy anonymous enlightened dude, right? And he writes these really funny books, starting with Spiritual Enlightenment: The Damnedest Thing. And I highly recommend them for people who are not mystical, not spiritual at all, but yet they know something is off, something is missing, something is wrong. It's like, you know, I think it was Morpheus, the way he describes the Matrix. He goes, a splinter in your mind, right? Do you feel that, Neo? It's like a splinter in your mind.

埃里克：对。

Eric: Yeah.

纳瓦尔：所以如果你觉得心里有一根刺，杰德·麦肯纳是一个好的起点。而且他有这个所谓对真理或上帝或无论什么之存在的证明。而它实际上是一位名叫安瑟伦的修士一个很老的证明的改写。我不知道他有没有意识到这一点，但它是一个已有证明的改写。总之，它并不真是一个证明。它不是数学证明，但它确实依赖这个想法：嘿，你相信真理存在吗？而如果你相信真理存在，那你知道那是一个和你相信不存在真理的宇宙不同的宇宙，对吧？而如果你相信若真理存在，那你是否相信真理可以在某个地方或某个时间不存在，或者它可以是暂时的？答案是不。所以如果你多少顺着那条推理链走下去，你会意识到真理必须有最宽、最深的触及。再说一次，它必须是这个包罗万象的理论。所以无论在科学里，无论在灵性里，无论在技术里，我觉得我们都只是在寻找某种完美而持久的东西。

Naval: So if you feel like there's a splinter in your mind, Jed McKenna is a good starting point. And he has this proof, quote-unquote, of the existence of truth or God or what have you. And it's actually a rework of a very old proof by a monk named Anselm. I don't know if he realized that, but it's a rework of an existing proof. Anyway, it's not really a proof. It's not a mathematical proof, but it does rely on this idea that, hey, do you believe that truth exists? And if you believe that truth exists, then you know that's a different universe than one where you believe that there is no truth, right? And if you believe that if truth exists, then do you believe that truth can be non-existent in a certain place or a certain time, or it can be temporal? And the answer is no. And so if you kind of follow that chain of reasoning you realize that truth would have to have the widest and deepest reach. Again it would have to be this all-encompassing theory. So whether in science, whether in spirituality, whether in technology, I think we're all just looking for something perfect and permanent.

而作为人类，我们总会不满。人类的心智总会不满，直到它找到那个，而那多少就是成就的驱动力。一个人越有成就，他们就越能升级，去追求更宽、更深、更远的东西。所以你知道，多伊奇比方说，并不满足于只做一个物理学家。他也在研究他所谓的四个最深的理论——计算、自然选择进化论，以及认识论——因为他试图用一种大一统理论解释一切。而且你知道，每一种哲学在其核心都必须对普遍性有某种诉求，对吧？就像解释一切。这多少就是人性。甚至当前整场 AGI 热潮，也是关于：哦，我们要发明上帝。它会解释一切。这是最后一项技术。我不知道人类是怎么回事，但它一直把我们往那个方向驱赶。每个人都想要一个万物理论。我们是绝对主义者。对。马克思主义多少是那样，你知道，它多少像我们都平等。我们都一样，你知道，我们都是意识。我们都只是一体。显然马克思主义有很多问题。我不会坐在这里整天批判马克思主义，尽管我乐意那样做。但那里面也有那种普遍主义的意图。所以，从深层来看，这种对一切之答案的探求，不断渗回我们生活的每一项事业。而无论你有没有意识到，它总像潜伏在底下。

And as humans we're always going to be dissatisfied. The human mind will always be dissatisfied until it finds that, and that's kind of the drive for achievement. And the more accomplished somebody becomes, the more they get to level up and go for something even broader, even deeper, even further. So you know Deutsch, for example, isn't just content with being a mere physicist. He's also studying what he calls the four deepest theories—computation, theory of evolution by natural selection, and epistemology—because he's trying to explain everything in kind of a grand unified theory. And you know, every philosophy at its core has to have some appeal to universality, right? To like explaining everything. It's just kind of human nature. Even the whole current AGI craze, it's about like, oh, we're going to invent God. It's going to explain everything. This is the last technology. I don't know what it is about humans, but it keeps driving us towards that. Everybody wants a theory of everything. We are absolutists. Yeah. Marxism is kind of that, you know, it's kind of like we're all equal. We're all the same, you know, we're all consciousness. We're all just one. Obviously Marxism has lots of problems. I'm not going to sit here and critique Marxism all day long, although I would be happy to. But it is also that there's that universalist intent in there. So, in a deep way, this quest for the answer to everything keeps seeping back into every endeavor of our lives. And whether you realize it or not, it's always like lurking underneath.

埃里克：你说过你的赚钱能力比以往任何时候都高，而且一直在稳定增加。我有点想拆开那里面的成分。就像那是怎样改变的？拐点都是什么？即便在你多少变得更退休了一些，我猜你会这样说，更不那么刻意的时候。

Eric: You said that your earning power is higher than it's ever been and been on a steady increase over time. I'm curious to sort of break down the components of that. Like how has that changed? What have the inflection points been? Even as you've sort of gotten more retired, I guess you would say, like less deliberate.

纳瓦尔：对。公平地说，我现在像是最低限度地退休。我在努力工作。我有一家新公司，对吧？在某种程度上我投入的工作大概和以往一样多。但你知道，其中绝大部分进入我真正在乎的新公司。仍在隐身模式。但你知道，有一些进入这边或那边做几笔投资，帮助一些我在这个生态里认识的人，再加上我的老公司。但就赚钱能力而言，有一些来自储存起来的资本，所以我可以资助自己的项目。有一些来自声誉，所以人们会多少信任我，并且，你知道，资助我，诸如此类。但很大一部分只是来自知识：知道该追求什么，怎样架构一家新公司，怎样把它启动，怎样招到好人，很早就能认出：哦，别费劲去招那个人，即便他们很好，因为他们自我太高，他们合不来，或者别推销得太猛，因为后来会脱开。或者去找这类人，你知道，开头你需要更多技术建造者，更少营销的人，诸如此类，否则你只会过早制造太多需求，等等等等。所以这只是大量已经被折进原则里的实用诀窍。你知道每件事都多少有学习曲线。我想那是叔本华或塞内卡，两个里的一个。我想这是塞内卡，他基本上说——是他写给卢西利乌斯的信之一。他说过大致这样的话：正确的学习方式是从具体到一般，而不是从一般到具体。而那意味着：你在现实里做事，你遭遇现实，你检验它，你从中学习，然后你再一般化。

Naval: Yeah. To be fair, right now I'm like minimally retired. I'm working hard. I got a new company, right? In a way I'm probably putting in as much work as I ever have. But you know, the vast majority of it goes into my new company, which I really care about. Still in stealth mode. But you know, some of it goes into making a few investments here or there, helping out some people that I know in the ecosystem, plus my old companies. But in terms of earning power, some of that comes from stored capital, so I can fund my own projects. Some of that comes from reputation, so people will kind of trust me and, you know, fund me and so on. But a lot of it just comes through knowledge of knowing what to go after, how to structure a new company, how to get it going, how to recruit good people, recognizing early on like, oh, don't bother recruiting that person even though they're good because they're too high-ego, they won't fit in, or don't sell too hard because it'll just come unstuck later. Or go after these kinds of people, you know, you need more technical builders at the beginning and less of the marketers and so on, because otherwise you're just going to create too many requirements too early, etc., etc. So it's just a lot of practical know-how which has been folded into principles. You know there's kind of learning curves to everything. I think it was either Schopenhauer or Seneca, one of the two. I think this was Seneca, where he basically said—it's one of his letters to Lucilius. He said something along the lines of that the right way to learn is from the specific to the general, and not from the general to the specific. And what that means is like you do things in reality, you encounter reality, you test it, you learn from it, and then you generalize.

然后那会让你知道，那条格言、那条警句、那条原则或那套价值系统，何时适用、何时不适用。因为拾取别人的价值和警句的问题之一是，你不知道它们何时适用、何时不适用。所以当人们在 Twitter 上说，好，你自相矛盾了。就像，好，那个词换一种用法，在这个情形里并不适用。并不是，你知道，即便我不怎么用形容词，我的意思是：在大多数情形里，按我思考它的方式，这个适用。而在其他情形里，按我思考它的方式，另一件事适用。而我只是给自己一条启发式，不是让人盲目遵从的。而这些人在寻找数学证明。

And then that lets you know when that maxim or that aphorism or that principle or that value system, when it applies and when it does not. Because one of the problems with picking up other people's values and aphorisms, you don't know when they apply, when they don't. So when people say on Twitter, well, you contradicted yourself. It's like, well, using that word a different way, it doesn't apply in this case. It's not, you know, even though I don't use a lot of adjectives, what I mean is in most cases, in the way that I'm thinking about it, this applies. And in other cases, the way I'm thinking about it, this other thing applies. And I'm just giving myself a heuristic, which is not to be blindly followed. And these people are looking for math proofs.

埃里克：对。你在创造一般的东西时，是带着具体的框架进来的。

Eric: Yeah. You came in with the frame of the specific when you created the general.

纳瓦尔：没错。我创造一般的东西，是为了帮我导航未来的具体情形，但每一种情境都是具体的，你知道，所以它可能有多条互相竞争的一般原理用在上面，其中一条压过其他，或多条一起压过。所以那就是你从具体走到一般的方式。但相反的情形是你走进学院。你知道，你在学校里学了太多东西。你学了所有这些宏大理论，然后到了实地你在抛理论，但你不知道哪一条在何处、何时适用，或者你只是为眼前这件事学错了理论。而那时候你多少就变成纳西姆·塔勒布所说的 IYI，一个intellectual yet idiot，有知识却是傻瓜。基本上就是受教育过度、实践不足的人。

Naval: That's right. I created the general to help me navigate future specifics, but each situation is specific, you know, so it may have multiple general things applied to it that compete, and one of them overrides, or multiple of them override. So that's how you go from the specific to the general. But the opposite is when you go to academia. You know, you study too many things in school. You learn all these grand theories, and then out in the field you're spouting theories, but you don't know which one applies where and when, or you've just learned the wrong theory for the current thing. And that's when you kind of end up as what Nassim Taleb says an IYI, an intellectual yet idiot. And it's basically someone who's overeducated and underpracticed.

埃里克：所以培养判断力的配方，就是这些启发式、这些格言、这些想法，再加上能插进去、好把它们接上的经验，是这样吗？

Eric: So is the recipe for building judgment then the combination of like these heuristics, these maxims, these ideas, and the experience to plug in to sort of splice them up?

纳瓦尔：经验。首先你得去做。首先，你知道，另一条塔勒布式的说法是：如果你想做哲学王，先做王，对吧？先成为王。那是更难的那个，也是会造就你的那个——成为王的过程会自动把你变成哲学家。成为哲学家的过程会让你离做王更远，而且你也不会是一个很有用的哲学家。没有什么铁律——也有一些人并没有做很多实际的事，却是了不起的哲学家，但他们非常、非常、非常罕见。但是对，我是说你想待在场上。就像，你知道，比方说埃隆——所以你在写一本关于他的书——他有一般原则，但那些原则来自做事。他知道，比方说，速度的价值，对吧？埃隆以做事有多快出名，他把循环时间这类东西压下来，而这是他的一条核心原则，他脑子里有许多关于这在实践里意味着什么的细致落实：好，工程师可以自己定需求，你知道，你总是把一切堵住的东西疏通，你不躲在邮件后面。他有各种各样的——我确信他脑子里有许许多多从那条大原则派生出来的启发式。但如果我只是坐在学院里，有人告诉我速度就是一切。去追求速度。我只会对此有点心智迟钝。我只会一直跑来跑去、喘不过气。我不会知道怎样应用它。所以，再说一次，生命是在场上过的。你必须去做那些事，才能学会那些事。只学不做是一个错误。

Naval: Experience. First you got to do. First, you know, another Talebism is: if you want to be a philosopher king, first be a king, right? First become a king. That's the harder one, and that's the one that'll make you—the process of becoming a king will turn you into a philosopher automatically. The process of becoming a philosopher will take you further from being a king, and you won't be a very useful philosopher either. By no hard and fast rules—there are people who didn't do a lot practically speaking that were incredible philosophers, but they're very, very, very rare. But yeah, I mean you want to be in the arena. Like, you know, for example Elon—so you're working a book about him—and he has general principles, but those principles come from doing things. He knows the value, for example, of speed, right? Elon is famous for how fast he does things, and he gets cycle time and things down, and that is a core principle for him, and he has a lot of detailed implementations of what that means practically about, okay, engineers get to set their own requirements, you know, you always unblock everything, you don't hide behind email. He has all kinds of—I'm sure many, many heuristics in his head that derive from that big principle. But if I just sat around academia and somebody told me that speed is everything. Go with speed. I'd just be kind of idiotic about it. I'd just be running around hyperventilating all the time. I wouldn't know how to apply it. So, again, life is lived in the arena. You have to do the things to learn the things. It's a mistake to just learn and not do.

我小时候是阅读的狂热爱好者，我曾经读很多。我是说，我读过几百本，也许上千本。我已经数不清了。而且大多数书我喜欢——我挑书，我会读它们，然后我会像：哦，这本书我十年前读过。我只是忘了。而我已经读到大约三分之二，因为我对那些不会真正粘在我身上的东西，或那些并非极其有用的东西，记性很差。而我曾经为这个事实自豪。现在我意识到，实际上我的阅读大部分没有价值。你知道，那全是学院式的书本知识。其中很多只是小说。而这些天我读得更少。但我读得非常有意。我读是因为我对某件事真正感兴趣，我在试图学到什么，或试图把某件事弄明白。而且我会读少量，然后想很多。所以我把那种阅读更多地当成点燃我自己思考的方式，而不是只是从别人那里接收东西，然后过后再把它们吐回去。

When I was a kid, I was a big fan of reading, and I used to read a lot. I mean, I read hundreds, maybe thousands of books. I've lost count. And most books I like—I pick the books and I'll read them and I'll be like, oh I read this book 10 years ago. I just forgot about it. And I'm like two-thirds of the way through the book, because I have a terrible memory for things that aren't—that don't really stick with me, or things that aren't incredibly useful. And I used to be proud of that fact. And now I realize actually most of my reading was worthless. You know, it was all academic book knowledge. A lot of it was just fiction. And these days I read less. But I read very deliberately. I read because I'm really interested in something and I'm trying to learn something or I'm trying to figure something out. And I'll read small amounts and then I'll think a lot. So I'll use that reading as more of a way to spark my own thinking rather than just kind of taking in things from other people and then regurgitating them back later.

就像塔勒布的很多东西并没有粘在我身上。比方说，他那个反脆弱的东西并没有真正粘住我，因为我在自己的生活里找不到那么多能应用真正反脆弱的实际例子，因为真正的反脆弱不只是韧性。它是你实际上通过逆境变得更强。是的，我能看见一些这样的情形，比如，你知道，你举重时的兴奋效应。但有很多情形我找不到反脆弱的实际应用。但我理解塔勒布为什么那样做——就像他整套投资策略是反脆弱的，你知道，系统崩了他赚得更多，或者只是系统里的波动他赚得更多。但我找不到那么多例子，所以反脆弱这个概念没有粘在我身上，即便我知道那是大多数人觉得——因为我对他们影响最大——而他也最看重的那本书。它对我不管用。所以我不读那本书，但我总是在重读《自己也要下注》。因为那一本有很多我能应用的金块，我能说：哦，那就是我在自己具体生活里已经注意到的某件事的一般化。所以少数规则就是一个好例子。我不断看见少数规则在各处出现。所以你确实需要生活里的实际应用。你必须去做。

Like a lot of Taleb doesn't stick with me. Like for example, his antifragile thing doesn't really stick with me, 'cause I can't find that many actual examples in my life where I can apply true antifragility, 'cause true antifragility is not just resilience. It is that you actually get stronger through adversity. And yes I can see some cases of that, like, you know, hormetic effects when you're, you know, weightlifting. But there are a lot of cases where I don't find a practical application of antifragility. But I get why Taleb does it—like his entire investing strategy is antifragile, you know, the system collapses he makes more, or it's just volatility in the system he makes more. But I don't find as many examples, so the concept of antifragility doesn't stick with me, and even though I know that's the book that most people think because I had the biggest impact on them, and he values it the most. It doesn't work for me. So I don't read that book, but I'm always rereading Skin in the Game. Because that one has a lot of nuggets that I can apply, that I can say, oh, that's the generalization of something that I had noticed in my specific life. So the minority rule is a good example. I keep seeing the minority rule show up everywhere. So you do need the practical applications in your life. You have to do.

埃里克：所以即便就今天而言，如果我想变得更聪明、更有智慧，那就意味着我必须工作？对。我正想说，你在持续地注入经验和原则。

Eric: So even to the extent today that I want to become smarter and wiser, that means I have to work? Yeah. I was going to say you're continuously injecting experience and principle.

纳瓦尔：没错。对。你从经验开始。你从具体知识开始。然后具体知识变成更一般化的知识。然后你可以把那个拿去变成价值，也就是，你知道，把它织进你已有的价值。它改善你的判断力。而且我觉得最终在这个无限杠杆的时代，判断力是最重要的东西。就像如果你知道该往哪里航行，你知道，人们会为此付钱给你。一艘船的船长被选中，是基于他把团队聚在一起、告诉他们我们要去哪里、激励他们前往那里的能力。所以，但去哪里才是最重要的部分。如果你有两个苹果公司 CEO 的候选人，对吧，目前世界上最有价值的公司——也许是英伟达，但无所谓。你有两个公司 CEO 的候选人，一个 80% 的时间是对的，一个 85% 的时间是对的，而公司价值数万亿美元。你会让谁掌管？你会给那个 85% 的时间是对的人多付很多。就像你会每年多付他数十亿美元，因为他在掌舵一艘数万亿美元的船。而方向比任何其他单一的事情都更重要。

Naval: Correct. Yeah. You start with experience. You start with specific knowledge. Then the specific knowledge turns into more generalized knowledge. Then you can take that and turn it into values, which are, you know, to tie it into your existing values. It improves your judgment. And I think ultimately in this age of infinite leverage, judgment is the most important thing. Like if you knew where to navigate, you know, people will pay you for that. The captain of a ship is chosen based on his ability to get the team together and tell them where we're going, inspire them to go there. So, but where to go is the most important part. If you have two candidates for CEO of Apple, right, the most valuable company in the world currently—maybe it's Nvidia, but whatever. You have two candidates for the CEO of the company and one is right 80% of the time and one is right 85% of the time and the company's worth trillions of dollars. Who are you going to put in charge? You'll pay that guy who is right 85% of the time a lot more. Like you'll pay him billions of dollars more per year because he's steering a multi-trillion-dollar ship. And the direction matters more than any other single thing.

然后最后我会说判断力——判断力真的很重要。判断力来自经验和反思。所以你经历事情，你对这些事情有诚实的反思，你建立起你的判断力。然后到了某一点，你的判断力变得如此之好，你甚至无法再解释它或把它说清楚。所以早先有一个阶段，你不知道该做什么，你必须理性地想清楚该做什么，你接受反馈。最终你做决定做得如此之好，你不再去找别人。是的，你可以获得一点点反馈来给判断力提供信息。但你知道你处在拿定主意的最佳位置。所以你运用判断力，并且你可以对别人说清楚我为什么那样做。但会到来这样一个点：你的判断力如此之好，你甚至无法把它说清楚。而那时它就叫品味，对吧？当你只是像：这对我来说感觉不对，对吧？我们就是该这样做。这就是我想做的方式，对吧？但在那一点上它是品味。而那些到达那一点的人，就像世界上的里克·鲁宾那样有很好品味的人，以及世界上的史蒂夫·乔布斯。我觉得那些人是最有创造力的，创造出最伟大的艺术和商业作品，因为他们有很好的品味。就像我觉得我会猜测，马斯克，比方说在 SpaceX，他有品味，他周围有几个关键工程师也有品味。如果你看那些领先的 AI 实验室，多少看到一些研究者在愿意谈他们怎样选择跑哪些实验、不跑哪些实验时发出的推文串，它归结为品味。他们有一种品味，就像：我要把几千块 GPU 朝这个扔上几百个小时，花很多钱，大概最后什么也得不到。但我的直觉、我的品味告诉我，我发展出来的直觉，也就是我的品味，告诉我要不要试一试。

And then finally I would say that judgment—judgment is really important. Judgment comes through experience and reflection. So you experience things, you have honest reflection about those things, and you build your judgment. And then at some point your judgment becomes so good that you cannot even explain it or articulate it anymore. So there's a point early on where you don't know what to do and you have to rationally think through what to do and you take feedback. Eventually you get so good at making the decisions that you don't go to other people. Yes, you can get a little bit of feedback to inform your judgment. But you know that you're in the best position to make up your mind. So you exercise the judgment and you can articulate to other people why did I do that. But there comes a point where your judgment is so good that you can't even articulate it. And that's when it's called taste, right? When you're just like, it doesn't feel right to me, right? This is just the way we should do it. This is the way I want to do it, right? But at that point it's taste. And people who have gotten to that point, like the Rick Rubins of the world, where they have really good taste, [and] the Steve Jobs of the world. I think those are the people who are the most creative and create the greatest works of art and business because they have really good taste. Like I think I would guess that Musk, for example on SpaceX, he has taste, and there are a few key engineers around him who have taste.
