If you look at the leading AI labs and you kind of see the tweet threads from some of the researchers there when they care to talk about how they're choosing which experiments to run, which ones not to run, it boils down to taste. They have taste about like I'm going to throw thousands of GPUs at this for hundreds of hours and spend lots of money and probably wind up with nothing. But my intuition, my taste tells me, my developed intuition, which is my taste, tells me whether to try it out or not.

埃里克：对，你有过一条推文。培养你的直觉需要时间，但一旦它培养好了，就别再听别的任何东西。

Eric: Yeah, you had a tweet. It takes time to develop your gut, but once it's developed, don't listen to anything else.

纳瓦尔：正是。对。而这只是品味的另一种说法。它是你的直觉感受。而且它可以围绕人。你知道，年纪更大的人对其他人有很好的判断力，因为我们所有人始终都在获得经验的一件事，就是人际互动。无论我们在做什么，我们都在和其他人互动。所以我们在积累关于该和谁共事、该信任谁、不该信任谁、不该和谁共事的经验。而那最终变成你的直觉感受。所以随着你变老，你得信任你的直觉感受。

Naval: Exactly. Yeah. And this is just another way of saying taste. It's your gut feel. And it can be around people. You know, older people have very good judgment about other people because the one thing that we are always all gaining experience is in human interaction. No matter what we're doing, we're interacting with other people. So we're building up experience about who to work with and who to trust and who not to trust and who not to work with. And that ends up your gut feel. And so as you get older, you got to trust your gut feel.

埃里克：你现在的投资主要感觉像品味吗？

Eric: Is your investing now primarily feel like taste?

纳瓦尔：是的。对。

Naval: Yes. Yeah.

几乎完全是。我讨厌把它说清楚。而且很多时候，你知道，我现在会放过一些东西。这不只是对公司的品味。这也是对我自己品味的理解，就我喜欢什么、不喜欢什么而言。所以现在有很多公司我不投资，我会放弃那笔投资，因为我不想和创始人一起走走。我没有学到任何东西，或者我只是真诚地对那个品类不感兴趣。我对此不好奇。我不会熬夜去读它或想它。所以它会感觉像一件杂务。或者它也可能只是像，你知道，我在这地球上只有一段短短的生命。我想和这种赚钱方式和自己联系在一起吗？大概不想。还有别的赚钱方式。所以显然那是一种有了财富之后的问题，或有了财富之后的品味。所以这不只是关于生意的品味。这也是对我所珍视的是什么、我想怎样花时间、我想让我的遗产看起来像什么、我会为什么自豪、我会从中学到什么的理解。而这又回到一种努力去懒。懒意味着不工作。不工作意味着不做你不想做的事。如果你想做它，它就不是工作。所以一种理想的生活会是：我正在做的一切、与我相关的一切、与我相关的每一个人，都是我反正都会做的事，你知道，几乎是免费的，并且和我真正喜欢待在一起、能从他们身上学习的人在一起。所以对我来说，那是非常有能力的人。他们做事。他们自我很低，所以你不必应付随之而来的那些乱七八糟。他们非常聪明，所以你总是在向他们学习。

Almost entirely. I hate articulating it. And a lot of times, you know, I'll pass on things now. It's not just taste on the company. It's an understanding of my own tastes in the sense of what I like and what I don't. So there's a lot of companies now that I don't invest in where I will pass up on the investment because I don't want to take a walk with the founder. I didn't learn anything, or I am just genuinely not interested in the category. I'm not curious about it. I'm not going to stay up late reading about or thinking about it. So it's going to feel like a chore. Or it could just be like, you know, I've got one short life on this earth. Do I want to be associated with this way of making money? Probably not. There are other ways to make money. So obviously that's a post-wealth problem or post-wealth taste. So it's not just taste about the business. It's also an understanding of what it is that I value, how I want to spend my time, what I want my legacy to look like, what am I going to be proud of, what am I going to learn from. And again, this is back to an effort to being lazy. And lazy means not working. And not working means not doing things you don't want to do. If you want to do it, it's not work. So an ideal life would be where everything I am doing and everything that I am associated with, everyone that I'm associated with, is something that I would do anyway, you know, almost for free, and being with people that I really enjoy being around and learning from them. So for me that's people who are very capable. They do things. They're very low-ego, so you don't have to deal with all the nonsense that comes with that. They're very intelligent, and so you're always learning from them.

埃里克：纯粹因为你的判断力而获得报酬，实际上是理想的吗？那是你谈过的一件事，但当我们这样谈下去，就像要把你的劳动从你的判断力里剥离出来非常难，至少按某种比例，对吧？

Eric: Is it actually ideal to be paid purely for your judgment? That's a thing you've talked about, but as we're talking through this, it's like it's very hard to extricate your labor from your judgment, at least to some ratio, right?

纳瓦尔：对，没错。你知道，这就是为什么我有另一条推文——如果你想谈数学，它们确实互相矛盾——就像，你知道，你做什么、和谁一起做，比你有多努力更重要，对吧？但是抱歉，还是要尽你所能地努力工作，但你知道你做什么、和谁一起做，比你有多努力更重要。但你仍然必须尽你所能地努力工作。所以它不应当感觉像工作。你知道，这回到：它对你应当感觉像玩，对别人看起来却像工作。那才是你真正想做的。那是理想化的。你没法，你知道，全时段都那样做。你知道，显然不只是努力工作。就像埃隆在经营多少家公司，对吧？开街角杂货店的那个人，花在杂货店里的时间比埃隆花在他任何一家公司上的都多。所以，这并不纯粹只关乎努力工作。话虽如此，你没法在不努力工作的情况下成为一个有很好判断力、很好人脉、很强能力的人，你也不会足够快地建立起你的知识。正是当你超级努力工作、当你真正强烈地投入某件事时，你才会真正做出最大的突破。

Naval: Yeah, that's right. You know, this is why another tweet I had—they do contradict each other if you want to talk math—where it's like, you know, what you do and who you do it with is more important than how hard you work, right? But sorry, work as hard as you can, but you know what you do and who you do it with are more important than how hard you work. But you still have to work as hard as you can. So it shouldn't feel like work. You know, this goes back to like it should feel like play to you but look like work to others. That's really what you want to be doing. That's idealized. You're not going to be able to do that, you know, the full time. You know, it's obviously not just hard work. Like how many companies is Elon running, right? The guy running the corner grocery store puts more hours in the grocery store than Elon puts into any one of his companies. So, it's not just purely about hard work. That said, you don't get to be someone with great judgment and great connections and great capabilities without working hard, and you will not build up your knowledge fast enough. It's when you're working super hard, when you're really intensely into something, that's when you actually make the biggest breakthroughs.

所以，我记得大学时，我有一个计算机科学项目，我们必须写一个编译器。而编译器是很难写的东西。你知道，编译器把一门高级编程语言一层层拆成更低级的编程语言，直到最后变成计算机能理解的一套指令。而写编译器在计算机科学里是相当高阶的。就像，你知道，那个层次上的其他事情也许是写一个操作系统，对吧，一个简单的操作系统。所以这不是开玩笑。而我只记得，当我走进机房，我得花上许多、许多、许多个小时，才能把问题装进脑子里，把前几天自己的代码过一遍，才能多少回到状态，就像：好，这边我当时是这个意思，这边是我卡住的部分，这边这部分很蹩脚，这是一个补丁，这边这个东西能运行而且很美。但如果我离开它一小段时间，就真的得花上几个小时才能再进去。所以后来我发现，我在那台电脑前写那个编译器最有产出的时段，是 24 到 36 小时不睡觉的那些时段。所以我会连续醒着 36 个小时，喝着可口可乐，多少就在写代码，或者只是想和走、写代码、想和走，然后当我终于睡着，问题会从我脑子里掉出去，然后我得花几天恢复，然后我得把整个问题重新装回脑子里。现在，那是一种夸张的例子，但我觉得一般来说，很多时候很多突破发生在你深深进入一个问题时。它就是需要努力工作和时间。

So, I remember back in college, I had this computer science project where we had to write a compiler. And a compiler is a difficult thing to write. You know, a compiler takes a high-level programming language and breaks it down to lower and lower level programming languages until finally it goes into a set of instructions that the computer can understand. And writing compilers is up there in CS. It's like, you know, other things in that level might be like writing an operating system, right, a simple operating system. So it's no joke. And I just remember when I would go in the computer lab, it would take me hours and hours and hours just to load the problem into my head, to go through my own code from the previous few days to kind of come back to speed, like, okay, this is what I meant over here and this is the part where I'm stuck on and this part over here is janky and this is a patch and this thing works over here and it's beautiful. But it would just literally take me hours just to get back into it if I'd been out of it for a little while. And so then I found that the most productive sessions that I had in front of that computer writing that compiler were 24 to 36 hour sessions where I wouldn't sleep. So I would just be up for 36 hours drinking Coca-Cola and just kind of coding or just thinking and walking and coding and thinking and walking, and then when I finally fell asleep the problem would fall out of my head and then it would take me a few days to recover and then I would have to reload the whole problem back into my head. Now, that's an exaggerated kind of example, but I think in general, a lot of times a lot of breakthroughs happen when you're deeply into a problem. It just takes hard work and time.

就像即便和我新公司的联合创始人在一起，他只是一个了不起的人。他才华横溢，就是我见过的最聪明的人之一。自我极低，非常愉快，而且非常努力。只要他醒着，他就在工作。我在这儿和你一起录这个、在这儿闲晃的时候，他大概正在工作。你知道，我甚至在他身上注意到，我们每天都谈。我们有很深的谈话。我们经常同步。而往往谈到一个小时，我已经准备挂掉、放弃，我们已经把所有话题都过完了。所以我们只是在绕圈、闲扯，然后那个大突破就会到来。那个大洞见就会到来。我们就像：啊，而且还有这个。哦，对。现在这一切都连在一起了。你的脑子渗过一个问题就是需要时间。所以，你在一个给定问题上投入的时间越多，你知道，你就越可能有突破。

Like even with my co-founder of my new company, who is just an amazing guy. He's brilliant, just one of the smartest human beings I ever met. Super low-ego, very pleasant, and very hardworking. If he's awake, he's working. He's probably working right now while I'm goofing off recording this with you. You know, I've even noticed with him like we talk every day. We have deep conversations. We catch up a lot. And it'll be an hour into the conversation when I'm already ready to hang up and give up and we've already covered all the topics. So we're just circling and shooting the shit, that the big breakthrough will come. The big insight will come. We're like, ah, but also this. Oh, yes. And now it all connects together. It just takes time for your brain to percolate through a problem. And so, the more time you devote to a given problem, you know, the more likely you are to have a breakthrough.

就像如果你试图解决一个非常难的问题，我有的一个技巧是：我想把它装进我的后台潜意识。我想让我的直觉在上面工作，这样我可以在 48 小时后、在淋浴时有那个突破。而你不能把那个排进日程。你不能给它定时。所以你必须做的是：如此强烈地聚焦于这个问题，以至于你整个心智都吸收了它，甚至你的潜意识也弄明白了，好，这很重要。你梦见它。然后在某个未知的时间尺度上，你会有某种洞见或某种创造力。但除非你早先用你的前台、用你的新皮层，辛苦地把这一切亲手做了一遍，否则你不会有那个。所以我确实觉得努力工作是重要的。你不能放弃它。但如果它感觉像工作，如果你在强迫自己走过那些动作，那你会输，因为某个不觉得这是工作的人在和你竞争，而对他们来说它是有趣的、好玩的，或至少是充实的。

It's like if you're trying to solve a really hard problem, one technique that I have is I want to load it into my background subconscious. I want my intuition working on it so I can have that shower breakthrough 48 hours later. And you can't put that on a schedule. You can't time that. So what you have to do is you have to so intensely focus on the problem that your whole mind absorbs it and even your subconscious figures out, okay, this is important. You're dreaming about it. And then on some unknown time scale, you'll have some insight or some creativity. But you're not going to have that unless you did the hard work of working through all of it manually with your foreground, with your neocortex, early on. So I do think hard work is important. You can't give it up. But if it feels like work, if you're forcing yourself to go through the motions, then you're going to lose, because somebody who doesn't feel like it's work is competing with you, and for them it's entertaining or fun or at least fulfilling.

埃里克：所以那么好的判断力来自这样一种组合：在外显知识上花这么多时间，以至于它多少变成内隐的。

Eric: So then good judgment comes from a combination of like spending so much time on the explicit knowledge that it becomes sort of implicit.

纳瓦尔：没错。对。你把时间花在外显的东西上，然后它变成内隐的。然后有一个阶段，它仍然是内隐的，但你仍然能把它说清楚。而且你知道这是一个类比。这并非严格正确，但我会说有一个点，你每次都必须用逻辑把它推理一遍，那就是基本的决策。然后有一部分你的潜意识可以多少进入其中，然后我会说像现在你有了判断力，并在发展品味。然后我觉得有一个点，你的整个身体都会对它起反应，对吧？因为身体里有智慧，身体里有知识，而在那一点上它就只是品味，然后你和那件东西就算完成了。我是说你并没有完成，显然它总会改善，但现在你处在这门手艺的顶端。

Naval: That's right. Yeah. You spend time on the explicit and then it becomes implicit. And then there's a phase where it's still implicit but you can still articulate it. And you know this is an analogy. This is not strictly correct, but I would say there's a point where you have to reason through it every time using logic, and that's basic decision-making. And there's a part where your subconscious can kind of enter into it, and then I would say like now you have judgment and developing taste. And then I think there's a point where your whole body reacts to it, right? 'Cause there is wisdom in the body, there's knowledge in the body, and at that point it's just taste, and then you're done with that thing. I mean you're not done, obviously it's always going to improve, but now you're top of the craft.

埃里克：约翰·克利斯，那个来自《蒙提派森》的人，有一句关于这个的很棒的话粘在了我身上，就是：你只需要让你的心智以一种友好而持久的方式倚靠在问题上。

Eric: John Cleese, the guy from Monty Python, had a great quote about this that stuck with me, which is you simply have to let your mind rest against the problem in a friendly, persistent way.

纳瓦尔：没错。那实际上说得真好。对，他说得对。你的心智必须参入，你必须聚焦于它，但它必须倚靠在问题上，因为你在推它，但以一种你不会放弃的方式。

Naval: That's right. That's actually really well put. Yeah, he's right. Your mind has to be involved and you have to focus on it, but it has to rest against the problem, because you're pushing on it, but in a way you're not going to give up.

而且对，以一种——是以一种友好的、友好而持久的方式。

And yeah, in a—was it in a friendly, friendly persistent way.

埃里克：友好而持久。

Eric: Friendly and persistent.

纳瓦尔：对。你不能放弃。

Naval: Yeah. You can't give up.

埃里克：而且友好，这样你才不会放弃。

Eric: And friendly so you don't give up.

纳瓦尔：正是。他说得对。对。

Naval: Exactly. He's right. Yeah.

埃里克：我觉得他和奥格威有这些非常有意思的方式，多少去激活他们围绕解决问题的潜意识。

Eric: I think he and Ogilvy have like these really interesting ways of sort of activating their subconscious around problem solving.

纳瓦尔：对。每个我认为有深度创造力的人，都既非常、非常努力地工作，但又知道它必须是享受的，否则你永远到不了那里。有一位画家的一句话，我想是巴勃罗·德斯·索罗斯、索罗索托，类似那样。我不记得名字了，但那句话很好。他说，你知道，十年来，我一周七天、一天十六个小时地画画，现在他们叫我天才。

Naval: Yeah. Everyone who I think is deeply creative both works really, really hard, but yet knows that it has to be enjoyable, otherwise you'll never get there. There was a line by a painter, I think Pablo Des Soros, Sorosoto, something like that. I don't remember the name, but the line was good. And he said, you know, for 10 years, I've painted seven days a week, 16 hours a day, and now they call me a genius.

埃里克：AI 有判断力吗？

Eric: Does AI have judgment?

纳瓦尔：不。我不这么认为。我不会指望 AI 去运用判断力。AI 所拥有的，是惊人的信息检索能力。所以它可以交叉关联全部人类知识，而如果人类以前已经弄明白了，它就能给你那个常规的正确答案，对已解决的问题，在大多数情形里那会是对的。所以你知道，如果你试图弄清，比如，某一具体点上的法律是什么，它可以去读全部法律文本，并根据全部常规智慧告诉你当前对法律的解释是什么。但如果你想做某种有创造性的事，或某种还没有人弄明白的全新的事，或者你在处理一个带有非常具体情境的高度复杂问题，你不要让 AI 替你做那个判断。所以我并不必然把 AI 想成拥有判断力，但我把它想成终极的杠杆式信息检索工具。此外，人类有价值。他们有观点。他们有约束性的原则。他们有支撑其相信为真、相信为不真之基础的东西。而这因人而异。它适应于他们所处的环境。AI 采取的是一种放之四海而皆准的路径，依据的要么是教科书或常规文献里的东西，要么是那些把数据标进 AI 的数据标注员当时怎么想。

Naval: No. I don't think so. I wouldn't count on AI to like exercise judgment. What AI has is AI has incredible information-retrieval capability. So it can cross-correlate all human knowledge, and if humans have figured it out before, it can give you the conventional correct answer, which is going to be correct in most cases for solved problems. So you know if you're trying to figure out like, you know, what is the law on a specific point, it can like read all the legal texts and tell you what the current interpretation of the law is according to all the conventional wisdom. But if you're looking to do something creative or something brand new that no one has yet figured out, or you're dealing with a highly complex problem with a very specific situation, you don't have the AI make that judgment call for you. So I don't think of the AI necessarily as having judgment, but I think of it as like the ultimate leverage information-retrieval tool. Also, humans have values. They have points of view. They have binding principles. They have things that undergird the foundation of what they believe to be true and what they believe to not be true. And this is idiosyncratic to the person. It's adapted to the environment they're in. The AI is taking a one-size-fits-all approach according to what's either in textbooks or conventional literature or what the data labelers who are labeling the data into the AI thought of it.

我觉得 AI 对人们看起来像魔法，因为很难把它包进你的脑子里，你知道，这些在所有这些高度数学化的维度里运转的极其巨大的数据集能做到什么。我是说它们能检索和交叉关联的信息量简直惊人。就像你每次都跑一次谷歌搜索，非常仔细地读完前一千条结果，然后把它们交叉关联，并且能把它们缝在一起，而你一路上会犯几个错，就像 AI 那样。但你知道那种水平的研究，再加上——我确实觉得它有一些惊人的计算能力，因为它能写程序，它能想出一些算法，你知道，低于人类创造力的水平，但高于一台单纯的计算器。所以，我觉得这些 AI 是了不起的捷径，但它们是在你不需要完美答案的已解决问题上，给你答案的捷径。所以，我听到的一句俏皮话是，你知道，当错误答案也没关系时，AI 很棒。就像，答案错了你不会死。答案错了你不会亏很多钱。但对任何有创造性的、或需要在边缘做判断的事——那才是你真正为此获得报酬的，你为创造力获得报酬。

I think AI just looks like magic to people because it's very hard to wrap into your mind, you know, what these incredibly huge data sets operating in all these highly mathematical dimensions are capable of. I mean the amount of information they can retrieve and cross-correlate is just astounding. It's like if you ran a Google search every time and you read all top thousand results very carefully and then you cross-correlated them and you were able to stitch them together, and you would make a few mistakes along the way just like the AI does. But you know that level of research combined with—I do think it has some tremendous calculation abilities, 'cause it can write programs and it figures out some algorithms, you know, below the level of human creativity but above a mere calculator. So, I think the AIs are incredible shortcuts, but they're shortcuts to giving you answers to solved problems where you don't need a perfect answer. So, the one quip I heard was, you know, AI is great when wrong answers are okay. Like, you're not going to die because the answer is wrong. You're not going to lose a lot of money 'cause the answer is wrong. But for anything creative or requiring judgment at the edge, which is what you really get paid for, you get paid for creativity.

所以，AI 把所有人的船都抬起来。如果你有一个 AI，每个人都有一个 AI。你得到 AI 的答案，每个人都得到 AI 的答案。没有 alpha。再也没有优势。所以，在那个意义上，AI 抬高了潮水。而眼下，你会得到一种优势，因为大多数人还没用 AI，或用得不多，或者他们还不知道怎样把它用得足够好，去对付前沿问题。所以，再说一次，像任何一项技术一样，如果你是早期采用者，如果你把它杠杆用得好，你会做得极好。所以这是我在 Twitter 上的那句俏皮话：并不是 AI 将取代软件工程师，而是 AI 将让软件工程师取代其他所有人。而且我坚持这一点，因为软件工程师——不是因为他们在写软件，因为软件工程师总是在用最新的工具。他们是结构化的、逻辑的系统思考者，试图建造一个系统去解决一个具体问题。而现在多亏了 AI，他们能解决越来越多的问题。他们能让汽车自己开。他们能让机器人走来走去。他们能做出专家系统或 AI 系统，在人类不必进入回路的情况下做出某些层次的决定。而那只是给了软件工程师惊人的杠杆。所以所有那些说编程已死、去学艺术或去学手艺的人，他们是傻瓜。他们完全错了。而软件工程师比以往任何时候都更富有、更有力量。最近的证明刚刚出现：马克·扎克伯格付上亿美元的待遇去招个别机器学习工程师，因为杠杆最高的工程师是那些在建造这些 AI 系统的人，然后他们下面是那些在使用这些 AI 系统的人，再往下是所有被这些使用 AI 系统的工程师所影响的人。

So, the AI lifts the boat for everybody. If you have an AI, everybody has an AI. You get the AI answer, everybody gets the AI answer. There's no alpha. There's no edge anymore. So, in that sense, the AI raises the tide. And right now, you're going to get an edge because most people aren't using AI or aren't using it for, you know, a lot, or they don't know how to use it well enough for bleeding-edge problems. So, again, like any piece of technology, if you're an early adopter, if you leverage it well, you're going to do extremely well. So this was my quip on Twitter that it's not that AI is going to replace software engineers, that AI is going to let software engineers replace everybody else. And I stand by that, because software engineers—not 'cause they're writing software, because software engineers are always using the latest tools. They're structured logical systems thinkers who are trying to build a system to solve a specific problem. And now thanks to AI they can solve more and more problems. They can make cars drive themselves. They can make robots walk around. They can make expert systems or AI systems that will make certain levels of decisions without humans having to be in the loop. And that just gives software engineers amazing leverage. So all the people saying that programming is dead, go learn art or the trades, they're idiots. They're just completely wrong. And software engineers are getting richer and more powerful than ever. And the most recent proof of that just popped up with Mark Zuckerberg paying hundred-million-dollar packages to recruit individual machine-learning engineers, because the most leveraged engineers are the ones who are building these AI systems, and then the ones below them are the ones who are using these AI systems, and then even below them is everybody affected by these engineers using these AI systems.

埃里克：对。你以前谈过，就像机器人革命已经在这里了。它只是被装进了数据中心，而那是十年前的话。

Eric: Yeah. You had talked before about like the robot revolution is already here. It's just packed into data centers, and that was 10 years ago.

纳瓦尔：没错。对。机器人革命已经在这里很久了。这颗星球上有数万亿个机器人。但它们只是被装进数据中心。它们不需要腿和胳膊。它们只是在计算。但现在有了 AI 和 AI 智能体。而且我想象，你知道，一个能流畅使用那些工具的人所能得到的杠杆是数量级的。它非常巨大。我是说，现在你可以用自然语言对他们编程。现在他们可以访问自然语言数据库。现在你只要灌入大量数据就能对他们编程。甚至不必编程。他们自己编程，他们为你编程。但做一个结构化的思考者仍然有帮助。就像我读计算机科学和物理时最有用的课，实际上是那些，你知道，我喜欢我那些关于计算机硬件、计算机网络、统计学、以及许多更深的物理的课——不是因为我直接用到了那些，而是因为它帮助了我的概念，帮助我以某些方式思考。所以即便你并不在写网络代码，知道计算机在底下怎样做网络也极其有用，因为那样你就知道什么是可能的。

Naval: That's right. Yeah. The robot revolution has been here for a long time. There are trillions of robots on the planet. But they're just packed into data centers. They don't need legs and arms. They're just computing. But now there's AI and AI agents. And I imagine, you know, the leverage that somebody who's fluent with those tools can get is orders of magnitude. It's massive. I mean, now you can program them speaking natural languages. Now they can access natural-language databases. Now you can program them just by pouring in huge amounts of data. Not even having to program them. They program themselves, they program for you. But it still helps to be a structured thinker. Like my most useful classes when I was doing computer science and physics were actually things on, you know, like I loved my courses on computer hardware, on computer networking, on statistics, on a lot of the deeper physics—and not because I got to use those directly, but because it just helped me with concepts, just helped me think in certain ways. So even if you aren't writing networking code, knowing how the computer is doing networking underneath is incredibly useful, because then you know what's possible.

史蒂夫·乔布斯和他的团队，使他们能够组装出 iPhone 这类东西的天才，是因为他们在非常深的层次上理解什么是可能的，什么在技术上是可能的，什么处在前沿，什么勉强可能，什么几乎不可能，以及什么实际上不可能。所以他们知道这些不同事物之间的界线，而且他们从材料科学上知道它。他们从制造上知道它。他们从供应链和交付能力上知道它。他们从成本上知道它。他们从计算机编程上知道它。他们从硬件上知道它。他们从电气工程上知道它。他们从电池需求和空间需求上知道它，他们从带宽需求上知道它。他们有理解所有这些不同零件的人，并能做出权衡，组装出这颗完美的、宝石一般的小小智能手机，它突然之间就成了你口袋里一台真正的个人计算机。而这就是使 iPhone 伟大的东西。我会争辩，现代最伟大的产品仍然是 iPhone。我是说，我敬畏他们做成的事。

Steve Jobs and his team, their genius that allowed them to assemble things like the iPhone was because they understood at a very deep level what was possible, what was technologically possible, what was in the bleeding edge, what was barely possible, what was almost impossible, and what was actually impossible. So they knew the lines between those different things, and they knew it in terms of the material science. They knew it in terms of manufacturing. They knew it in supply chains and deliverability. They knew it in costing. They knew it in computer programming. They knew it in hardware. They knew it in electrical engineering. They knew it in battery requirements and space requirements and they knew it in bandwidth requirements. They had people who understood all these different pieces and could make the trade-offs to assemble this perfect jewel-like little smartphone that all of a sudden was a true personal computer in your pocket. And that's what made the iPhone great. I would argue the greatest product of the modern age is still the iPhone. I mean, I'm in awe of what they managed to do.

但如果你只是看眼下每个人都渴望、都不会放弃的那一个设备，至少在 2025 年的这个时刻，如果你走到人们面前说你可以拥有任何你想要的东西中的一件，但如果你不拿那件东西，你就完全不能拥有它。他们全都会拿 iPhone。所以，而在那之前，我觉得激发过同样程度渴望的上一个产品，大概是 MacBook，再之前是麦金塔。所以向苹果致敬，但他们是这颗星球上最好的产品建造者，因为他们在细节技术层面上深深理解所有那些艰难的权衡。即便你给了乔布斯和他的团队一个 AI 去做所有的氛围编程，你知道，以及所有的氛围设计，如果他们不深深知道 AI 正在隐藏的抽象是什么、它在哪里犯错、它不理解什么、以及所有这些零件怎样拼在一起，他们也造不出那么好的产品。因为他们真正在创造某种新东西。而如果你在创造某种新东西，你仍然需要理解一切，一直到很深、很深的细节层面。只不过 AI，至少按 2025 年当前的结构——这些现代 AI，它们会替你去掉苦差事。如果这事以前做过，那你知道，带着一个小小的幻觉率，它们今天大概就能替你做。而且它们越来越好。所以最终所有以前做过的东西，你都可以让它们做。但那没关系。对已经做过的事情，你没法以任何有意义的方式获得报酬。你也不应当想要那样。那极其无聊。

But if you just look at like the one device that every human craves and would not give up right now, at least this moment in time in 2025, if you went to people and said you can have one of anything you want, but if you don't take that thing, you can't have it at all. They would all take the iPhone. So, and the last product before that that I think inspired that same level of desire was probably the MacBook, and before that it was a Macintosh. So kudos to Apple, but they were the best product builders on the planet because they understood all the hard trade-offs deep down at the detail technical levels. Even if you'd given Jobs and his team an AI to do all the vibe coding, you know, and all the vibe design, they could not have built that good of a product without knowing deep down what is the abstraction that the AI was hiding, where was it making mistakes, what did it not understand, and how do all the pieces fit together? Because they were truly creating something new. And if you're creating something new, you still need to understand everything down to the deep, deep detail levels. It's just that the AI, at least as currently structured in 2025—these modern AIs, they will take the drudgery out of it for you. If it's been done before, then you know with a small hallucination rate, they can probably do it for you today. And they're getting better and better. So eventually all the stuff that's been done before you can have them do. But that's okay. You can't get paid in any meaningful way for things that have already been done before. Nor should you want to be. It's incredibly boring.

有一件事，你知道，一个好的创业者会告诉你：他们讨厌日复一日做同一件事。那表明自动化失败了，想象力也失败了。所以好的创业者把事情自动化。而这就是为什么，顺便说，我没遇见过哪个创业者说：哦，AI 是坏东西。对创业者来说，它是工具。它是机会。创业者并不害怕 AI 取代他们，对吧？没有哪个创业者会被一个 AI 取代。他们也许会被另一个把 AI 用得更好的创业者取代。而他们需要把 AI 用好，就像他们需要把任何工具用好。创业者并不比他们曾经害怕 MacBook 取代他们、或 AirPods 取代他们、或自动驾驶汽车取代他们，更害怕 AI 取代他们。它只是又一个机会。所以我是说，我的愿景、信念和希望是：每个人都想有创造力。每个人都想控制自己的命运。每个人都想做出新东西。每个人都想被卷入、被挑战。而且你知道，也许对有些人来说太晚了，因为他们已经放弃了，对吧，犬儒地。但我觉得每个孩子当然都是这样。你知道，每个孩子一开始都超级有创造力。每个孩子一开始都只是想改变周围的现实，去做新的、令人喜悦的事情。没有孩子想要苦差事。没有孩子在第一天就放弃了希望。所以我确实觉得更新的世代会利用这些东西。

One of the things that, you know, a good entrepreneur will tell you is they hate it when they're doing the same thing day in, day out. That shows that there's a failure of automation and a failure of imagination. So good entrepreneurs automate things. And that's why, by the way, there's no entrepreneur I've met who says, oh, AI is a bad thing. To an entrepreneur, it's a tool. It's an opportunity. The entrepreneurs are not scared of AI replacing them, right? No entrepreneur is going to be replaced by an AI. They might be replaced by another entrepreneur who uses AI better. And they need to get good with AI just like they need to get good with any tool. Entrepreneurs are not afraid of AI replacing them any more than they were afraid of MacBooks replacing them or AirPods replacing them or self-driving cars replacing them. It's just another opportunity. So I mean my vision and belief and hope is that every human being wants to be creative. Every human being wants to control their own destiny. Every human being wants to make new things. Every human being wants to be engaged and challenged. And you know, maybe it's too late for some people 'cause they've given up, right, cynically. But I think certainly every child does. You know, every child starts out super creative. Every child starts out just wanting to alter their reality around them and to do new and delightful things. No child wants drudgery. No child has given up hope on day one. So I do think that newer generations will take advantage of these things.

埃里克：对。我觉得，你知道，有些人容易把创业者看成一个固定的人群类别，而不是每个人都可以立志去成为的某种东西。而你以前说过，你知道，这颗星球上有 70 亿人。

Eric: Yeah. I think, you know, it's tempting for some people to see entrepreneur as a fixed class of people rather than something that everyone could aspire to be. And you said before, you know, there are 7 billion people on the planet.

学习幸福

Learning Happiness

纳瓦尔：我希望有一天会有 70 亿家公司。

Naval: I hope one day there are 7 billion companies.

埃里克：而我觉得很多工具和结构，我们正在朝那个方向走。

Eric: And I feel like a lot of the tools and structure, we're moving in that direction.

纳瓦尔：这是好事。

Naval: It's a good thing.

埃里克：幸福。我真的喜欢这个框架：它是一种高度个人的技能，可以像健身或营养那样学习，或者像我们谈过的财富那样。而这看起来真的像是你那些想法里一条非常常见的线，就像基础原则和个人应用的结合。

Eric: Happiness. I really like the frame of it being a highly personal skill that can be learned like fitness or nutrition, or as we talked about, wealth. And that really seems like a very common thread of your ideas, like the combination of foundational principles and personal application.

纳瓦尔：对。我是说你看，幸福是那种对不同的人就意味着不同东西的词之一。有些人会说我不想幸福。另一些人会说你什么意思，我天生不幸福，或者我天生就不快乐？每个人都有一个借口、一个理由或一种解释。这是一个如此个人的话题，对我来说很难谈。我是说，我最近对它的想法是：根本没有幸福这种东西，对吧？所以，我可以就它给你二十句相互矛盾的陈述。其中每一句，你知道，要么你会当成陈词滥调或荒谬而不予理会，要么会让你思考。但愿它会让你思考。但我最近对它的想法是：我并不真的在乎幸福，我甚至不确定它是否真的存在。你知道，幸福是心智的一种建构。它是一个想法，而你会有念头说：「哦，我现在幸福了。」然后当那些念头消失时，正在发生什么？你不幸福还是幸福？那里什么都没有。对吧？这就是佛教那个老说法，比如无我，对吧？当你有一个念头时，那个念头进来。那个念头声称自己是自我。那个念头从某处浮现，从一个我们称之为自我、定义得非常非常差的实体。而当你仔细去找它时，很难弄清那东西到底是什么。所以在那个意义上，幸福就像自我本身一样，只是一个念头。所以幸福只是一个念头，不幸福也只是一个念头。所以你可以有幸福的念头，也可以有不幸福的念头，而有某些行为和心态会导向更多幸福的念头，有某些行为和心态会导向更多不幸福的念头。

Naval: Yeah. I mean look, happiness is one of those words that just means different things to different people. Some people will say I don't want to be happy. Other people will say what do you mean I'm naturally unhappy, or I'm naturally not happy? Everyone has an excuse or a reason or interpretation. It's such a personal topic, it's very hard for me to talk about. I mean, my latest thinking on it is like there's no such thing as happiness, right? And so, I can give you 20 contradictory statements on it. Each one of them, you know, either you'll dismiss as a cliché or absurdity or will make you think. Hopefully, it'll make you think. But my latest thinking on it is like, I don't really care about happiness and I'm not even sure it really exists. You know, happiness is a construct of the mind. It's an idea, and you have thoughts that say, "Oh, I'm happy now." And then when those thoughts are gone, what's going on? Are you unhappy or are you happy? There's nothing there. Right? This is the old Buddhist thing of like no-self, right? When you have a thought, that thought comes in. That thought claims to be the self. That thought emerges from somewhere, from an entity that we call the self that is very, very poorly defined. And when you look for it carefully, it's very hard to make out what the heck that thing is. So in that sense happiness is just like the self itself is just a thought. So happiness is just a thought and unhappiness is just a thought. And so you can have happy thoughts or you can have unhappy thoughts, and there are certain behaviors and mindsets that will lead towards more happy thoughts, and there are certain behaviors and mindsets that will lead to more unhappy thoughts.

然而，如果你认同那个念头，如果你像是：我就是那个念头。是我在有那个念头。那么在那一刻，你就创造了一个在你有那个念头时是幸福的「我」。然后当那个念头消失时，那里不再有「我」，也没有念头，也没有幸福。然后当不幸福的念头在那里、而你认同它时，你就创造了一个念头：我不幸福，我有一个不幸福的念头，那就是我，我不幸福。然后当那个念头消失时，那里没有人，也没有念头，也没有不幸福。然后那整个建构是通过在你心里来来去去的念头被创造出来的。我甚至犹豫要不要说「心」，因为就像：当你没有一个关于心的念头时，这颗心在哪里，对吧？就像我们在这里谈的这个话题的一切，都存在于念头的层面上。但如果你并不强烈陷入那些念头，或不认同那些念头，那你就不会有幸福或不幸福的概念。而外面的世界里当然没有幸福。这有两种非常不同的含义。一种含义是：外面的世界里没有幸福，因为这是你内在拥有的一种心智建构。所以你可以重新解释你的过去。你可以重新解释你的处境，去常常改变那些东西或你的习惯。但第二种是：外面的世界里没有幸福，而且外面的世界极其无常。它不在你的控制之中。外面的世界里总有不幸福的理由，同样，外面的世界里也没有不幸福。你是在心里把它编出来的，因为它没有按你想要的方式走。而世界本身并不在乎。而那个外面的世界能让你不幸福的程度，不会超过你允许它的程度。

However, if you identify with the thought, if you're like, I am that thought. It's me having that thought. Then at that moment, you've created a me that is happy while you're having that thought. And then when that thought disappears, there's no me there anymore and there's no thought and there's no happy. And then when the unhappy thought is there and you identify with it, then you've created a thought that I'm unhappy and I'm having an unhappy thought and that's me and I'm unhappy. And then when that thought disappears, there's no one there and there's no thought and there's no unhappiness. And then that whole construct is created through thoughts that are kind of coming and going in your mind. And I even hesitate to say mind because like where is this mind when you're not having a thought of the mind, right? It's like everything that we talk about here of this topic exists at the level of thought. But if you are not strongly into those thoughts or identify with those thoughts, then you're not going to have a concept of happy or unhappy. And there's certainly no happiness outside in the world. Both in very different meanings. One meaning is there's no happiness outside in the world because this is a mental construct you have internally. And so you can reinterpret your past. You can reinterpret your situation to often change those things or your habits. But the second is that there's no happiness in the outside world and that the outside world is incredibly impermanent. It's out of your control. There are always reasons to be unhappy from the outside world, the same way there's no unhappiness in the outside world too. You're making that up in your mind 'cause it's not going the way you want it to. And the world itself doesn't care. And that outside world can't make you any more unhappy than you let it.

现在这有一些明显的例外。比如如果你处在身体疼痛中，你的身体是不幸福的。那会让你的心不幸福。那会让你强烈认同疼痛，而你会不幸福。对吧？事实上这就是为什么你可以争辩说，整个心的建构之所以存在——是为了感觉那东西，为了回应疼痛。你知道，疼痛的一个定义是：疼痛要求回应，对吧？疼痛是一个东西。它就像：现在就注意我。放下你正在做的事。停止做白日梦。现在就注意我。而情绪上的疼痛是那上面的一种变体。但所以幸福和不幸福，这些是非常个人的概念。我会说我对此的新想法，我最近的想法，随时可能改变。我不是一个开悟的存在。我不是一个永远幸福的人。所以这字面上只是一个人的个人探索。我对此最近的想法是：我并不必然想要幸福。我有点只是想要，你知道，禅宗那种对事物本来样子感到没问题。而我觉得当我的欲望很少、并且是有意识地选择的，会有助于处在那种状态。当我出于非自私的理由做事情——而这在某种意义上是自私的。就像如果我做比我自己更大的事情，那无论最终结果是什么，我会更能接受。比如，如果我真诚地试图让世界变得更好——我不是说我是，但假设我是特蕾莎修女或某种等同的人，而我真诚地试图让世界变得更好。如果世界不让我把它变得更好，我不会把它当成针对我个人，对吧？因为那不是一项个人使命。同样，当有人为上帝做这件事，或有人以无私的方式为他们的孩子做这件事，当事情并不完全按你想要的方式发展时，实际上会少疼一些。所以拥有更高的动机在那方面是有帮助的。你知道，抑郁的人在反刍自己。他们真的在想自己。所以不想自己，你知道，是有帮助的。你知道，我关于这个的简单推文是：你越想自己，你就会越不幸福。就这么简单。所以，我对平和更感兴趣。那么，我是否一直想要平和？我不知道。那看起来有点无聊。

Now there are obvious exceptions to this. Like if you're in physical pain, your body is unhappy. That will make your mind unhappy. That will make you strongly identify with the pain and you will be unhappy. Right? That's why in fact you could argue that the whole mind construct exists—it's to feel that thing, to respond to pain. You know, one definition of pain is that pain demands a response, right? Pain is a thing. It's like pay attention to me right now. Drop what you're doing. Stop daydreaming. Pay attention to me right now. And emotional pain is a variation on that. But so happiness and unhappiness, these are very personal concepts. I would say my new thinking on this, my latest thinking, subject to change. I'm not an enlightened being. I'm not a perpetually happy person. So it's literally just one person's personal exploration. My latest thinking on this is that I don't necessarily want happiness. I kind of want just, you know, the Zen thing of being okay with things the way they are. And I think it helps to be in that state when my desires are few and consciously chosen. When I am doing things for non-selfish reasons—and that is selfish in a way. Like if I do things that are larger than myself, then I will be more okay with whatever the ultimate outcome is. Like for example, if I'm genuinely trying to make the world a better place—I'm not saying I am, but say I was Mother Teresa or some equivalent and I was genuinely trying to make the world a better place. I wouldn't take it personally if the world wouldn't let me make it a better place, right? Because it's not a personal mission. The same way when like someone's doing it for God or someone's doing it for their children in a selfless way, it actually hurts less when you know things don't work out exactly the way that you want them to. So having higher motives is helpful that way. You know, depressed people are ruminating upon themselves. They're really thinking about themselves. So not thinking about yourself is, you know, is helpful. You know, my simple tweet on this was the more you think about yourself, the less happy you're going to be. It's that simple. So, I'm more interested in peace. Now, do I want peace all the time? I don't know. It seems kind of boring.

有一种论点认为无聊——这是叔本华的论点——无聊揭示了存在的空虚。因为当你什么都不做，当你有那么短暂的一刻感到满足，你所有的欲望都实现了，你就有点只是坐在那里。会发生什么？无聊立刻出现。为什么？因为存在本身多少是空虚的。所以然后你想去再做点别的。这是悲观的观点。乐观的观点其实是：那是心。你的心对快感上瘾，所以它想跳起来去做某种愉快的事，因为那是它习惯的。也许有帮助的是认识到：所有问题都是心所创造的，这有充分的理由。我不是说心是坏东西。它在那里是为了保护你的身体，但我们有一种承担并不影响我们的问题的方式。政治就是一个好例子。你知道，地球另一边的战争，或政党，诸如此类。而你的心就是捡起那些并不重要的问题和欲望。所以，对，如果你是佛陀，你可以活在无心里。但如果你是一个正常人，你至少可以——一个理性的人可以通过培养对直接控制之外事物的一种策略性冷漠，找到某种程度的平和。对吧？我过去把这话说得更好，但我现在稍微加一点限定，因为我其实是在当场重构，而不是在引用，因为我觉得你只是必须抓住你自己，对吧？

There's one argument that boredom—this is a Schopenhauer argument—that boredom reveals the emptiness of existence. Because when you're not doing anything, when you're satisfied for one brief moment, all your desires are fulfilled, you're kind of just sitting around. What happens? Boredom shows up immediately. Why? Because existence itself is somewhat empty. And so then you want to go and do something else. This is the pessimistic viewpoint. The optimistic viewpoint is actually that's the mind. Your mind is addicted to pleasure and so it wants to jump up and go do something pleasurable because that's what it's used to. What may help is to realize that all problems are mind-created, for a good reason. I'm not saying the mind is a bad thing. It's there to protect your body, but we have a way of taking on problems that don't affect us. Politics is a good example. You know, wars halfway around the world, or political parties, or so on. And your mind just picks up problems and desires that are just not important. And so, yeah, if you're Buddha, you can live in no-mind. But if you're a normal person, you can at least—a rational person can find some level of peace by cultivating a strategic indifference to things that are outside of their immediate control. Right? I've said this better in the past, but I'm caveating it a little bit now because I'm actually reconstructing on the fly rather than quoting, because I think you just have to catch yourself, right?

冥想的目的不是让你开悟。它不会做到那一点。它甚至不必然是为了立刻让你更幸福，或解决一个问题，或让你平静下来。冥想的目的只是变得能观察自己。而当你能观察自己、更有自我觉察时，你会抓住你的心在做那些不符合你长期最佳利益的事。然后你可以重置它，或者你可以停下来问：这是真的吗？这是我真的想经历的事吗？这是一个真正重要的欲望吗？这是我需要为之受苦的事吗？哪样更容易：重新解释这件事，还是真正去处理底下的问题？因为我们总是在捡起事情来做。所以总之，你知道，如果我只是想要所谓的幸福，对吧？再说一次，我不确定有这种东西。就像如果我想要平和，那我就什么都不做，但我会无聊。那么，那是个问题吗？是的，我觉得那是个问题。我不想无聊。那么，我能升到心的层面之上，像一个禅僧那样吗？也许。但我也有点喜欢生活，你知道？我喜欢拥有某些天赋。我喜欢自我实现。我喜欢做事。你知道，假设佛教徒是对的，你知道，而我是梵。那我为什么不就停留在梵里？我为什么来到这里？对吧？这是那个古老的问题。我为什么在这里？对吧？而我觉得对「我为什么在这里」唯一实际的回答，就是去做我所做的。所以如果我只是去做我所做的，不过度思考它，那它自然会把我带到一个幸福的地方。这又回到「划呀划呀划你的船」，对吧？是轻轻地顺流而下。只要我继续划，并且轻轻地划，我就多少在玩我注定要玩的那场游戏。

The purpose of meditation is not to get you enlightened. It's not going to do that. It's not even necessarily to make you happier immediately or to solve a problem or to calm you down. The purpose of meditation is just to become self-observant. And when you're self-observant and more self-aware, you catch your mind doing things that are not in your long-term best interests. And then you can reset it, or you can stop and say, is this true? Is this something I really want to go through? Is this a really important desire? Is this something that I need to suffer over? What's easier: reinterpreting this or actually dealing with the underlying problem? Because we're always just picking up things to do. So anyway, you know, if I just wanted to be quote-unquote happy, right? Again, I'm not sure there's such a thing. Like if I wanted to be peaceful, then I would just do nothing, but I would get bored. Now, is that a problem? Yeah, I think that's a problem. I don't want to be bored. Now, could I rise above the level of the mind and be like a Zen monk? Maybe. But I also kind of like life, you know? I like having certain gifts. I like self-actualizing. I like doing things. You know, let's say that the Buddhists are right, you know, and I am Brahman. Then why don't I just stay Brahman? Why did I come here? Right? It's the age-old question. Why am I here? Right? And I think the only practical answer to why am I here is to do what I do. And so if I just do what I do without overthinking it, then it's naturally going to lead me to a place of happiness. This is back to row, row, row your boat, right? It's gently down the stream. As long as I keep rowing and I'm doing it gently, I'm kind of playing the game I'm meant to play.

埃里克：对。我觉得这本书真正似乎能引起人们共鸣的事情之一，是关于财富和幸福的原则的结合。就像那么多人只谈其中之一，却从不谈两者之间的张力、对照。而我觉得很多人多少向你寻求的——至于你是否认为自己是这方面的好例子，我会好奇听一听——是一个投入、但却平和地投入的人。

Eric: Yeah. I think one of the things that really seemed to resonate with people about this book is the combination of principles about wealth and happiness. Like so many people talk about one or the other, but never the tension, the contrast between the two. And I think what a lot of people sort of look to you for—and whether you consider yourself a good example of this or not, I'll be curious to hear—is someone who's engaged but peacefully.

纳瓦尔：对，我不确定我是完全平和的。但肯定比我以前更平和。我没有禅僧那么平和。但我大概比你平均那个紧张的创始人平和得多。其中一部分——有钱是有帮助的。我不会在这件事上撒谎，对吧？但我觉得我在赚到一大笔钱之前就已经更平和了。而那是因为我当时其实处在一个压力非常高的时期，我只是必须弄清楚怎样对付它。而我读了一些书，你知道，克里希那穆提和奥修，还有，你知道，对，我用过一些迷幻剂，你知道，像湾区其他所有人一样。但不是以那种方式，你知道，我觉得有些人他们会去做，他们去火人节，他们用他们的迷幻剂，并且为迷幻剂而活。不，它是一条信息。你接到信息，你挂掉电话。然后我做了很多冥想。我做了很多阅读。我做了很多思考。我做了很多内省。我指不出任何一颗单独的银弹。但这一切都有帮助。它帮助我在着手那件事时保持禅意与平和，你知道，而不是痛苦。再说一次，我有点想要一切的懒人解决方案。所以，我想要开悟的懒人解决方案。我想要财富的懒人解决方案。我想要艺术和创造力的懒人思维解决方案。而我说的懒，是指做某种对我来说感觉不像工作的事，对吧？所以思考对我来说感觉不像工作。思考那些大问题，就像不去想它们会比想它们更费力。对吧？如果我深夜来回踱步，已经午夜了，我独自一人。我会自然地开始琢磨：这一切到底是怎么回事？你知道，为什么——你怎么能对你的存在不好奇？对吧？而我觉得我们都只是放弃了。

Naval: Yeah, I'm not sure I'm completely peaceful. But definitely more peaceful than I used to be. I'm not as peaceful as a Zen monk. But I'm probably way more peaceful than your average nervous founder. Some of it—it helps to have money. I'm not going to lie about it, right? But I think I got more peaceful before I'd made a bunch of money. And it was because I was actually in a very high-stress period and I just had to figure out how to deal with it. And I read some books, you know, Krishnamurti and Osho, and, you know, yeah, I did some psychedelics, you know, like everybody else in the Bay Area. But not in a way where, you know, I think some people they do, they go to Burning Man, they do their psychedelics, and they live for psychedelics. No, it's a message. You get the message, you hang up the phone. And then I did a lot of meditation. I did a lot of reading. I did a lot of thinking. I did a lot of introspection. And I can't point to any single silver bullet. But all of it helped. It helps me be Zen and peaceful while I go about the thing, you know, not be miserable. Again, I kind of want the lazy-man solution to everything. So, I want the lazy-man solution to enlightenment. I want the lazy-man solution to wealth. I want the lazy-mindset solution to art and creativity. And by lazy, I mean working on something that doesn't feel like work to me, right? So thinking doesn't feel like work to me. Thinking on the big questions, like it would be more work to not think about them than to think about them. Right? If I'm pacing around late at night and it's midnight, I'm by myself. I will naturally start just pondering like what is this all about? You know, why—how could you not be curious about your existence? Right? And I think we all just give up.

我们只是像：「哦，对。好吧。曾经有那么一个阶段我对存在感到好奇。我到了某个年纪就把那事熬过去了。而现在我只是一只猴子，将要，你知道，做一堆事情然后死去。」

We're just like, "Oh, yeah. Okay. There was a point where I was curious about my existence. I got over that when I was a certain age. And now I'm just a monkey that's going to, you know, do a bunch of things and then die."

埃里克：我想这其中也有它的乐趣。这取决于你较劲这些问题时是否觉得有趣，还是觉得它们折磨人。

Eric: I suppose it has its fun. It depends whether you're having fun wrestling with those questions or whether you find them torturous.

纳瓦尔：我觉得它们不可避免。我觉得每个人都在跟它们较劲。只是承认这一点并不流行。我觉得我对 Twitter 做出的贡献，不是靠做了某种独特的事，而是靠成为最先做这件事的人之一。你知道现在有很多账号，坦率地说以非常有意思的方式说着同样的事或不同的事。我只是早做了那件事。而我觉得人们——我们在生活中划界限，对吧？对我来说史蒂夫·乔布斯之所以是创业的典范，原因之一是他不只是一个商业人士，他也是一个艺术家，而且你知道他用过 LSD 并谈论它，他也做他的冥想。他几乎成了僧人，而他是禅宗佛教徒。所以他也试图解决生活中的一切，所有那些大问题。而你知道我把它想通了，多少回到的是——我最近其实有一条关于这个的推文，忘了这条推文的确切措辞，但我知道那些原则，也就是什么是重要的。原则是：首先你必须保持健康。你生来健康，你不会以健康收场，所以你能把那件事拖得越久越好，对吧？所以这就是为什么我们会被布莱恩·约翰逊这样的人激励，或者你知道无论你的健身导师或健康导师是谁。你必须保持健康。那是底线。然后你想变得富有。

Naval: I think they're inevitable. I think everybody wrestles with them. It's just not popular to acknowledge it. I think the contribution that I made to Twitter was not by doing something unique, but by being one of the first to do it. You know now there's a lot of accounts that frankly say the same things or different things in very interesting ways. I was just early in doing that. And I think people—we draw boundaries in life, right? One of the reasons why Steve Jobs was to me the apotheosis of entrepreneurship was because he was not just a business person but he was also an artist, and you know he did LSD and talked about it, and he did his meditation. He almost became a monk, and he was a Zen Buddhist. So he also tried to solve everything in life, all the big questions. And you know I thought it through and kind of what I came back to is—I had a recent tweet on this actually, forget the exact wording of this tweet, but I know the principles, which is what's important. The principles are: first you have to stay healthy. You're born healthy, you don't finish healthy, and so the longer you can drag that out the better, right? So that's why we're inspired by people like Bryan Johnson, or you know whoever your fitness guru is or health guru is. You got to stay healthy. That's the baseline. Then you want to get wealthy.

如果你说你不想变得富有，你是在撒谎。每个人至少都想要某种程度的财富，对吧？

You'd be lying if you said you don't want to be wealthy. Everyone wants at least some level of wealth, right?

埃里克：也许不是。

Eric: Maybe not.

纳瓦尔：好吧。对，如果你在美德标榜社会主义，也许你不想要它。或者如果你，你知道，不想高出同伴太多，你不想要它。但你想要某种程度的舒适和财富。

Naval: Okay. Yeah, if you're virtue-signaling socialism, maybe you don't want it. Or if you, you know, don't want to be too high above your peers, you don't want it. But you want some level of comfort and wealth.

埃里克：对。你不必为钱操心。没有人想要更少的财富。他们不想往回走。

Eric: Yeah. You don't have to worry about money. Nobody wants less wealth. They don't want to go backward.

纳瓦尔：没错。而且你知道，那句老话是什么？你知道，钱解决不了你所有的问题，但它能解决你所有的钱的问题。所以你想解决你所有的钱的问题，然后剩下的是什么，对吧？每次我在这件事上绕圈子，我都回到我感兴趣的只有三件事，那就是——这听起来会像陈词滥调，但我会深入谈它们——是真理、爱和美。而真理就是：我想知道所有为真的事物，我想消除所有为假的事物，我想在精神层面、哲学层面、科学层面、技术层面、人性层面上知道所有为真的事物。真理是永久的。真理是，你知道，最接近完美的东西。真理有宽广的覆盖。真理会让我的生活更好。即便它让我的生活更糟，我也想知道真理。它在那方面非常独特。真理和爱是两样即便让你的生活更糟、你仍然会接受的东西。就像处于——你知道，爱你的孩子，即便它让你的生活更糟，但让他们过得更好，你想爱你的孩子，因为恋爱的感觉很好。它感觉充实。而真理，即便真理让你的生活更糟，就像如果原来我们全都是缸中的黑客帝国大脑，你知道，你仍然想知道那个。就像，事实上，在电影《黑客帝国》里，你知道，基努·里维斯的角色尼奥，他脱离了矩阵。而锡安比矩阵更糟。那是一种糟糕的生存，一座真实的城市。还有另一个家伙，我忘了名字。

Naval: That's right. And you know, what's the old line? You know, money doesn't solve all your problems, but it solves all your money problems. So you want to solve all your money problems, and then what remains, right? Well every time I go around in circles on it I come back to just three things that I'm interested in, and that is—and it's going to sound clichéd but I'll dive into them—is truth, love and beauty. And truth is just I want to know all the things that are true and I want to eliminate all the things that are false, and I want to know all the things that are true on a spiritual level, on a philosophical level, on a scientific level, on a technological level, on a human-nature level. Truth is permanent. Truth is, you know, the closest thing to perfect. Truth has wide reach. Truth will make my life better. Even if it makes my life worse, I want to know the truth. It's very unique in that way. Truth and love are two things where even if it makes your life worse, you would still take them. Like being in—you know, loving your kids, even if it makes your life worse, but makes them better off, you want to love your kids 'cause it feels good to be in love. It feels fulfilling. And truth, even if the truth makes your life worse, like if it turns out that, yeah, we're all Matrix brains in a vat, you know, you still want to know that. It's like, in fact, in the movie The Matrix, you know, Keanu Reeves's character, Neo, he breaks out of the Matrix. And Zion is worse than the Matrix. It's a shitty existence, a real city. And there's another guy, I forget the name.

埃里克：你确实说过，你越接近真理，就越接近平和。

Eric: You did say that the closer you get to truth, the closer you get to peace.

纳瓦尔：对。我觉得我的说法是：你越接近真理，你内在就会越安静。所以，而这是有意选择的。因为当你知道什么为真，你受苦更少，你花更少时间反刍虚假。而穿过你心里的大多数念头都关乎虚假。佛教徒会争辩说，终极的虚假是自我这个概念，它是这种定义得非常差的、部分身体、部分心智的建构，不知怎的跨越念头持续存在。而你把越多事情判定为真或假，你应当有的内心嘈杂就越少，一直到那样的地步：超级智慧的开悟之人内在完全安静，因为他们看穿了自我的幻觉，他们没有分离的自我，没有分离的、持续的自我体验。所以如果你的心嘈杂得更少，那是一个你正朝正确方向走的信号。话虽如此，以我的经验，你的心其实嘈杂得并不会少很多。但但愿焦虑会更少。而这发生在冥想中。如果你在做冥想，你会发现，至少我发现前二十分钟你的心会到处发狂。然后在某个时刻它平静下来，而你——大约进入三十分钟、四十分钟、五十分钟——它就只是空白，而那实际上是一种非常幸福的状态。或者它可以因你如何冥想而不同，但它肯定不会嘈杂得那么多。而这很大程度上是因为你在化解问题，而你的很多问题并不需要现实世界的解决。它们只需要被承认。

Naval: Yeah. I think the way I said it is the closer you get to truth, the more silent you become inside. So, and that's deliberately chosen. Because when you know what is true, you suffer less and you spend less time ruminating about falsehoods. And most of the thoughts that are going through your mind are concerned with falsehoods. And the Buddhists would argue that the ultimate falsehood is a notion of the self, which is this very poorly defined partial-physical, partial-mental construct that somehow persists across thoughts. And just the more things you resolve as being true or false, the less mental chatter you should have, all the way to the point that like the super wise enlightened people are completely silent inside because they've seen through the illusion of self and they have no separate self, no separate persistent self experience. So if your mind is chattering less, that is a sign that you're heading in the right direction. That said, in my experience, your mind doesn't really chatter much less. But hopefully there's less anxiety. And this happens in meditation. If you're doing meditation, you'll find that at least I find the first 20 minutes your mind goes berserk all over the place. And then at some point it calms down and you're—for about 30, 40, 50 minutes in—it's just blank and it's actually a very blissful state. Or it can be different depending on how you meditate, but it's certainly not going to be chattering as much. And a lot of that's 'cause you're resolving problems, and a lot of your problems don't need real-world resolutions. They just need to be acknowledged.

一种经典的练习是：当你有一种情绪，当你有一种强烈的感受，你只是注意它，你拆解它，而你会像：好，身体里关联着一点点感觉。那种身体感觉实际上令人不快吗？并不真的。它只是像胸口的一种空洞，比如说，感觉没那么糟。并且有一个念头与它关联。那么，那个念头令人不快吗？念头本身并不能感觉疼痛。它只是一个——你知道，那么是谁把它归类为不快的？是你。那么那种情况下的「你」是什么？它只是另一个念头。所以它是，你知道，你的一个念头在说，我不想让这另一个念头在这里，然后与一种身体感觉相关联。但真的，当你拆解它时，你会意识到：除非有真正的紧急情况，那里并没有多少东西。而当你擅长做这件事，或当你开始看出那场游戏时，它会帮助你度过令人不快的处境。所以，几年前我有过一次非常不快的处境，那时我极其不幸福。但我不会忘记这个。

One of the classic practices is when you have an emotion, when you have a strong feeling, you just pay attention to it and you deconstruct it and you're like, okay, there's a little feeling associated within the body. Is that bodily sensation actually unpleasant? Not really. It's just like a hollowness in the chest, for example, and doesn't feel that bad. And there's a thought associated with it. Well, is that thought unpleasant? Well, the thought itself can't feel pain. It's just a—you know, so who's classifying it as unpleasant? Well, you are. Well, what is you in that case? It's just another thought. So it's, you know, one thought of yours saying, I don't want this other thought here, and then associated with a bodily sensation. But really, when you deconstruct it, you'll realize like unless there's an actual emergency, there's not a whole lot there. And when you get good at doing that or when you sort of start seeing that game, it will help you get through unpleasant situations. So, I had one very unpleasant situation a few years ago, where I was extremely unhappy. But I won't forget this.

当时也有一部分的我只是在看着那个非常不幸福的部分，只是像：哈哈，你知道，这是你为了感觉自己重要而演的一小出戏，但这里实际上什么都没有。就像——没有什么正在被攻击。没有什么正在受伤。你并不处在身体疼痛中。你知道，它只是一个你正在想过去的处境，而你在召唤出这种深深的不幸福，你在制造一出戏剧。你在制造一个舞台和一出戏剧，好感觉自己重要。而我觉得很多时候当我们不幸福，我们只是迷恋于这种感觉自己重要的想法。而这又回到对永恒的欲望。我们想感觉自己重要。我们想感觉永恒。我们不想感觉到自己的必死。

There was also a part of me that was just watching that very unhappy part and just being like, haha, you know, it's a little act you're putting on to feel important, but there's nothing actually here. There's like—nothing is being attacked. Nothing is being hurt. You're not in physical pain. You know, it's just a situation that you're thinking through, and you're conjuring up this deep unhappiness and you're creating a drama. You're creating a stage and a drama to feel important. And I think a lot of times when we're unhappy, we're just obsessed with this idea of feeling important. And it's back to this desire for permanence. We want to feel important. We want to feel permanence. We don't want to feel our own mortality.

埃里克：有真理，还有爱和美。

Eric: There's truth and there's love and beauty.

纳瓦尔：对。所以那是另外两样。哦对。而关于爱，我会说，你知道，一旦你的健康被照顾好了，一旦你的物质需求被照顾好了，你追求真理、爱和美。我们谈过真理。爱，我觉得是重要的，因为人们想被爱，因为那帮助他们克服自己的必死。它让他们感觉安全一点点。你知道，猴子们在黑暗中围着营火挤在一起，害怕树林外面有什么。但我为自己意识到的一件事是：处于爱中，比被爱更好。如果有人太爱你，比如你妈妈一直过来抱你，或者你知道，某个女孩或某个男孩对你着迷，那会有点腻，对吧？感觉像是一种负担。你几乎不想要那个。但当你感觉爱上某个人，那才是你高潮的时候。那才是你兴高采烈的时候。所以那违反直觉，但爱上某人或某物实际上对你非常有益。它确实包含牺牲。它包含风险。但我觉得那些在生活中放弃爱的人，你知道，那是一种有点悲哀的生活，对吧？你变得厌倦得太快。你不必一定爱人。你可以爱，你知道，宇宙、上帝、动物，诸如此类。但每个人都需要在生活中找到某种他们爱得超过自己的东西。他们的使命，他们的家庭，他们的孩子，他们的宗教，否则那会是一种悲惨的生活。

Naval: Right. So those are the other two things. Oh yeah. And on love, I would say that, you know, once your health is taken care of, once your material needs are taken care of, you pursue truth, love, and beauty. We talked about truth. Love, I think, is important because people want to be loved because that helps them get over their mortality. It makes them feel a little safer. You know, monkeys huddling in the dark around the campfire, scared of what's out there in the woods. But one thing I've realized for myself is that it's better to be in love than to be loved. If somebody loves you too much, like your mom's coming up and hugging you all the time, or you know, some girl or some guy is obsessed with you, like it can get a little cloying, right? It feels like it's a burden. You almost don't want that. But when you feel in love with somebody, that's when you're high. That's when you're elated. And so that's counterintuitive, but falling in love with someone or something is actually very beneficial to you. It does involve sacrifice. It involves risk. But I think that people who give up on love in their lives, you know, it's kind of a sad life, right? You get too jaded too fast. You don't have to love people necessarily. You can love, you know, the universe, God, animals, what have you. But everybody needs to find something in their life that they love more than themselves. Their mission, their family, their children, their religion, otherwise it's going to be a miserable life.

然后最后，你知道，我们是主动的生物。所以这就是我真正与所有佛陀、所有禅僧等等分道扬镳的地方。对，砍柴挑水，但那不是人类的繁盛，对吧？这就是多伊奇真正派上用场的地方。我们是普遍的解释者。我们注定要解决问题。我们注定要逆转熵。我们注定要在宇宙中推进文明和知识。所以我们能够创造。创造力是我们最高的艺术形式。所以我们应当在创造。而你可以在死亡面前被动，说：好吧，这一切都不重要。或者你可以主动，说什么都不重要。我只是要创造出最好的东西。我要按你自己的定义过最好的生活。所以我想创造东西。而你想创造什么？那么，你怎么知道某物值得创造？那么，它是美的。它难以改动。它里面嵌着深层的解释和知识。人们想使用美的东西。所以，你想创造传达情感、人们想使用的美的东西。而对我来说，这就是为什么像 iPhone 这样的东西属于有史以来最伟大的艺术品之列，因为它是一件美的物件。它像一件珠宝，但它也极其有用。它体现了大量知识。它非常难以改动。而且它确实把世界变得更好了。

And then lastly, you know, we are active creatures. So this is where I really diverge from all the Buddhas and all the Zen monks and so on. Yeah, chop wood, carry water, but that's not human flourishing, right? This is where Deutsch comes in really helpful. We're universal explainers. We're meant to solve problems. We're meant to reverse entropy. We're meant to advance civilization in the universe and knowledge. And so we can create. Creativity is our highest art form. So we should be creating. And you can be passive in the face of death and say, well, none of it matters. Or you can be active and say nothing matters. I'm just going to create the best thing. I'm going to live the best life by your own definition. So I want to create things. And what do you want to create? Well, how do you know something is worth creating? Well, it's beautiful. It's hard to vary. It has deep explanations and knowledge embedded inside of it. People want to use beautiful things. So, you want to create beautiful things that convey emotion that people want to use. And to me, that's why something like an iPhone is among the greatest works of art ever created, because it is a beautiful object. It's like a piece of jewelry, but it's also incredibly useful. It embodies a lot of knowledge. It's very hard to vary. And it did change the world for the better.

埃里克：对。那条推文原文是：「保持健康，获得财富，寻求真理，给予爱，并创造美。」

Eric: Yeah. The exact tweet was, "Stay healthy, get wealthy, seek truth, give love, and create beauty."

纳瓦尔：就是这样。那就是我想做的。

Naval: That's it. That's what I want to do.

埃里克：我想纹在眼皮上的那一条是：任何一个你过得不好、你并不真正幸福的时刻，你并没有帮到任何人。并不是你的不幸福让任何别人过得更好。而我不知道为什么。我不知道它是在哪里被示范给我的，或者你知道是不是只是我想认真对待自己，但就像培养不幸福会给一种情境带来某种严肃，尤其是作为领导者，或者你知道试图认真对待某件事时，你会多少带上一个不幸福版本的自己，我发现。而且我知道那是一个错误。我事后应当发这条推——把这个记下来。推文休息。

Eric: The one I want to tattoo on my eyelids is: any moment where you're not having a great time, when you're not really happy, you're not doing anyone any favors. It's not like your unhappiness is making anyone else better off. And I don't know why. I don't know where it was modeled to me, or you know if it's just me trying to take myself seriously, but like there's something about cultivating unhappiness that brings like seriousness to a situation, like especially as a leader, or you know trying to take something seriously, you sort of bring an unhappy version of yourself, I find. 纳瓦尔：对。推文休息。急性的不幸福是真实的，或者说是有用的，但慢性的不幸福是一场自我之旅，对吧？因为慢性的不幸福是你只是想更多地感觉到你自己。你想感觉分离。你想感觉重要。你知道，你有一个身份缠在里面。就像你的身份越薄，你就越能看见实在，因为身份常常站在你和按实在本来样子看见它之间。它是动机性推理的极致。当某件事与你的身份矛盾时，你会与它战斗。你会用你的命去与它战斗。你知道，如果你是白人而我说白人糟透了，就像那是你的身份，对吧？现在，你会与那个战斗——或者说黑人糟透了，无论什么，对吧？所以那就是种族主义的问题。就像，人们无法改变他们的身份。所以，当你是种族主义者时，你立刻把他们变成敌人。而它制造战争。所以，你必须让你的身份保持很薄。你不想选择你的身份，对吧？身份就像你无法摆脱的东西。

Naval: Yeah. Tweet break. Acute unhappiness is real, or is useful, but chronic unhappiness is an ego trip, right? Because chronic unhappiness is you just want to feel more you. You want to feel separate. You want to feel important. You know, you have an identity wrapped up in it. Like the thinner your identity, the more you can see reality, because identity is what often stands between you and seeing reality the way it is. It's the ultimate in motivated reasoning. When something contradicts your identity, you're going to fight it. You're going to fight it with your life. You know, if you're white and I say white people suck, like that's your identity, right? Now, you're going to fight that—or black people suck, whatever, right? So that's the problem with racism. Like, people can't change their identity. And so, when you're racist, you make enemies out of them immediately. And it creates warfare. So, you got to keep your identity thin. You don't want to choose your identity, right? Identity is like what you can't get away from.

但任何被选择的身份都是一件约束衣。它把你锁在里面。这对幸福成立，对真理也成立。而在这里我会说，像那些不幸福的人——就像曾经有一段时间它对他们有用。我想我前面提过他，威廉·格拉瑟，他算是我最喜欢的心理学家类型的作者，他围绕他称之为选择理论的东西写了一系列非常好的书。但他最有争议的观察之一是：抑郁其实是一种选择，或者它是一系列已经做出的选择。它是你还是孩子时做出的一系列选择的结果，那些选择后来变成一种无意识的习惯，并且它自我延续。所以当你年轻时，你知道，作为婴儿，你一开始哭是为了得到父母的注意，而每次你哭，他们就过来回应，然后最终你发现其实对陌生人哭并不管用。它只会把他们推开。所以你开始微笑，或者你开始交流，但最终你会遇到这样的情形：你试图得到某人的注意，无论是你的父母、老师还是同伴，而他们不给你注意。所以你在内部哭。你发出信号：你如此不幸福，以至于你愿意伤害自己。而这就是抑郁。它是通过你自己的不幸福来伤害自己，而你开始反刍它。而它在一小段时间里也许有效。它把一些人拉得更近，并且它会导致这样一种情形：你现在无意识地或潜意识地最终抑郁了，没有意识到这是一种学来的行为。

But any chosen identity is a straitjacket. It's locking you in. And that's true for happiness, but it's true for truth. And here I would say like people who are unhappy—like there was a time when it served them. I think I mentioned him earlier, William Glasser, who was kind of my favorite psychologist-type writer, and he wrote a series of really good books around something he calls choice theory. But one of his most controversial observations is that depression is really a choice, or it's a series of choices that was made. It's a result of a series of choices that were made when you were a child that then became an unconscious habit and it's self-perpetuating. So when you were young, you know, as a baby, you start out you're crying to get your parents' attention and every time you cry, they come and they respond, and then eventually you figure out actually with strangers crying doesn't work. It just forces them away. So you start smiling or you start communicating, but eventually you run into situations where you're trying to get someone's attention, whether it's your parents or your teachers or your peers, and they won't give you the attention. So you cry internally. You signal that you are so unhappy that you're willing to hurt yourself. And that's what depression is. It's hurting yourself through your own unhappiness and you start ruminating on it. And it may be effective for a little period of time. It pulls some people closer together and it can lead to a situation where you are now unconsciously or subconsciously ending up depressed, not realizing that this is a learned behavior.

现在我不是说那里不可能有化学成分。显然总是有的。饥饿有化学成分。运动能力有化学成分，智力也有，你知道，遗传成分，但你能把它弄得更糟。而说这全是化学失衡并不能解释任何东西。那就好像说我们为什么在这里？好吧，那是因为大爆炸的粒子碰撞，对吧？它还原得太过头了。所以，解释必须在与问题相同的层面上被给出。所以，当我们说，比如，我为什么不幸福？忘掉化学失衡吧。你将会改变你的大脑，对吧？而吃药只会在其他方面把你搞砸。每个长期在这些事情上吃药的人，他们最终都会放弃自己身上某种别的根本的东西。而且嘿，很多最优秀的艺术家在别的方面是疯的，对吧？所以你不想把你的心态平均掉。所以抑郁也许是一系列坏选择的结果，那些选择后来变成坏习惯，而它们确实会创造出一个自我，因为它们创造出一个分离的「我」的身份，然后你开始如此强烈地认同那个「我」。就像，好吧，我还能是谁呢？好吧，我只是一个不幸福的人，但那也是使我杰出、使我特别、使我有趣的东西。它使我未被发现，诸如此类。所以你可以把整个身份缠在上面。而要解除那个身份很难，因为然后你会像：好吧，是什么使我独特，但这值得去做。顺便说，这就像如果你读杰德·麦肯纳，对吧，精神自溶，他在做什么？他在过一遍他的整个身份并把它剥掉。我觉得他把那有多痛苦戏剧化得过头了。但那是一种有点意思的方法。

Now I'm not saying that there couldn't be a chemical component there. There obviously always is. There are chemical components to hunger. There are chemical components to athleticism, to intelligence, you know, genetic components, but you can make it worse. And saying that it's all chemical imbalances doesn't explain anything. That's like saying like why are we here? Well, it's because of particle collisions from the Big Bang, right? It's too reductive. So, the explanation has to be offered at the same level as the question. So, when we say like, why am I unhappy? Forget the chemical imbalance. You're going to change your brain, right? And doing drugs will just mess you up in other ways. Every person who takes like these long-term drugs on these things, they end up giving up something else fundamental about themselves. And hey, a lot of the best artists were crazy in other ways, right? So you don't want to average out your mindset. So the depression may be the result of a series of bad choices that then became bad habits, and they do create an ego 'cause they create a separate identity of me, and then you start identifying with that me so much. It's like, well who else would I be? Well, I'm just an unhappy person, but that's also what makes me brilliant, makes me special and makes me interesting. It makes me undiscovered and all that. So you can wrap a whole identity around it. And it's hard to undo that identity because then you're like, well, what makes me unique, but it's worth doing. By the way, this is like if you read Jed McKenna, right, spiritual autolysis, what is he doing? He's going through his whole identity and stripping it away. I think he over-dramatizes how painful that was. But it's kind of an interesting method.

Naval: That's right. The most important thing with getting wealthy and being happy is just realize you can do it. It's your choice. You can do both and you can be both and there's nothing wrong with trying to do both.

埃里克：这整一节开头的推文是：人生中三件大事是财富、健康和幸福。我们按那个顺序追求它们，但它们的重要性是反过来的。而我好奇这个顺序是否应当反过来追求，或者你觉得它们全都是——就像最优路径是多少找到平衡，并行地追求它们全部。

Eric: The opening tweet of this whole section is the three big ones in life are wealth, health and happiness. We pursue them in that order but their importance is reversed. And I was curious whether the order should be pursued backwards, or you think they're all—like the optimal path is to sort of find balance and pursue them all in parallel.

纳瓦尔：我对顺序感觉并不强烈。我对那些组成部分感觉更强烈，对吧？你可以按不同的顺序追求它们。就像有些人就是生来幸福，天生幸福，没有什么能摧毁它。他们就是永远幸福，而那是最伟大的礼物，对吧？所以这就是为什么我觉得它是最重要的。就像甚至有些人会不健康，而他们仍然幸福。他们确实存在，你知道他们是有福的。所以如果你能得到那一样，你基本上就定了。因为其他的只是通往那两样东西的尝试路径，或那两样东西的前提。但如果你不需要另外两样作为前提，如果你是这些神奇的人之一，那就务必立刻去获得幸福，并且幸福。就财富和健康而言，对，我觉得健康比财富更重要。去问任何一个年老而不健康的人，他们会用很多钱跟你交换，好把健康换回来。或者那句孔子的老话，我以前引用过，但你知道，健康的人想要一万件事。生病的人只想要一件事。而这非常真实。当你生病时，你会，你知道，你只想再站起来。所以健康非常重要，然后财富，你知道，它解决你所有的物质问题，也真的很重要。老实说我不会放弃这三样中的任何一样。话虽如此，如果你只能选一样，你知道，幸福会是第一，但你不应当必须只选一样。你有一张凳子的三条腿。大多数人我觉得会想要全部三样。而财富最好在你更年轻时追求，只因为你精力更高。你更灵活。你义务更少。你在住哪里、做什么之类的事情上更可塑。你就是更乐观。你可以以后再追求它。只是更难。你知道那首老歌怎么唱的？就像凡事都有定期。

Naval: I don't feel strongly about the order. I feel more strongly about the components, right? You could pursue them in different orders. Like some people are just born happy, naturally happy, nothing destroys it. They're just always happy and that's the greatest gift, right? So that's why I think it is the most important. Like even there's some people who will be unhealthy and they'll still be happy. They do exist and you know they're blessed. So if you can get that one, you're pretty much set. Because the others are just attempted routes towards those two things or prerequisites for those two things. In terms of the wealth and health, yeah, I think the health is more important than the wealth. Ask any old unhealthy guy and they'll trade you a lot of money for getting their health back. Or it's the old Confucius line, which I quoted before, but you know, a healthy man wants 10,000 things. A sick man only wants one thing. And it's very true. When you're sick, you'll, you know, you just want to get back up. So health is very important and then wealth, you know, it solves all your material problems, is also really important. You know what does that old song go? Like for everything there is a season.

而且你知道，如果你十多岁，你应当在学习和做事。如果你二十多岁，你大概想非常努力地工作，并且可以说是在竞技场里。三十多岁，你想开始收获。那是你有人脉、并且仍然有很多精力的时候。四十多岁，你绝对想在收获。而这里是你真正想照看健康的地方。而到现在你最好开始变得幸福，因为剩下的时间不够了。然后当你五十多岁，你可以做你的政治回馈，以及你的，你知道，对社会的贡献，去慈善、去捐助，而当你六十多岁，你可以做一个哲学家，因为足够多的经历已经发生，你已经把足够多的事情想通了。所以所有这些事情都有一种自然的时节。我想回到罗马时代——我读的罗马史不多，但我的感觉是，在从前，你十多岁时会学习。你二十多岁时会去打仗。你三十多岁时会做生意。你四十多岁时会从政。五十多岁，你做你的哲学。对吧？大致是那样。也许每个桶里前后差几年。所以凡事都有定期。所以，你想先获得财富。年轻时健康被当成理所当然，但愿如此。然后你想在变老时保住健康。然后到了某个时刻，你最好弄清楚怎样变得幸福，因为你不想成为那个脾气越来越坏、对世界越来越愤怒、越来越苦涩、越来越犬儒的老人。我们都在很老的人身上见过那个。我在老人身上只看到两个极端。他们要么是对一切都没问题的那些人。你知道，他们已经与世界和解，以及那些对一切都苦涩的人，对吧？因为无论我们处在哪个方向，随着变老我们都会变得更像我们自己。C. S. 路易斯谈过这个，你知道，对他来说道德是——你知道，而自由意志意味着你做的每一个小小行动，它们会复利，而你是这些复利行动三十年、五十年、一百年的产物，你应当对每一个小小行动负责，因为它把你带得更接近天堂或更接近地狱，而如果你只是不断做出小的坏决定，它们导向中等的坏决定，导向大的坏决定，就很难回来，而你最终会有一个失败的人生。所以对，我觉得如果你遵循时节哲学，凡事都有定期，你从财富开始，你保住健康，然后你变得幸福。但重要性多少是反过来的，那些天生就幸福的人，他们有最伟大的礼物。他们不需要其他的。而那些没有健康的人并不真的在乎他们的财富。所以这就是为什么我多少坚持这个排序，但我对此感觉并不特别强烈。

And you know, if you're in your teens, you should be learning and doing. If you're in your 20s, you probably want to be working very hard and in the arena, so to speak. In your 30s, you want to start harvesting. That's when you have your contacts and you still have a lot of energy. In your 40s, you definitely want to be harvesting. And this is where you really want to be watching your health. And you better be getting happy by now 'cause there's not enough time left. And then when you're in your 50s and you can do your political give-backs and your, you know, contribution to society and be philanthropic and charitable, and when you're in your 60s you can be a philosopher because enough experiences have happened, you've thought through enough things. So there is kind of a natural season to all these things. I think back in the Roman days—I don't read much about the history of Rome, but my sense is in olden times when you were in your teens you would study. When you were in your 20s is when you would go to war. When you were in your 30s, you would do your business. When you're in your 40s, you would serve in politics. In your 50s, you'd do your philosophy. Right? Something along those lines. Maybe a few years here and there in each bucket. So there is a season for everything. So, you want to get wealth first. Health is taken for granted when you're young, hopefully. Then you want to preserve your health as you get older. And then you better figure out how to get happy when you're, you know, at a certain point, 'cause you don't want to be that grumpy old person who's just getting angrier and more bitter and more cynical at the world as you get older. We've all seen that in very old people. There's only two extremes that I see in old people. They're either the ones who are fine with everything. You know, they've made their peace with the world, and the ones who are bitter about everything, right? Because we become more of ourselves in whatever direction we're in as we get older. C. S. Lewis talks about this where you know to him morality is—you know, and free will mean that every little action you make, they compound, and you are the product of 30, 50, 100 years of these compounding actions, and you should take responsibility for each little action because it leads you closer to heaven or closer to hell, and if you just keep making small bad decisions they lead to medium-sized bad decisions lead to large bad decisions, it's very hard to come back and you end up having a failed life. So yeah, I think if you follow the seasons philosophy, for everything there's a season, you start with wealth, you preserve your health and then you get happy. But the importance kind of is the reverse, where the people who are just naturally happy, they have the greatest gift. They don't need the others. And the people who don't have their health don't really care about their wealth. So that's why I kind of stand by the ordering, but I don't feel super strongly about it.

埃里克：对。很多人落入的一种失败模式是：他们花太多时间追求财富。他们牺牲幸福。他们牺牲健康，然后发现得太晚，他们已经远远射过了，你知道，他们的财富标杆。他们赚到的钱远比他们需要的多，或者他们只是变成——身份被供奉得太牢固，他们无法改变。

纳瓦尔：而且你知道，对，我觉得健康那部分每个人变老时都会弄明白。没有人想变得更不健康。每个人都想更健康，因为不健康的反馈回路非常紧。疼痛要求回应，而疼痛最终会出现。所以每个人都想更健康，所以我觉得人们会弄明白那个，尤其是一旦他们变富了，然后你知道他们对自己的时间有更多控制，他们可以把时间投入健康。而这些日子坦白说，你知道你有了 GLP-1，所以人们可以减肥，而肥胖大概是不健康最大的大众市场驱动因素，所以那个正在被解决。但幸福那一项很难。很多人甚至不相信它是可能的。我会说我大多数赚了很多钱的同代人，他们不是幸福的人，甚至不只是不幸福。他们没有一种想象：他们可以按自己的条件建造自己的生活。他们过着非常结构化的生活，模仿他们年轻时拥有的东西，而且不一定是以一种好的方式。我会说他们穿着约束衣，就像，你知道，孩子在上学所以我不能旅行。你知道，我没有雇帮手，因为，你知道，我们应该自己洗碗。这些就像是这样行事的亿万富翁，对吧？或者他们就是不跳出盒子思考，对吧？他们去那些强制性的会议、活动、节日或仪式，那些只是乏味的。你知道，就像和乏味的人开乏味的会，对吧？所以我只是发现他们太多人不能像黑进他们的事业或黑进他们的生意那样黑进他们的生活。我是说以一种好的方式黑，对吧？

Naval: And you know, yeah, I feel like the health part everybody figures out as they get older. Nobody wants to be less healthy. 埃里克：但是能动性。

纳瓦尔：我有一个了不起的生活。如果人们知道我的生活有多好，我会被赶出城。我的生活真的很好。我是说，基本上，一天中任何给定的时刻，我都在做我想做的事。它被好好想过。它是经过考虑的。它不是强制的。它被设计成这样：它确实与我的家人和我在乎的人保持非常好的、真实的关系。但我没有做任何我不想做的事。而且我享受我想做的事。而如果我不享受它，我非常、非常快地改变它。而且是的，这不是按任何人的规则生活。就像一切都是为我建造的。
