And I'm enjoying what I want to do. And if I'm not enjoying it, I'm changing it very, very quickly. And yes, it is not living by anybody's rules. Like everything is built for me.

埃里克：你称自己懒，但我觉得它实际上大概是一种对付出与产出之比的关注。

Eric: You've called yourself lazy, but I think it's actually probably a focus on the effort-to-output ratio.

纳瓦尔：对。我想要一切中的二八。我实际上想要——我想要那个九五、九九的，分形的二八。

Naval: Yeah. I want the 80/20 in everything. I want to actually—I want the 95, 99 one, the fractal 80/20.

埃里克：对，正是。

Eric: Yeah, exactly.

纳瓦尔：我想在做得最少的同时得到我能得到的最多，而且是在我生活的每个方面，然后你知道再开辟更多事业。而实际上即便在哲学家当中，就像我爱叔本华，因为他如此以真理为导向，但他看起来并不像在享受他的生活，对吧？而像奥修这样的人，即便他有点疯狂，我也不同意他说的一切，所以至少他在享受他的生活。奥修客观上在享受他的生活。那些纵欲狂欢和 17 辆劳斯莱斯，或纪录片里无论是什么，你知道，我不同意那些里的任何一点，但他绝对过得很愉快。而对我来说，听一个奥修或史蒂夫·乔布斯，比听某个非常成功、甚至开悟、但看起来只是阴郁或低落或痛苦的人，更有意思。

Naval: I want to get the most that I can while doing the least, but in every aspect of my life, and then you know open up more business. And actually even among the philosophers, like I love Schopenhauer because he's so truth-oriented, but he did not look like he was enjoying his life, right? Where someone like an Osho, even though he's a bit of a whack job and I don't agree with everything he says, so at least he was enjoying his life. Osho was objectively enjoying his life. The orgies and 17 Rolls-Royces or whatever is in the documentary, you know, I don't agree with any of that, but he was definitely having a good time. And it's more interesting to me to listen to an Osho or Steve Jobs rather than somebody who was, you know, very successful or even enlightened but just seems dour or down or miserable.

埃里克：对。好，我觉得幸福那部分工作、幸福那些章节、幸福那些想法的承重支柱之一，是对感知的能动性。或者说感知——对感知的能动性，去理解就像——我是说你的推文：没有影响你情绪的外部力量。就像无论感觉起来怎样，你都能控制你如何解释正在发生的事情。

Eric: Yeah. Well, I think one of the sort of load-bearing pillars of the happiness, you know, work, the happiness chapters, the happiness ideas, is agency over perception. Or perception—agency over perception, of understanding that like there—I mean your tweet: there are no external forces affecting your emotions. Like no matter what it feels like you can control how you interpret the things that are happening.

纳瓦尔：对。改变你对过去的解释。你并不存储你过去的记忆。你存储的是对过去记忆的解释。所以改变那些解释，或重新审视那些解释，可能痛苦，但有用。

Naval: Yeah. Changing your interpretation of the past. You don't store memories of your past. You store interpretations of memories of your past. And so changing those interpretations or revisiting those interpretations can be painful but useful.

它需要某种程度的谦卑。你知道自我是挡在路上的那个东西。自我其实并不存在。它只是一个概念，但自我就像是：你不想经历那些重新评估你以为自己是谁、你以为是什么使你重要的痛苦念头。所以，你不愿意重新审视你的过去。就像，假设出于无论什么理由，你知道，你和父母有过冲突，对吧？于是，现在你有一个身份，一种对过去的解释：你的父母是坏人，你和他们合不来，因此你处在你现在的处境里。那是他们的错。好吧，那大概不是真的。它大概让你远离父母。它大概让你的生活痛苦。它大概也让他们痛苦。然后当你有了孩子，你的孩子无法接触他们的祖父母。而现实是，如果你只是回去，试图非常客观，你知道，中立，并且把怀疑往对他们有利的方向想，并意识到他们在基因上和你非常相似。他们只是有不同的境遇，他们做出了他们不得不做的艰难选择，也许那并不完全是他们的错，也许你可以对此更宽容。而如果你重新解释那个，那你就能和他们过一种客观上更好的生活。但这会要求你承受自我上的那一击：不，其实——而你也并非没错。所以那意味着你生活中出问题的某些事情是你自己的坏选择。现在看见那个真理也许能让你做出更好的选择，但它确实意味着你要承受自我上的一击。你必须有痛苦的念头。

It requires a certain level of humility. You know the ego is a thing that stands in the way. The ego doesn't really exist. It's just a concept, but the ego is just like you don't want to go through the painful thoughts of re-evaluating who you think you are and what you think makes you important. So, you're not willing to reexamine your past. Like, let's say like for whatever reason, you know, you had conflict with your parents, right? And so, now you have an identity, an interpretation of your past where your parents were bad people and you didn't get along with them and therefore you're in the situation that you're in. It's their fault. Well, that's probably not true. It's probably keeping you away from your parents. It's probably making your life miserable. It's probably making them miserable. And then when you have kids, your kids don't have access to their grandparents. And the reality is, if you just went back and tried to be very objective, you know, neutral, and give them the benefit of the doubt and realize they're genetically very similar to you. They just had different circumstances and they made the hard choices they had to make, that perhaps it wasn't entirely their fault and perhaps you can be more charitable about it. And if you reinterpret that, then you could have an objectively better life with them. But it would require you to take the ego hit of, no, actually—and you weren't wrong. So that means some of the things that went wrong in your life were your own bad choices. Now seeing that truth might allow you to make better choices, but it does mean that you take an ego hit. You have to have painful thoughts.

而这就是冥想或理解自我的本性这类事情有帮助的地方，因为你明白那些念头不是你。会有一堆愉快的念头，会有一堆不愉快的念头，然后又会有一堆愉快的念头。这些只是念头。念头实际上没有感觉。念头不会受伤。念头什么也不做。念头不意味着任何东西。而稍稍与它们断开，好对它保持客观，是有帮助的。所以那是冥想过程的一部分。顺便说，它也发生在迷幻剂里：人们会重新解释他们的过去，他们出来时更幸福却不知道为什么，但真正的原因是因为他们处理了过去的问题，并且重新解释了它们。我觉得我做了一些那样的事。不是作为一种有意识的、刻意的练习，或像是好吧我要过一遍我的过去并重新解释它，而只是当我对此头脑开放，并且我像是我只想知道真理，或者你知道，或者甚至像你在精神上受苦，你只想熬过去，你只想挺过去，你厌倦了它，你受够了它，对吧，你受够了受苦，那么客观地重新评估那些念头会有帮助，或者哪怕只是放下它们，如果它们曾是，你知道，有用的信念。即便它像是你无法放下它是正确还是错误的解释，就像如果为了你的未来而重构它对你有用。

And this is where things like meditation or understanding the nature of the self help, because you get that those thoughts aren't you. There's going to be a bunch of pleasant thoughts and be a bunch of unpleasant thoughts and there'll be a bunch of pleasant thoughts again. These are just thoughts. Thoughts don't actually have feelings. Thoughts don't get hurt. Thoughts don't do anything. Thoughts don't mean anything. And it helps to disconnect from them a little bit to be objective about it. So that's part of the process of meditation. By the way, it also happens in psychedelics where people will reinterpret their past and they'll come out happier not knowing why, but the real reason is because they've worked through problems in the past and they've reinterpreted them. I think I did some of that. Not as a conscious deliberate exercise, or like well I'm going to go through my past and reinterpret it, but it's just that when I was open-minded about it and I was like I just want to know the truth, or you know, or even like you're mentally suffering, you just want to get over it, you just want to get through it, you're sick of it, you're done with it, right, you're done with the suffering, then it helps to objectively re-evaluate those thoughts or even just to let go of them if they were, you know, useful beliefs. Even if it's like you can't let go of it being a correct or incorrect interpretation, like if it's useful to you for your future to reframe it.

那就是宽恕。宽恕是你原谅他们，因为你重新解释了发生的事。你会像：好吧，也许那并不完全是他们的错。但另一种只是像：那是他们的错，但我仍然必须真诚地原谅他们。这很难，但我要去做，这样我才能向前走，这样它就不会占据我的心和我的脑子。而我不会坐在这里喷毒液和血，只是对整件事感到愤怒，只是感觉很糟，因为愤怒让你痛苦。愤怒，你知道，会拉高你的皮质醇，让你的肾上腺运转起来，让你衰老，让你感觉不好。很多恶习和罪，那些传统的那些，它们让你感觉不好。惩罚直接落在你身上。你立刻感觉到它的负担。所以你熬过去不是因为他们，不是为了原谅他们，而是去原谅，而是只为把它从你自己心里弄出去，清理你自己的心。

That's what forgiveness is. Forgiveness is you forgive them because you reinterpret what happened. You're like, okay, maybe it wasn't entirely their fault. But another one is just like it was their fault, but I still have to genuinely forgive them. This is hard, but I'm going to do it so I can move on so it's not occupying my mind and my brain. And I'm not sitting here spitting venom and blood and just feeling angry about the whole thing, just feeling terrible, because anger makes you miserable. Anger, you know, spikes your cortisol, gets your adrenal glands going, ages you, makes you feel bad. A lot of the vices and sins, the traditional ones, they make you feel bad. The punishment is directly on you. You feel the burden of it immediately. And so you get over it not because of them, not to forgive them, but to forgive, but to just get it out of your own mind, to clear your own mind.

埃里克：对。如果你不能出于无私让自己去原谅，你也许能出于自私去原谅。我喜欢玩的一个思想实验是：哪一个人，如果我们能量每个人类大约一个月或一年的神经化学水平，就像地球上哪一个人正在体验最多的主观幸福，而那个人是谁，他们在哪里，他们怎样生活？他们相信什么？还有你知道他们的习惯是什么？我觉得很可能是，你知道，一个有四口之家或类似的、做志愿者的信教水管工。

Eric: Yeah. If you can't get yourself to forgive out of selflessness, you might be able to out of selfishness. A thought experiment I like to play with is which human being, if we can measure everyone's sort of neurochemical levels over a month or a year, like which human on earth is experiencing the most subjective happiness, and who is that person and where are they and how are they living? What do they believe? And you know what are their habits? I think it's highly likely that it's you know a religious plumber with a family of four or something, volunteers.

纳瓦尔：好，我是说互联网了不起的事情之一是：外面真的有开悟的人，而过去他们是接触不到的。你知道曾经是你去遥远的山顶，遇见佛陀。他们像是一个神话。

Naval: Well, I mean one of the things that's amazing about the internet is that there are actual enlightened people out there, and in the past they were inaccessible. You know there was you go to a remote mountaintop and you meet the Buddha. They were a myth, it seems like.

埃里克：按神话来说，你一生总共会遇见一千个人。你找到当前在世佛陀的几率是多少。

Eric: Mythologically you would encounter a thousand people total in your life. What are the odds you're going to find the current living Buddha.

纳瓦尔：好，现在多亏了互联网，YouTube 上、Twitter 上有几十个这样的人。Air Chat 上有过几个。我遇见的大概接近一打。我和他们互动过。在他们身边待了足够久之后，我按自己的满意程度核实过他们是开悟的。而且，你知道，我向他们学习。所以，这些人存在。这一切都可获得。就像，你可以去找这样的人——他们不是常规多巴胺快感意义上的幸福，但他们时时都处于平和。没有什么打扰他们。就像你知道，被告知他们有——被诊断出癌症，对他们来说会引起的恐惧和不快，会和拿到一张停车罚单一样。就像他们对此很幽默，你知道。所以这些人存在。他们完全可以接近。你甚至可以雇他们中的一些做你的私人教练，或者你可以看他们无限多的 YouTube 视频。我是说我现在就能给你点出十五个。

Naval: Well, now thanks to the internet, there's dozens of them on YouTube, on Twitter. There were a couple on Air Chat. I've met probably close to a dozen of them. I've interacted with them. I verified to my own satisfaction that they are enlightened after spending enough time around them. And, you know, I learned from them. So, these people exist. This is all available. Like, you can go find people who are—they're not happy in a conventional dopamine pleasure sense, but they're at peace at all times. Nothing bothers them. Like you know, being told that they have—getting diagnosed with cancer to them will invite the same level of fear and unhappiness that getting a parking ticket would. Like they're funny with it, you know. And so these people exist. They're completely approachable. You can even hire some of them as your personal coach, or you can watch infinite YouTube videos with them. I mean I can name you 15 of them right now.

纳瓦尔：哦我是说有更知名的，像 YouTube 上的鲁珀特·斯皮拉和穆吉，你知道，他们并不出名。有一个人，斯瓦米·纳尔达南德，我和他待过很长时间。他现在半出名了。他做悉地大瑜伽。他更像是那种经典上师类型。还有我的私人朋友。史蒂文·贝尔彻尔，他和我一起上过 Air Chat。他也在 Twitter 上。詹姆斯·皮尔斯，你知道，他推了很多。Effortless Stoicism，卡皮尔·古普塔，我过去与他共事过。有一个在 YouTube 上的 Ascendor。我最近把他推出去过。还有另一个人在中西部某家餐厅当服务员。我忘了他的名字。我把他推出去过。有 Saja。他们有好多。我是说，我的 YouTube 里全是他们，而我看过他们足够多的视频，多到我知道这些是真货。这些不是骗子。

Naval: Oh I mean there are better-known ones like Rupert Spira and Mooji on YouTube, you know, they're not famous. There's a guy, Swami Nardanand, who I spent a lot of time with. 埃里克：当你处在他们的身体存在中时，差别是可以感知的吗？

Eric: Is it a palpable difference when you're in their physical presence?

纳瓦尔：不，没有那种你会漂在九霄云上的花哨光环。我是说，那很多是夸张，但我觉得你会发现的是：他们有持续的无我体验。他们看穿了自我只是一个念头这个事实。它是一个被建构的身份，那里并没有一个真正会幸福或不幸福的人。所以正因为如此，他们现在基本上作为纯粹的觉知或意识而活。所以他们实质上与他们的环境融在一起。他们感到一种平和，因为你的觉知是你整个生命中从未改变过的那一件事。它是你生命中从生到死完美而永恒的那一件事。

Naval: No, there's no like fancy aura where you're going to be floating on cloud nine. I mean, a lot of that is hyperbole, but I think what you find is that they have the persistent experience of no-self. They see through the fact that the self is just a thought. It's a constructed identity and there's no one there to actually really get happy or unhappy. So because of that they now live basically as pure awareness or consciousness. And so they are essentially melded with their environment. They feel a sense of peace because your awareness is the one thing that's never changed about your entire life. It is the one thing that is perfect and permanent in your life from birth to death.

而他们不再困惑，或把自己想成一个分离的身心建构，所以没有什么打扰他们。那算是共同的一点。而我通常辨认他们的方式是：我只是在他们身边待足够久，以至于我看见某件真正糟糕的事发生在他们身上。你必须待得足够久，好让某件真正糟糕的事发生在他们身上，然后你看他们的反应，甚至没有一丝扰动，而那就是你知道的方式。

And they are no longer confused or think of themselves as a separate body-mind construct, and so nothing bothers them. That's kind of the common thing. And the way I usually figure them out is I just spend enough time around them that I see something genuinely bad happen to them. You have to hang around long enough for something genuinely bad to happen to them, and then you see their reaction and there's not even a perturbation, and that's how you know.

埃里克：有意思。而开悟让它看起来——开悟是一个超级花哨的词。

纳瓦尔：我不确定有这种东西。

纳瓦尔：它是二元的。它是二元的，因为——而这有点违反直觉。它不是一条路径。它不像是——顺便说，萨古鲁是另一个。我遇见过他们一堆。我应当列一份名单。我在 Twitter 上有一个开放的求道者名单，它追踪的不一定是开悟的人——上面有几个那样的，但也有正在朝那个方向努力的人。他们在乎它。他们是真诚的求道者。话虽如此，我不确定有任何所谓进步这种东西，因为要么你相信你是一个分离的自我，要么不信。就这么简单。甚至不是相信。相信是错误的词。你知道，信念不够强。信念并不够。所以，要么你知道你是一个分离的自我，要么你知道你不是。现实是每个人都不可知。你知道，真正的无神论者非常少，真正的宗教人士也非常少。如果你真的信教，你就会像：好吧，一切都在上帝手里，一切都好。而如果你真的是无神论者，你就会像：好吧，这一切都不重要，所以一切都好。对吧？但我们都不可知。

Naval: It is binary. It is binary because—and this is a little counterintuitive. It's not a path. It's not like a—Sadhguru is another one, by the way. I met a bunch of them. I should keep a list. I have a seekers list on Twitter which is open, which tracks not necessarily enlightened people—that has a couple of those on there, but also people who are working towards it. They care about it. They're genuine seekers. That said, I'm not sure there's any such thing as progress because it's either you believe that you are a separate self or not. It's that simple. Not even believe. Believe is the wrong word. You know, belief is not strong enough. Belief will not suffice. So, either you know that you're a separate self or you know that you are not. The reality is everyone's agnostic. You know, there are very few actual atheists and there are very few actual religious people. If you were actually religious, you'd just be like, well, everything's in God's hands, everything's fine. And if you were actually an atheist, you'd be like, well, none of this matters, so everything's fine. Right? But we're all agnostic.

埃里克：这些人有像关系和孩子以及——

Eric: Do these people have like relationships and kids and—

纳瓦尔：对，我——这其实是一个很好的问题，对吧？因为你会想到——首先，开悟这个词很糟糕。它暗示有某种你最终进入的状态，在那里一切完美，你永远幸福。根本不是那样。只是你消失了。而那一切就像——有一个很棒的古老哲学谜题，叫做哲学僵尸问题，而我看见它被提及——不一定是它，但他从像什么是意识之类的开始，对吧？意识是干什么的？正是那个问题。而哲学僵尸问题基本上是说，你知道，假设你必须过完一生，你必须解决问题，你必须对像对环境做出反应，你必须把事情想通——你需要意识干什么？为什么不能只是有一个机器人在做所有那些？你为什么需要拥有这种有意识的觉知？因为一个机器人可以对刺激做出反应，可以解决问题，可以做所有那些事。意识完全不必要。所以问题是：为什么要有意识？而这是一个非常西方的概念。为什么要有意识？而所有开悟的人会告诉你：实际上意识是唯一存在的东西。一切都出现在意识中。它由意识构成，它在意识中消失，它被意识看见。除了意识之外什么都没有。你并不体验外面世界里的任何东西。你只是作为意识体验意识。所以那实际上就是全部。而真正的意识观察一切，包括你的身体和你的心。所以它不是你的身体。它不是你的心。它是它自己的东西，而一切由它构成。所以那就是全部存在，那就是你的全部。所以他们会把答案反过来，说：为什么要有僵尸？你知道你在问为什么要有意识？不。答案是：为什么要有僵尸？没有僵尸。僵尸不存在。没有分离的自我。外面没有别的东西。所以这是一个完全不同的框架。所以在那个意义上，没有开悟。没有一个分离的存在去开悟。只有生活。只有事情在发生。你知道，只有事情在发生。有念头出现。有念头消失。它们彼此连接。它们彼此交谈。它们有效。它们存在。有身体。有心智在走来走去。并且有对这些事物的一种觉知。而你基本上是一种局域化的觉知。你以为你是的东西是局域化的觉知。而这回到那个最古老的问题，我想你知道拉玛那·马哈希常谈的，就是：我是谁？

Naval: Yeah, I—it's actually that's a great question, right? Because you think of—first of all, the word enlightenment is horrible. It implies that there's like some state that you end up in where everything is perfect and you're always happy. It's not the case at all. It's just that you disappear. And all that's like—there's a great old philosophical puzzle called the philosophical zombie problem, and I saw it referred to—not it necessarily, but he starts off with like what are consciousness and all that, right? What is consciousness for? It's exactly that question. And the philosophical zombie problem basically says, you know, let's say you have to go through life, you have to solve problems, you have to like react to the environment, you have to think through things—what do you need consciousness for? Why can't there just be a robot doing all of that? Why do you need to have this conscious awareness? 'Cause a robot could be reacting to stimuli, could be solving problems, could be doing all of those things. Consciousness is completely unnecessary. And so the question is why consciousness? And this is a very Western concept. Why consciousness? And all the enlightened people would tell you actually consciousness is the only thing there is. Everything appears in consciousness. It's made of consciousness and it disappears in consciousness and it's seen by consciousness. There's nothing other than consciousness. You don't experience anything in the outside world. You just experience consciousness as consciousness. So that's all there actually is. And the actual consciousness observes everything including your body and your mind. So it's not your body. It's not your mind. It's its own thing and everything is made of it. So that's all that exists and that's all you are. So they would reverse the answer and say why zombie? You know you're asking why consciousness? No. The answer is why zombie? There's no zombie. The zombie doesn't exist. There is no separate self. There's nothing else out there. And so this is a completely different frame on it. So in that sense, there is no enlightenment. There's no separate being to be enlightened. There's just life. There's just stuff happening. You know, there's just stuff happening. There are thoughts appearing. There are thoughts disappearing. They connect to each other. They talk to each other. They're valid. They exist. There are bodies. There are minds walking around. And there is an awareness of those things. And you're basically a localized awareness. What you think you are is localized awareness. And this goes back to the oldest question that I think you know Ramana Maharshi used to talk about, is just who am I?

我认为开悟的人——又是一个糟糕的词——到达那里的那种方式是：他们有持续的无我体验，而那变成他们新的默认。它是二元的。通往它的并不是一条路径。而他们中一些人到达那里的方式之一——他们中一些人就是天生的，几乎生来如此。他们中一些人有一次神秘体验。他们中一些人有一次精神崩溃。那字面上就是克里希那穆提已经有过的，对吧？他有那个叫做「过程」的东西，它不断回到他身上，而那看起来就像一次精神崩溃，加上他的兄弟去世，他经历了一段糟糕的时光。有些人通过冥想到达那里，你知道，几十年的冥想。有些人通过多少把生命奉献于服务他人而到达那里。就像他们的自我消失了，他们看穿了它。但我发现最有意思的那种是：他们只是做所谓的自我审察。而自我审察只是寻找那个自我，寻找这个分离的自我。你确信你是埃里克。我确信我是纳瓦尔。

The one thing that I think the way the enlightened people—again, bad word—get there is they have a persistent experience of no-self and that becomes their new default. It is binary. It's not a pathway to it. And one of the ways that some of them get there—some of them are just naturals, almost born that way. Some of them they have a mystical experience. Some of them they have a psychotic break. That's literally what Krishnamurti already had, right? He had this thing called the process which like kept coming back to him and it just seems like a psychotic break plus his brother died and he went through a bad time. Some people get there through meditation, you know, decades of meditation. Some people get there through just kind of devoting their life in service to others. Like their self disappears, they see through it. But the one that I found the most interesting was they just do what's called self-examination. And self-examination is just looking for the self, looking for this separate self. You're convinced you're Eric. I'm convinced I'm Naval.

我们那样说是什么意思？那是什么？那是一个非常模糊的定义。那究竟是什么？给我找出这个不断地谈论自己、指称自己的埃里克。给我找出这个受苦的埃里克。给我找出这个感到骄傲的埃里克。给我找出这个兴旺的埃里克。给我找出这个感觉好的埃里克。给我找出这个感觉不好的埃里克。这家伙藏在哪里？这是谁？就像去找那个。而你会发现的是：如果你找、找、找，你永远钉不住它。它总是像总在你视力的边缘。那就是艾伦·瓦茨所描述的：就像你拿一根燃烧的棍子旋转它，看起来你有一个火轮。你并没有一个火轮。你所拥有的是许多转来转去的念头，它们说服你那里有这个叫埃里克的角色。但并没有一个实际的埃里克。自我只是一个念头。这些只是正在发生的念头，它们说服你那里有某个人。所以即便当我在对你说话时，有词语出来，有念头出来。那不是我。它们只是飞出来。我没有在控制它们。没有一个分离的我坐在那里控制它们。然后有那么一刻，我对我怎样说了这些、怎样向你表达感到有点骄傲。那种骄傲的感觉是什么？它只是一种感觉。那种感觉在指称谁？它没有在指称任何人。没有它在指称的那个人。它只是另一个念头。那种骄傲只是另一个没有靶子的念头。好，如果我足够久地继续寻找那个靶子，那么也许我终于会意识到：实际上那个靶子并不存在。然后当我意识到那个靶子不存在，或者那个靶子从未存在过。而如果那个靶子从未存在过，那么最终那些朝向一个假靶子而去的念头会开始平息，因为它们是假的。它们没有用。那种知识将不再以多伊奇的意义在我心里被复制。然后我会处于平和，因为我将不再有这些自我中心的情绪和起伏，并且我会意识到这里从来没有任何人，从头到尾都只是意识。而有些人把那个叫做上帝。

What do we mean by that? What is that? It's a very fuzzy definition. What is that exactly? Find me this Eric that's constantly talking about himself, referring to himself. Find me this Eric who suffers. Find me this Eric who feels pride. Find me this Eric who thrives. Find me this Eric who feels good. Find me this Eric who feels bad. Where is this guy hiding? Who is this? Like look for that. And what you find is if you look, look, look, you can never pin it down. It's always like it's always on the edge of your vision. It's what Alan Watts described as like you take a burning stick and you whirl it around and it looks like you have a flaming wheel. You don't have a flaming wheel. What you have is you have a lot of thoughts going around that convince you that there's this character in there called Eric. But there's no actual Eric. The self is just a thought. These are just thoughts that are happening that are convincing you there's someone there. So even when I'm speaking to you, there's words coming out and there's thoughts coming out. That's not me. They're just flying out. I'm not controlling them. There's no separate me sitting there controlling them. And then for a moment, I felt sort of proud of how I said this and articulated to you. What is that feeling of pride? It's just a feeling. Who is that feeling referring to? It's not referring to anyone. There's no one that it's referring to. It's just another thought. That pride was just another thought without a target. Well, if I keep looking for that target long enough, then maybe I'll finally realize actually that target doesn't exist. And then when I realize that target doesn't exist, or that target has never existed. And if that target has never existed, then eventually those thoughts that were going towards a fake target will start dying down because they're false. They're useless. That knowledge will no longer be replicated in my mind in the Deutschian sense. And then I will be at peace because I will no longer have these egotistical emotions and ups and downs and I'll realize there was never anybody here and it was all just consciousness all along. And some people call that God.

埃里克：我得花一段时间才能弄懂那一切。

Eric: It's going to take me a while to grok all that.

纳瓦尔：但那就是重点。对。我是说也许——我是说有些人非常幸运，它立刻就发生在他们身上，而大多数人从来不想要它，所以没关系。

Naval: But that's the point. Yeah. I mean maybe—I mean some people are very lucky it happens for them right away and most people never want it so it's fine.

埃里克：对。

Eric: Yeah.

纳瓦尔：你——真正开悟的人，如果你问他们，就像你知道，就像我以不同方式和他们所有人都走过这个，但你知道我测试过他们。我在这件事上有点贫嘴，但我测试过他们，看他们是否以任何方式感到情绪上的痛苦，对吧？而我最喜欢测试他们的方式——就像，好，你知道，最好的测试是微妙的，他们并不，你知道，并不明显，但我喜欢问他们的那一个是：好，如果你没有开悟呢？如果你失去了你的开悟呢？或者如果你从未开悟过呢？他们对此完全没问题，对吧？因为真理并不会因为你知不知道它而改变。而如果你不在那里去知道真理，如果你在任何分离的意义上并不存在，你知不知道它并不重要。

纳瓦尔：对。他们绝对可以。他们其实就在那样做。我提到的那些人里，有些有工作，其中一个在经营一家公司，你知道，其中一个就像很擅长约会，约会很多，对吧？他们就是绝对拥有完全清晰的心智。他们有完全能运转的身体。他们甚至有欲望。他们甚至有想要和需要，但那些不是自我性的。它们没有缠在一种很容易受到威胁、会生气或会像抑郁或任何那样的渺小自我感里。他们会采取你和我会采取的行动，但他们会平静得多地采取它们，并且他们不会把任何事当成针对个人。所以，他们仍然在从环境得到反馈。他们仍然在反应。而且如果要说有什么的话，我会争辩他们比以往任何时候都更有能力，而他们的欲望更真诚。他们更少模仿。他们更少以地位为导向。那么，他们是否比比如说像我这样的人更少动力？对，我还没遇见过一个和我一样有动力的开悟的人。另一方面，我也没遇见过很多和我一样有动力的人。对吧？也许我觉得我这辈子可能遇见过五个更有动力的人。对吧？所以，很难和那个集合相交。而我会接受那个交换吗，你知道，稍微少一点动力，以换取成为一个——

It's another chase for a shiny object. It's a chase for things being different than they are. And the key thing about enlightened people is that they are fine with everything the way it is. And which includes not being fine with the way things are. They self-actualize. They may self-actualize in more authentic ways that are different than how they would have self-actualized pre-enlightenment, but they still self-actualize.

埃里克：我真的对你打破这些虚假二元对立的方式感兴趣，对吧？而你刚才就提到了一个，也就是，你知道，开悟的、看起来缺少动力的人潜在地更有效。你知道，你变得更幸福时，你也变得更有效，即便每个人都有这种恐惧：哦，如果我变得幸福，或者如果我接受现状，我会更低产。

纳瓦尔：对。你宁愿和哪一个战斗？一个开悟的终结者，还是一个裹在自我里的终结者。裹在自我里的终结者更容易被操纵。也许有内疚，也许有自我怀疑，也许有愤怒，会更低效。一个开悟的终结者才是真正的终结者。你需要立刻离那东西远一点。

埃里克：这很有意思。

纳瓦尔：情绪有它的位置。现代社会非常安全。所以我们感到的很多愤怒，源自愤怒是为身体暴力或身体原因而存在的那些时代。但我们现在把它当成心智上的来承受。现代痛苦很大一部分来自：我们慢性地感受那些本应在短时间里被急性感受的东西。所以我只是不认为你因为更幸福就会更低效。你会选择做不同的事情。所以它也许看起来像：哦，你在往更坏的方向改变，因为你也许看起来动力更少，或动力指向另一件事，但你动力所向的，是那种会在你幸福的同时让你更有效的东西。顺便说，没有人选择回到更不幸福。

纳瓦尔：正确。变得幸福。没错。这些是严格优势策略。这些是纯粹的善。所以当人们说「我不重视幸福」，那只是不真。在行动上那不真，因为每一次你幸福过，你就不想放弃那个。而每一次你做了让你稍微更幸福一点的事，你都不会放弃那个。你知道，就像你多少走过人生进展时的一件事：有一个阶段你有室友，然后你到达了不再有室友，对吧？你不想回到像有室友之前的生活，但然后你有了孩子，他们是最差的室友，也是最好的。孩子们长大离开时也有点糟，对吧？你不想回到通勤，你知道，如果你有过很长的通勤，然后你开始走路去上班或有很短的通勤。再回到通勤有点糟。退休，你知道，或者为自己工作。这些是单向门。很难再从那些门走回来。

Naval: Yeah, we will disagree because I think that it's very difficult to be happy if you're bound up in needy relationships, and just needing the relationship is needy. Now, obviously, I have my family and I love them very much and they're very important to me, but I'm not going to stay in any relationship that makes me unhappy. I will do my duty. The family's functioning really well, but at the same time, you have to be happy on your own. You know, the secret to a happy relationship is two happy people. Okay? 'Cause you can't be happy with your spouse. They'll drag you down or you'll drag them down. So, it's important that each person find happiness. And happiness is personal. They each have to work on it. The worst thing you can do in a relationship is go to somebody else and say, "Cheer up. Be happy. You know, what's wrong with you? Why are you depressed?" Right? It doesn't work, right? So it has to be tackled at an individual level and then once your own house is in order then you can form genuine relationships with other people. But putting relationships ahead of your own work and your own happiness I think is a mistake. You will get neither.

埃里克：我觉得那是一个很好的表述，就像它们是有价值的、重要的。你想按那个顺序追求它们。而如果我描述得正确，你的路径是：你想让它们最大限度地真实。就像你不想进入一段关系去试图让别人幸福。

纳瓦尔：而且我不想他们做不诚实的事来让我幸福。我不想他们，你知道，觉得我是一个负担或一项义务。你知道，就像我遇到的问题之一是：作为父母，我做事情，你知道，为我的孩子，因为我爱他们。而我周围的人总是试图训练他们说谢谢。他们会说：「哦，你知道，你爸爸给了你一样东西。你说谢谢了吗？」我就像：别，别那样做。别训练他们。他们不是动物。就像如果他们真诚地感到感激，它会以某种方式出来。我不需要它。我不是为了感激才做的，对吧？而且我不想像训练动物那样训练他们。如果他们真诚地感谢，它会出来，你知道？如果他们不，它就不会。但别强迫它。我不想要任何方向上任何被强迫的义务关系。对。我唯一相信的关系是同伴关系。就像显然有人为你工作，但即便那时你也总是想在给定情境下尽可能把他们当同伴对待，因为否则它最终会形式化，最终会仪式化，最终会虚假，最终会不幸福。你知道，你在哪里找到——这很有意思，如果你去一个像婚礼这样的活动，或你去一个大派对，很多时候你会发现很多关于幸福的表演，你知道人们欢呼，对你知道，但就像他们并不是——真货很少在那里，你知道。真正的幸福来自更小的、更真诚、更少被强迫的互动。就像如果你被强迫穿晚礼服，你被强迫在某个时间到场，然后你被强迫听一篇演讲，然后你被强迫敬酒，你被强迫四处走动，而这就是：那并不是真正让你幸福的东西。如果那真正是让你幸福的东西，那你每天都会那样做。你会去每一个派对、每一个婚礼、每一个成年礼，以及每一个你被邀请的活动。但在某种层面上，那些事情是乏味的。你为别人做它们，他们为你做它们。就像谁是为自己做的，对吧？所以我觉得真正的庆祝是一件美的事，但它被仪式化得太多了，就像情人节，必须送花，你知道，贺曼贺卡，所有这些东西都是仪式性的。真正的幸福是当你和别人在一起时真诚地感激，并且你知道你为他们做一件善事只因为你想做，而你必须在生活中为那个创造空间，你必须创造让你真实地感到它的情境，好让人们真诚地，你知道，在你身上激发出那个，而我不想要任何仪式性的东西。我永远不想要一张卡片。我永远不想要一个，你知道，仪式性的礼物。我不想要一枚勋章。我不想要一个奖项。你知道，所有这些东西都没有用。

Naval: And I don't want them to do things to make me happy that are dishonest. I don't want them to, you know, feel like I'm a burden or an obligation. You know, like one of the issues I have is like, as a parent, like I do things, you know, for my kids because I love them. And people around me are always trying to train them to say thank you. They'll say, "Oh, you know, your dad gave you something. Did you say thank you?" I'm like, don't don't do that. Don't train them. They're not an animal. Like if they genuinely feel gratitude, it'll come out in some way. I don't need it. I'm not doing it for the gratitude, right? And I don't want to train them like an animal. If they're genuinely thankful, it'll come out, you know? And if they're not, it won't. But don't force it. I don't want any forced-obligation relationship in either direction. Yeah. The only relationships I believe in are peer relationships. Like obviously people work for you but even then you always want to treat them as much like a peer as you can given the circumstances, because otherwise it ends up formalized, it ends up ritualistic, it ends up false, it ends up unhappy. You know, where do you find—it's funny, if you go to an event like a wedding or you go to like a big party a lot of times you'll find a lot of show about happiness, you know people cheering, yeah you know, but like they're not—the genuine article is rarely there, you know. The genuine happiness comes in smaller interactions that are more genuine and less forced. Like if you're forced to wear a tux and you're forced to show up at a certain time, then you're forced to listen to a speech, then you're forced to do a toast and you're forced to go around and it's just like that's not what actually makes you happy. If it was genuinely what makes you happy, then you would be doing that every day. You'd be going to every party and every wedding and every bar mitzvah and everything you get invited to. But at some level, those things are tedious. You're doing them for other people and they're doing them for you. Like who's doing it for themselves, right? So I think like true celebration is a beautiful thing but it has been ritualized so much, like Valentine's Day, having to send flowers, you know, Hallmark cards, all this stuff is ritualistic. Real happiness is when you're genuinely grateful with somebody else and you know you do something kind for them because you just want to, and you have to create space for that in your life and you have to create situations where you feel it authentically for people to genuinely, you know, inspire that in you, and I don't want anything ritualistic. I never want a card. I never want a, you know, a ritualistic gift. I don't want a medal. I don't want an award. You know, all these things are useless.

埃里克：我本来想问你，你知道，帮助人们多少找到在生活中如此真实的勇气。但是，你知道，我觉得它只是回到长期的看法，就像，你知道，真理，即便它造成短期的痛苦，就像，嘿，我不会做这个来让你幸福。我不想你只为了让我幸福而做这个，是长期造就这个的最好方式。

Eric: I was gonna ask you about, you know, helping people sort of find the courage to be that truthful in their lives. But, you know, I think it just goes back to the long-term view like, you know, that the truth, even if it causes short-term pain of like, hey, I'm not going to do this to make you happy. I don't want you to do this just to make me happy, is the best way to create this long term.

纳瓦尔：那绝对是真的。但我觉得它甚至比那更深。那就是：真理不可谈判。就像给我找出一个说我不想要真理的人。那是一个罕见的人，对吧？

Naval: That's absolutely true. But I think it's even deeper than that. It's that truth is non-negotiable. Like find me a single person who says I don't want the truth. That's a rare person, right?

埃里克：好，就表达出的偏好而言——我觉得大概有很多人活在、选择活在谎言里。我觉得他们不想让别人失望，所以我觉得我们都被困在这个集体陷阱里，对吧？

Eric: Well, the expressed preference—I think there's probably plenty of people living, choosing to live lies. I think they don't want to disappoint other people and I think so we're all caught in this collective trap, right?

纳瓦尔：就像新冠做的好事之一是：它打破了通勤的集体陷阱，对吧？它让在家工作合法化了。现在有些人也许走过了头，他们完全退出了。但总体上它打破了社会对通勤的瘾，对不必要地通勤去开不必要的会的瘾，而那是好事。所以我觉得同样，如果我们能打破社会对排场和仪式的瘾，会是好事，对吧？就像情人节，就像那应当一直和你爱的人自发地、自然地发生，而不是在 2 月 14 日，每个人都试图挤进同一家定价过高的餐厅，买同样定价过高的花，送同样定价过高的巧克力，勾上同一个格子，对吧？而如果你并不自然地想做这件事，那就意味着你的关系出了问题，你应当修复它，或者你应当走向另一段关系。

Naval: Like one of the things COVID did that was good was it broke the collective trap of commuting, right? It legitimized work from home. Now some people might take it too far and they just checked out completely. But overall it broke society of its addiction of commuting, of unnecessary commuting to unnecessary meetings, and that was good. So I think the same way like it would be good if we can break society of its addiction to pomp and circumstance, right? Like Valentine's Day, like that should be happening all the time with the person you love spontaneously and naturally instead of February 14th, everybody tries to get in the same overpriced restaurant and buys the same overpriced flowers and gives the same overpriced chocolates, checks the same box, right? And if you don't feel like doing it naturally, then that means there's something wrong with your relationship that you should fix or you should move on to another relationship.

拯救自己

Saving Yourself

纳瓦尔：但正确的答案并不是每年做这种勾格子的虚假之事，把罐子往前踢，只是浪费每个人的时间。

Naval: But what's not the correct answer is to do this falsehood of checking the box every year and kicking the can down the road, just wasting everybody's time.

哦，我有过整整一条帖子串，讲很多伟大的美德是自私的。我几乎可以把每一种美德重新解释成你为了长期自私而做的事。那就是诀窍，对吧？现代的魔鬼是廉价多巴胺。所以我发了那条推，而这其实是我发推的原因之一，因为有时我会得到一个很棒的回应。所以，我发推：「现代的魔鬼是廉价多巴胺。」有人回应：「从来都是。」这是真的。魔鬼从来都是廉价多巴胺。你回到七宗罪，全是廉价多巴胺。它基本上是在说：不要做一件现在让你感觉好、因为它以后会坑你的事。所以反过来，你知道，我发出的推文之一，我想埃隆喜欢，他评论了它，是：美德是那样一套价值，如果我们全都作为个体把它们承担起来，会给社会带来双赢结果。所以美德是对你作为个体长期有益的事情——然后那是自私的，长期自私——并且它们对集体长期有益，因为它们让我们能玩重复的囚徒困境，或玩重复的猎鹿一类博弈，从博弈论角度看处在一种正面均衡里，导向好的博弈论结果。如果社会中每个人哪怕遵循经典的圣经美德，整个社会都会升级，而每个个体都会过得更好。所以那是真的。但我也觉得，所有美德，如果你作为个体在足够长的时间框架上遵循它们，它们会让你个体过得更好。所以它是终极的棉花糖实验。顺便说，那个实验并不能重复。所以，抱歉提起它，但它是那些每个人都知道的行为心理实验之一。但它在某种意义上是——好生活很大一部分就是棉花糖实验。

Oh, I had this whole thread on how a lot of the great virtues are selfish. I can reinterpret almost every virtue as something you do for long-term selfishness. That's the trick, right? The modern devil is cheap dopamine. So I tweeted that, and this was actually one of the reasons why I tweet, because sometimes I get a great response. So, I tweeted, "The modern devil is cheap dopamine." And someone responded, "Always has been." It's true. The devil has always been cheap dopamine. You go back to the seven deadly sins, it's all cheap dopamine. It's basically saying, don't do something that makes you feel good right now 'cause it's going to screw you over later. So on the flip side, you know, one of the tweets that I sent out which I think Elon liked, he commented on it, was that virtues are the set of values that if we were to all take them on individually would lead to a win-win outcome for society. So the virtues are things that are long-term good for you as an individual—and then which is selfish, long-term selfish—and they're long-term good for the collective because they allow us to play iterated prisoner's dilemma, or to play iterated stag-hunt kind of games, in a positive equilibrium from a game-theory perspective that lead to good game-theoretic outcomes. If everybody in society followed even the classic biblical virtues, all society would level up and each individual would be better off. So that's true. But I also think that all the virtues, if you were to follow them as an individual on a long enough time frame, they would make you individually better off. So it's the ultimate marshmallow test. By the way, that experiment doesn't replicate. So, sorry to bring it up, but it's one of those behavioral-psych experiments everybody knows. But it is in some sense—a lot of a good life is the marshmallow test.

而且有很多事情人们认为你必须按标准方式去做。就像慈善就是一个好例子，对吧？你必须去为慈善做志愿者，去帮助远方的人。那么，帮助就在你旁边的人怎么样，对吧？住在街道那头的人，为你工作的人，和你一起工作的人。一般来说帮助那些人在自私意义上回报非常高。你知道，你可以从中得到一些好处回来。所以，那是开始做慈善的好地方。它非常本地，非常可行动。所以，你知道，你的钱没有被浪费。你没有被骗。它没有被转走。而它只是一种做实际之事的方式。我的另一条推文是：你家破碎了，但你要去修复世界。对吧？这就是整个现代行动主义病：你在为几千英里之外、你从未亲眼见过的事业鼓噪，依据的是你听说的东西，因为它们在社交上流行。但现实是你可以直接走上街去帮助那个无家可归的人，或那个退伍军人，或那个找工作的穷人，诸如此类，或者你知道字面上走去和你叔叔或你父母和解。当你连自己本地的家庭都弄不好时，你有什么权利说我知道怎样修复世界是对的。对吧？帮助世界另一边的人没问题，但当你连自己的本地问题都解决不了时，不要鼓噪着说我知道什么是正确的。你在解决本地问题之后，才得到解决全球问题的权利。这在环保主义里也成立。每个人都在担心二氧化碳。那么，河流和森林等等呢？

And there are a lot of things that people think that you have to do it the standard way. Like philanthropy is a good example, right? You have to go like go volunteer for charity and help out people who are far away. Well, how about helping out people who are right next to you, right? People who live down the street, people who work for you, people who work with you. Generally helping out those people is selfishly very rewarding. You know, you could get some benefit back from it. So, it's a good place to start with charity. It's very local, very actionable. So, you know, your money is not being wasted. You're not being tricked. It's not being redirected. And it's just a way to do something practical. Another tweet I had was your family is broken, but you're going to fix the world. Right? This is the whole modern activism disease where you're agitating about causes that are thousands of miles away that you've never laid eyes on based on what you hear because they're socially popular. But the reality is you could just go walk down the street and help the homeless person or the vet or the poor person looking for a job or what have you, or you know literally go make amends with your uncle or your parents. What right do you have to say I know what's right to fix the world when you can't even get your local family going. Right? It's okay to help people on the other side of the world, but don't agitate for saying, I know what's correct, when you can't even solve your own local problems. You get the right to solve global problems after you solve local problems. That's true in environmentalism, too. Everybody's worried about CO2. Well, what about rivers and forests and so on?

埃里克：而这回到你的——我想那是你的推文：你的能力越大，你的部落就越大，以及那种资源分配的迭代循环：社会把更多信任、更多杠杆、更多影响力给予那些把周围的人照顾得很好的人。

Eric: And this goes back to your—I think it was your tweet, the greater your ability, the greater your tribe, and the sort of iterative loop of like resource allocation where society gives more trust and more leverage and more influence to people who are doing a good job of taking care of the people around them.

纳瓦尔：对。很久以前有人在 Twitter 上问我关于埃隆。那是好多年好多年以前，在他成为世上最富的人以及那一切之前。有人说，你怎么看埃隆？我说，好，你知道，当有人把整个生命奉献于给予社会它所需要的东西时，社会就别无选择，只能给予他们想要的一切，对吧？但是你不能依赖那个。你不能那么自私地去做，或算得那么精确。但最终因为他的欲望是纯粹的——无论人们今天怎么说埃隆，忘掉和埃隆有关的那些——但当你拥有比你自己更大的志向时，其他人就会与你对齐，因为有其他人觉得：好，你的动机是纯粹的。我也想要那个东西。所以让我们排起队来，而你变成一个谢林点。在博弈论意义上，你变成其他想看到那件事的人的集结口号，让他们聚集过来。你知道在那个意义上，如果你说我想去火星，我想让人类成为能航行太空的物种。那么我们的技术建造者人口中有很大一部分分享同一个愿景，他们很杰出，他们感到被阻碍，他们现在看到一辆能去做那件事的载具。所以他们会字面上走去在 SpaceX 下面建造一辆载具去做成那件事。所以通过让那个使命比关于你自己更大，你把人聚集起来。类似马丁·路德·金说「我有一个梦想」演讲时，它非常包容。它非常高层。它非常统一。它是普遍的。所以它把人拉到一起。这也是马克思主义有如此诱人吸引力的原因，因为，你知道，马克思说，不，它是一块，你知道，白板。我们都平等。我们都一样。圣灵，全是意识。大家都聚到一起。它是一种朝向普遍性的宗教拉力。所以使命越普遍，它就会把越多人拉到一起，无论它是否有效。这算是这里的讽刺。

Naval: Yeah. Somebody asked me about Elon a long time ago on Twitter. This is years and years and years ago before he was the richest man in the world and all that. And somebody said, what do you think of Elon? And I said, well, you know, when someone devotes their entire life to like giving society what it needs, then society has no choice but to give them everything they want, right? And but you can't rely on that. You can't do it that selfishly or count that closely. But ultimately because his desire was pure—regardless of what people say about Elon today, forgetting whatever with Elon—but when you have aspirations that are larger than yourself, then other people will align with you because there are other people who feel that okay, your motives are pure. I want that thing too. So let's line up and you become a Schelling point. In a game-theory sense, you become a rallying cry for other people who want to see that thing, to mass around. You know in that sense if you say I want to go to Mars, I want to make humans a space-faring species. Well there is a large percentage of our technical builder population that shares that same vision, that are brilliant, that have felt stymied, that now see a vehicle to do that. So they will literally go and build a vehicle under SpaceX to go do that. So by having that mission be larger than about yourself, you assemble people. Similar like Martin Luther King when he says I have a dream speech, it's very inclusive. It's very high level. It's very unifying. It's universal. And so it pulls people together. It's also the reason why Marxism has such a seductive appeal because, you know, Marx says, no, it's a, you know, blank slate. We're all equal. We're all the same. Holy Spirit, it's all consciousness. Everybody get together. It's a religious pull towards universality. So the more universal the mission, the more people it's going to pull together regardless of whether it's effective or not. That's sort of the irony here.

埃里克：对。好，这就到了，你知道，一个想法的迷因学会相对于它的可传播性而传播，而不是相对于它的正确性。

Eric: Yeah. Well, this gets to the, you know, the memetics of an idea will spread relative to its spreadability, not relative to its correctness.

纳瓦尔：它会按它的可传播性传播，但然后它会按它的正确性存活。所以，一个因为某种坏想法的良好传播特性而采纳它的文明，会把自己从地图上抹掉，有点像苏联那样。所以，你现在剩下的是俄罗斯，但它不是苏联。它是完全不同的结构。所以，比如说中国共产党，你知道，当它在毛泽东治下、他们搞了大跃进，有了所有那些苦难和饥荒，你知道，在那次事件中死去的中国人比第二次世界大战的全部伤亡还多，而且是把所有人加在一起——不是中国人，只是大跃进中的中国死亡，对比第二次世界大战中所有原因的全部伤亡，对吧？所以那就向你表明一种坏想法能做什么。但然后他们怎么活下来的？好，他们转向了资本主义。他们开始有可以——这是一种政府控制的资本主义，但实质上他们用私人耕作在经济中创造了一个资本主义部分，并最终用仍然由国家资金支持的私人企业，但他们变得越私有化，他们起来得就越快。所以最终你必须采纳好想法或好解释，因为你只会被消灭。自己也要下注会把你淘汰。所以你是对的，坏想法可以传播，而不幸的是那些坏想法被清除的方式，并不那么是我们必然吸取了教训，而是拥有那些坏想法的实体、人、制度活不下来。它们被淘汰。所以你真的想成为一个拥有一堆正确想法的实体，这样你才不会被淘汰。

Naval: It will spread according to its spreadability, but then it'll survive according to its correctness. So, a civilization that adopts a bad idea because of its good spread characteristics wipes itself off the map, kind of like the Soviet Union did. And so, you're left with Russia now, but it's not the Soviet Union. It's a completely different structure. So, and for example the Chinese Communist Party, you know, when it was under Mao and they made the Great Leap Forward and there was all that suffering and starvation, you know, more Chinese people died during that event than all the casualties in World War II, and from all people put together—not the Chinese, just Chinese deaths in the Great Leap Forward versus all casualties in all causes in World War II, right? So that just shows you like what a bad idea can do. But then how did they survive? Well they switched to capitalism. They started having local farms that could—it's a government-controlled capitalism, but essentially they created a capitalist section to their economy with private farming and eventually with private enterprises that are still backed by state money, but the more privatized they became, the faster they came up. So eventually you have to adopt good ideas or good explanations because you'll just get wiped out. The skin in the game will eliminate you. So you're right, bad ideas can spread, and unfortunately the way those bad ideas get taken out is not that we necessarily learn our lessons so much, but it's that the entities, the people, the institutions that had those bad ideas don't survive. They get eliminated. So you really want to be an entity with a bunch of correct ideas so you don't get eliminated.

对。我对多伊奇关于知识的定义有一种伪精神性的看法——他每次提到时总会笑，因为他知道我很投入它，而它带有一种他并不投入的伪精神语境——那就是：真理是多元宇宙中的一块晶体。所以如果你采用量子物理学的多元宇宙理论，那是最直接的解释，就像如果你基本上相信，并没有某种导致坍缩的观察者有什么特殊之处，并且观察者、未被观察者和被观察者全都一样，那么最直截了当的解释——或者说在那些条件下运转的系统完全一样，那是最直截了当的解释——那么多元宇宙理论就从那里自然浮现。然后它说：不，仅仅因为我看某样东西，仅仅因为我看那只猫，并不意味着在薛定谔的猫里那只猫是活的或死的。而是那只猫在多元宇宙的某些部分是死的，在多元宇宙的其他部分是活的。而直到我打开盒子，我只是不知道我在哪个宇宙里。

Yeah. My pseudo-spiritual take on Deutsch's definition of knowledge—which he always laughs about when he mentions because he knows like I'm into it and it has a pseudo-spiritual context to it which he's not into—is that truth is a crystal in the multiverse. So if you go with the multiverse theory of quantum physics, which is the most direct interpretation, like if you basically believe there's nothing special about an observer that causes the collapse, and that observers, unobserved and the observed are all the same, then the most straightforward interpretation—or systems operating in those conditions are exactly the same, which is the most straightforward interpretation—then the multiverse theory naturally emerges from that. Then it says no, just because I look at something, just because I look at the cat doesn't mean that the cat is alive or dead in Schrödinger's cat. It's that the cat was dead in some parts of the multiverse and it was alive in other parts of the multiverse. And I just didn't know which universe I was in until I opened the box.

而如果你采用那种解释，那么就有一个结果的多元宇宙。基本上有无限数量的宇宙，它们总是根据粒子向左走还是向右走而分化成子部分。这件事发生了还是那件事发生了？并且有各种各样的事情能导致那些宇宙分叉。但如果你去看那些宇宙的内容，知识是一种倾向于传播的东西，因为它是正确的。真理存活，因为它对得上。它确实如此。你可以用真理做事。你避开虚假。所以真实之事为真的宇宙，比虚假为真的宇宙更多，因为虚假是无限可变的。对我拥有的每一个真理，都有无限的虚假解释，而真解释的数量是有限的。相对而言，你可以有大体为真的解释。你永远得不到绝对真理。但真解释的集合比虚假的集合小得多。然而，一旦真理被找到，因为它难以改动，并且因为它会传播，虚假就被淘汰。所以如果有一个多元宇宙，并且有无限个版本的你——再说一次，我觉得这是对量子物理学一个相当直截了当的解释。如果有一个多元宇宙，并且有无限个版本的你，那么那些最繁盛的、以及那些彼此最相像的，实际上就是最真的那些你。那些找到了最多真理的。假的那些以各种各样的方式分化。但真的那些彼此更相似。而它们每一个所实现的、穿透过去的真理，比虚假的所有变体更相似。所以你想成为的，是你自己最以真理为导向的那个版本，而那就是它自身最真的版本。

And if you take that interpretation, then there is a multiverse of outcomes. There's basically an infinite number of universes that are always differentiating into subsections based on did the particle go left or did it go right? Did this happen or did that happen? And there are various things that can cause those universes to diverge. But if you were to look at the content of those universes, knowledge is a thing that tends to spread because it's correct. Truth survives because it matches up. It does. You can do things with truth. You avoid falsehoods. So there are more universes in which the true thing is true than the falsehood, because falsehoods are infinitely variable. For every truth I have, there's unlimited false explanations, where there is a finite number of true explanations. Relatively speaking, you can have mostly true explanations. You never get the absolute truth. But the set of true explanations is much smaller than the set of falsehoods. However, once the truth is found, because it is hard to vary and because it spreads, the falsehood gets eliminated. So if there's a multiverse and there are infinite versions of you—and again I think there's a pretty straightforward interpretation of quantum physics. If there's a multiverse and there's infinite versions of you, then the ones that are flourishing the most and the ones that are most alike are actually the most true versions of you. The ones that have found the most truth. The false ones are differentiated in all kinds of ways. But the true ones are more similar to each other. And the truth that each of them have realized that cuts through is more similar than all the variations of falseness. So what you want to be is you want to be the most truth-oriented version of yourself, and that is the most true version of itself.

而现在我们可以转到《瑞克和莫蒂》，因为在《瑞克和莫蒂》里有无限个瑞克，对吧？有无限的宇宙，有无限个瑞克，而瑞克是一个英雄科学家。并且有这个瑞克议会，就像无限个瑞克，因为他们可以穿越多元宇宙。他们碰面，而你注意到的有趣事情之一是：瑞克 C-137，也就是故事的主角，他就像最——他是瑞克，他很怪，但他是瑞克里最正常的。其余的都以某种方式很怪。他们都以某种方式是假的。而他们甚至叫他——他们说他是瑞克中最瑞克的，对吧？所以，我不知道他们是否把这个想通了，或者他们是撞上了它，或者它只是又一条进入这里的原则，但我把那看成你想成为的那个自己版本的一个有趣例子。你想成为瑞克中最瑞克的。我想成为纳瓦尔中最纳瓦尔的。你想成为埃里克中最埃里克的。而那就是你自己最以真理为导向的版本，而这——要找到真理意味着最少的动机性推理，意味着最低的自我，意味着最少的自己，意味着那个为了最伟大、最普遍的原则而在最高层面上运转的人。它是那个对科学和哲学最有知识的人，并且你知道大概是最可能在创造美的作品的那一个，并且大概就是最幸福的。

And now we can segue into Rick and Morty, because in Rick and Morty there's infinite Ricks, right? There's infinite universes, there's infinite Ricks, and Rick is a hero scientist. And there's this Council of Ricks where like the infinite Ricks, because they can cross across the multiverse. They meet up, and one of the funny things you notice is that Rick C-137, which is the hero of the story, he's like the most—he's Rick, he's weird, but he's the most normal of the Ricks. The rest of them are all weird in some way. They're all false in some way. And they even call him—they say he's the Rickest of the Ricks, right? So, I don't know if they thought this through or if they stumbled upon it or if it's just another principle that gets in here, but I view that as like a funny example of the version of yourself that you want to be. You want to be the Rickest of the Ricks. I want to be the most Naval of the Navals. You want to be the most Eric of the Erics. And that's the most truth-oriented version of yourself, which—and to find truth it means the least motivated reasoning, which means the lowest ego, which means the least self, which means the one who's operating at the highest level for the greatest, most universal principles. It's the one who is the most knowledgeable about science and about philosophy, and you know is probably the one who's most likely to be creating works of beauty and is just probably the happiest.

埃里克：你怎样看待降低自我与找到真实性之间的互动？就像我觉得有些人把他们的身份想成使自己独特的那些东西，也把真实性想成投入那些使你独特的东西。

Eric: How do you think about the interaction between lowering of ego and finding authenticity? Like I think some people think of their identity as like the things that make them unique, and authenticity also as like leaning into the things that make you unique.

纳瓦尔：我把真实性想成只是不戴上一副假面具，不拥有一张面具。而我们为什么戴上假人格和面具？那是因为我们想被以某种方式看见。我们在乎别人怎么想。我们想融入。我们有一个我们试图投射的形象和身份。所以当你试图投射一个与你实际是谁对不上的自我形象时，那就是你不真实的时候。所以你需要把那张面具拿掉。而如果你把那张面具拿掉，那你就面对尴尬的风险，也就是人们说：哦，你知道，你不该那样做。就像我刚开始发推时，我收到，你知道，来自风投的信息，像是说：哦，那很怪。我的合伙人说你不可投资。你是一个怪人。你脱轨了。我把真实性看成不假装成某个你所不是的人或某物的一种产出。所以，那就是戴面具。那就是太过努力。那就是示意地位。那就是使用行话，你知道，好让自己听起来聪明。那就是声称知道你并不知道的事，或声称不知道你确实知道的事。就像谦卑也是一个陷阱，就像虚假的谦卑，你知道人们会点破它，你知道谦虚式炫耀，但谦卑也是一场自我之旅。你知道如果你实际上是低自我的，就没有谦卑这种东西，正如没有骄傲这种东西。它们多少是一起的。所以真实性导向更好的生活，再说一次是长期的，像所有美德一样。所以它除去那些在周围、你知道只因为他们以为你是某个你所不是的人才在那里的人。所以你可以放下伪装。你必须在他们身边如履薄冰。它把你从那些你必须假装、强迫、挣扎或努力才能留在里面的情境中除去。所以它帮助你在关系和情境中导航到正确的答案。但其中有痛苦，因为有变化。你知道，这件事的一部分是：是的，我懒，但我不会接受坏结果。你知道，我有一种能力，能从次优的关系或情境中走开。即便它痛苦，我就是不会留在里面。

Naval: I think of authenticity as just not putting on a fake persona, not having a mask. And why do we put on fake personas and masks? It's because we want to be seen a certain way. We care what other people think. We want to fit in. We have an image and an identity that we're trying to project. So when you're trying to project a self-image that doesn't match up to who you actually are, that's when you are inauthentic. So you need to take that mask off. And if you take that mask off, then you face the risk of embarrassment, which is people say, oh, you know, you're not supposed to do that. Like when I was first tweeting, I got, you know, messages from VCs like saying, oh, that's weird. My partners say you're not backable. You're a weirdo. You've gone off the rails. I view authenticity as an output of not pretending to be someone or something other than you are. So, that's wearing masks. That's trying too hard. That's signaling status. That's using jargon, you know, to sound smart. That's claiming to know something that you don't know or claiming not to know something you do know. Like humility is also a trap, like false humility, you know people call it out, you know humble-bragging, but humility is also an ego trip. You know if you're actually low-ego there's no such thing as humility any more than there is such a thing as pride. They kind of go together. So authenticity leads to a better life, again the long term, like all the virtues. So it removes the people who are around that you know are only there because they think you're someone that you're not. So you can drop the pretense. You have to walk on eggshells around them. It removes you from situations where you're having to pretend or force or struggle or work hard to stay in. And so it helps you navigate to the right answers in relationships and in situations. But there's pain involved 'cause there's change. You know, part of the thing is like yes, I'm lazy, but I will not accept bad outcomes. You know, I have an ability to walk away from suboptimal relationships or situations. Even if it's painful, I just won't stay in them.

埃里克：那是否只针对你选择去欲望的一个非常特定的子集？

纳瓦尔：它对一切都成立。

纳瓦尔：就像，那是很难的一个。我是说，我们总是掉进陷阱。现代社会有很多陷阱，多巴胺陷阱，对吧？你会被卷进政治。你会被卷进毒品使用。你会被卷进屏幕成瘾、刷屏，你知道，色情，酒精。现代社会就是有太多陷阱。他们把大麻合法化了，你知道，自由意志主义的我想说：好，同意的成年人，做你想做的任何事。但在现实中，我觉得它只是把年轻人的能量吸走。就像，如果你一直嗨着，你不可能有志向。它会字面上摧毁你去做事的能力和欲望。而且它是一块创可贴。它是一块把事情盖住的创可贴。这对社会也许没关系。它是现代的面包与马戏团，但对个体不那么好。而我并不在乎社会。我在乎个体。是的，社会必须能运转。但我觉得现代社会已经放弃了那个。我们在取自由意志主义错误的那一部分，也就是我们基本上说：你不仅有自由做任何你想做的事，而且只要它不立即伤害别人，你知道，它就应当被庆祝。但然后社会作为整体多少会散架，因为每个人都是享乐主义者。

Naval: Like, that's a hard one. I mean, we're always falling into traps. Modern society has a lot of traps, dopamine traps, right? You can get sucked into politics. You can get sucked into drug use. You can get sucked into screen addiction, scrolling, you know, porn, alcohol. There's just so many traps in modern society. They've legalized weed, which you know, the libertarian me wants to say, okay, consenting adult, do whatever you want. But in reality, I think it just sucks the energy out of young men. Like, you can't be ambitious if you're stoned all the time. It'll literally destroy your ability and desire to go and do anything. And it's a band-aid. It's a band-aid that's covering things up. Which may be fine for society. It's the modern bread and circuses, but not so good for the individual. And I don't care about society. I care about the individual. Yes, society has to be functional. But I feel like modern society's given up on that. We're taking the wrong part of libertarianism, which is we basically said not only you're free to do anything you want, but as long as it doesn't immediately hurt somebody else, you know, it should be celebrated. But then society as a whole kind of falls apart because everyone's a hedonist.

没有人对这个系统有长期利害。没有共享的伦理。没有共享的规则。没有对基本事项的理解。然后你最终得到高犯罪。你最终得到肮脏。你最终得到浪费的政府。而国家变成宗教的替代品。人们身上的宗教本能如此强烈。你知道，宗教是人们的一种合作系统，以至于如果你没有一个宗教系统，国家就会接手并创造一个。而我不认为国家的美德感和伦理比更古老的宗教好多少。你知道，更古老的宗教对美德和伦理有更好的把握。现在，它们现代化得不够快。所以它们对避孕、女性进入劳动力，以及你知道女同性恋和男同性恋权利等等适应得很差。但我觉得我们把孩子和洗澡水一起泼掉了，而且我不认为宗教本能消失了。就像人们无论怎样都会去寻找它。

No one has a long-term stake in the system. There's no shared ethos. There's no shared rules. There's no understanding of the basics. And then you end up with high crime. You end up with dirt. You end up with wasteful government. And the state becomes a substitute for religion. The religious instinct is so strong in people. You know, religion is a cooperating system for people that if you don't have a religious system, then the state will take over and create one. And I don't think the state's sense of virtue and ethics is much better than the older religions. You know, the older religions had a better grasp of virtue and ethics. Now, they didn't modernize fast enough. So they were poorly adapted towards things like contraception and women entering the workforce and, you know, lesbian and gay rights and so on. But I think we threw the baby out with the bathwater and I don't think the religious instinct is gone. Like people are going to search for it no matter what.

埃里克：你有一条很棒的推文让我发笑：现代社会的问题是无神论传到了大众那里。

Naval: Yeah, that's right. Yeah. I mean like atheism in the masses is really bad because people need an operating system and most people are not going to figure it out for themselves and it has to be a relatively common operating system. So if you don't have one you either end up with anarchy or end up with the state dictating it, which either means you're in an oligarchy or you're in a communist authoritarian hole. 埃里克：你最标志性的句子之一是：你人生的目标是找到最需要你的那些人、生意、项目或艺术。

Eric: One of your most iconic is: your goal in life is to find the people, business, project or art that needs you the most.

纳瓦尔：对，我真的觉得这是我们与演化过去大幅分道扬镳的地方之一。所以更新你的先验是重要的。而在过去，你处在一个小数目的人的部落里，处在一个小社会、一个小文明里，并且那只是你步行能遇见的人。所以做一个坏选择、多少被拒绝的后果真的很高。所以你很早结婚，你，你知道，如果你被你的追求者或那个女人拒绝，那对你就是可怕的。你的名声，你知道，完全是本地的。你必须融入。而现在正好相反。它是一个搜索功能。你可以飞到世界上任何地方。如果你足够年轻、足够有动力，你可以进入任何职业。你可以和几乎任何一群人待在一起。有无限的机会。而那令人生畏。它可怕。它实际上真的很可怕。因为然后如果你没有做成，你会抑郁，你会觉得那是你的错。但好消息是你不必留在任何糟糕的处境里。

Naval: Yeah, I really think that this is one place where we diverge from our evolutionary past in a big way. And so it's important to update your priors. And in the past, you were in a tribe of a small number of people in a small society, a small civilization, and it was just people you could meet on foot. So the consequences to making a bad choice, sort of getting rejected, were really high. So you got married early, you, you know, if you were rejected by your suitor or the woman, then it was terrible for you. Your reputation, you know, was all entirely local. You had to fit in. And now it's the exact opposite. It's a search function. You can fly anywhere in the world. You can enter any career if you're young enough and motivated enough. You can hang out with almost any set of people. There's infinite opportunity. And that's daunting. It's scary. It's actually really scary. Because then if you don't make it, you get depressed and you feel like it was your fault. But the good news is you don't have to stay in any bad situation.

好。现在，那个你想找到最适合你的东西的论点，它有两部分。第一部分是你有无限选择，那并不必然是好的。它令人紧张。而如果没有忠诚，它对社会也有点糟。如果每个人都总在跳槽，如果每个人都总在换地方，那你对你的亲缘、你的部落或你的邻里就没有忠诚。或者如果你总在换关系，对吧？你建不成任何永恒。你没有孩子。但我觉得它是：探索的范围很宽。所以如果有人拒绝了你，别哭。它就像一条糟糕的谷歌搜索结果。按返回，再搜索一次，或点下一条链接。有无限的多样性。我是说，你一生会遇见一万、两万人。而世上有 80 亿，你知道，你可以点点点，随时找到新的人去约会。就像有那么多可选性。所以，你应当广泛探索，直到找到契合。但然后生活中所有的好处都来自复利。所以，你必须投资于某样东西。你必须投资于一个地方。你必须投资于人。你必须投资于事业。你必须投资于知识。你必须投资于一份职业。所以你必须投资于你的身体健康和你的身体。所以你知道你找到对你来说感觉像玩、对别人来说看起来像工作的工作。你找到那个你独特地——你知道他们通过成为他们自己让你幸福，你通过成为你自己让他们幸福的人。你找到你可以像复利一样积累的地点。你可以建一座房子。你可以建立一个家庭。

Okay. Now, that argument where you want to find the thing that fits you the best, there's two parts of it. The first part is you have infinite choice, that's not necessarily good. It's nerve-wracking. And it's kind of bad for society if there's no loyalty. If everyone's always job-hopping, if everyone's always location-hopping, then you have no loyalty to your kinship or your tribe or your neighborhood. Or if you're always relationship-hopping, right? You don't build any permanence. You have no children. But I think what it is is there's—the exploration range is wide. So if you get rejected by someone, don't cry. It's like a bad Google search result. Hit back and just search again or click on the next link. There's infinite variety. I mean, you're going to meet 10, 20,000 people in your life. And there's 8 billion, and you know, you can click click click and find new ones to date all the time. Like there's so much optionality. So, you should explore widely until you find a fit. But then all the benefits in life come from compound interest. So, you have to invest in something. You have to invest in a place. You have to invest in people. You have to invest in business. You have to invest in knowledge. You have to invest in a career. So you have to invest in your physical health and your body. So you know you find the work that feels like play to you and looks like work to others. You find the person who you uniquely—you know they make you happy by being who they are and you make them happy by being who you are. You find the location where you can like compound. You can build a house. You can build a household.

你周围有你喜欢和信任的人。天气很重要，信不信由你。我是说这相当平淡，但它重要。这就是为什么人们那么多谈论它。你必须找到那样一样你可以只管投资进去的东西，然后你得到复利。所以有一个探索阶段，有一个利用阶段。利用是军事意义上的利用，不是，你知道，奴隶制意义上的。执行阶段——或者说探索阶段和执行阶段，以及或者说投资阶段。探索，然后投资。

You have people around you that you like and trust. The weather matters a lot, believe it or not. I mean it's pretty prosaic but it matters. That's why people talk about it so much. You have to find the thing into which you can just invest and then you get compound interest. So there's an exploration phase and there's the exploitation phase. Exploitation in the military sense, not in the, you know, slavery sense. Execution phase—or exploration phase and execution phase, and or investment phase. Exploration and then investment.

所以你探索、探索、探索，直到你找出什么是适合你的正确契合，然后投资于它几乎不费力，然后一旦你开始投资，你就得到复利回报。两个阶段你都不能跳过。所以在某些情形里我们探索得太少。通常在像找工作里我们探索得太少。你知道我们会花像——我理解，有时你是受财务约束——但你知道人们会花三周找一份工作，然后在那份工作里待五年。那是探索太少、投资太多。但然后另一边，就像人们会单身到 50 岁，然后像：哦，现在我需要安定下来。好，探索太多而投资不够，对吧？或者决定就像无论什么理由生孩子太晚了，太固守自己的方式，或过了生育年龄。所以这些之间有一种平衡，但我觉得我会说现代社会让探索容易了很多。所以，你应当只是意识到好处仍然全在投资里。如果你从不投资于任何东西，那是一种空洞的生活。那是一种没有灵魂的生活。你知道，你会以糟糕的方式退出。

So you explore, explore, explore until you find out what is the right fit for you, and then it's almost effortless to invest in it, and then once you start investing then you get the compound-interest returns. You can't skip either phase. So in some situations we explore too little. Usually in like job hunting we explore too little. You know we'll spend like—and I get it, sometimes you're doing it by financial constraints—but you know people will spend 3 weeks looking for a job and they'll be in the job for 5 years. That's too little exploration and too much investment. But then on the other side, like people will stay single till they're 50 and then be like, oh, now I need to settle down. Well, too much exploration and not enough investment, right? Or decide like it's too late to have kids for whatever reason, too set in their ways or past the biological age. So there's a balance to these, but I think I would say modern society has made exploration a lot easier. And so, you should just realize that the benefits are still all in investment. If you never invest in anything, it's an empty life. It's a soulless life. You know, you'll exit badly.

埃里克：两者之间的转换感觉像是轮胎真正碰到路面的地方。你知道，它是避开某种局部极大，或者理解你是否真的处在一个真正扎根并投资的正确位置。

Eric: The transition between the two feels like where the rubber really meets the road. You know, it's avoiding kind of a local maximum, or understanding whether you're actually in the right place to really root down and invest.

纳瓦尔：对。我觉得这就是经验派上用场的地方，对吧？所以，我们怎样学习？我们不是通过时间学习。那就是我觉得马尔科姆·格拉德威尔错了的地方。不是一万小时。好吗？他在那一点上完全错了。我是说，我理解他从哪里出发。我不是在批评格拉德威尔，但它已经变成一个迷因，对吧？这一万小时。它真正是的是一万次迭代。而迭代是什么？显然不是一万，但重复的迭代是我们学习的方式。而迭代是当你——它不是重复。它非常刻意。不要用重复这个词。迭代意味着你做某件事，然后你诚实地反思结果，你做一个改变，然后再试一次。然后你诚实地反思结果，你做一个改变，然后再试一次。你在任何事业里把这个做足够多次，它就会发展你的判断力。然后一旦你的判断力发展得足够好，你就会开始不去尝试一大堆事情。你会拒绝一大堆事情，直到最后你说：好，其实，我只想和这种人在一起，或者我只想住在这种地方，或者我只想做这种事。就是这样。现在你完成了。现在你可以开始投资。看，顺便说，所有这些东西都是事后追溯的推理。我在做的时候从未真正把这个想通。就像回头看、看见那个模式真的很容易。你向前走时很难看见它。

Naval: Yeah. I think this is where the experience comes in handy, right? So, how do we learn? We don't learn through time. That's where I think Malcolm Gladwell was wrong. It's not 10,000 hours. Okay? He's completely wrong about that. I mean, I get where he's coming from. I'm not criticizing Gladwell, but it's become a meme, right? This 10,000 hours. What it really is is 10,000 iterations. And what are iterations? It's not 10,000 obviously, but repeated iterations is how we learn. And iterations are when you—it's not repetitions. It's very deliberate. Do not use the word repetitions. Iteration means you do something, then you honestly reflect upon the outcome, you make a change, and you try again. Then you honestly reflect upon the outcome, you make a change, and you try again. You do this enough times in any endeavor, it will develop your judgment. And then once your judgment is well-developed enough, you will start not trying a whole bunch of things. You will reject a whole bunch of things until finally you say, well, actually, I just want to be with this kind of person, or I just want to live in this kind of place, or I just want to do this kind of thing. And that's it. Now you're done. Now you can start investing. Look, by the way, all this stuff is ex post facto reasoning. I never really thought this through while I was doing it. It's like really easy to look back and see the pattern. It's very hard to see it when you're moving forward.

对。我们谈的任何东西，我猜它只会在某个人自己经历过那个体验、并且他们像「啊，现在我有一个词来称呼它」时引起共鸣。然后他们可以为它附上一个词，那帮助他们以后更多地记住它。但你不能纯粹在抽象里教某人，因为他们不知道它何时适用于他们的处境。很多时候人们在 Twitter 或 Reddit 上向我征求建议，而它就像太宽泛了。就像我不在你的处境里。我不能给你那个答案。我就是不知道。对，我能给你的只是一条一般原则，它也许适用也许不适用，对吧？所以，这相当不令人满意，但这是真理。但这就是我喜欢像 Air Chat 以及当年 Clubhouse 这类平台的地方：有人可以问你一个问题，你可以和他们深入进去。你可以问他们下一层的问题，下一层的问题，下一层的问题，直到你找到：好，那么，如果我处在一个根据你告诉我的情况，我会这样处理。

Yeah. Anything that we talk about, my guess is it will only resonate with somebody if they went through the experience themselves and they're like, ah, now I have a word for it. And then they can attach a word for it and that helps them remember it more later. But you can't teach someone purely in the abstract because they don't know when it applies to their situation. A lot of times people ask me advice on Twitter or on Reddit and it's like way too broad. It's like I'm not in your situation. I can't give you the answer. I just don't know. Yeah, all I can give you is a general principle which may or may not apply, right? So, this is pretty unsatisfying, but it's the truth. But this is where I like platforms like Air Chat and Clubhouse back in the day, where someone could ask you a question, you could get into it with them. You could ask them the next-level question, the next-level question, the next-level question, until you find like, okay, well, if I was in a situation given what you've told me, here's how I would handle it.

埃里克：对，你大概可以在两个层面上进入它，也就是一般的或具体的，但中间的任何东西都真的很难——

Eric: Yeah, there's kind of two levels you can engage on it, which is the general or the specific, but anything in the middle is really difficult to—

纳瓦尔：对。一般的要求人们自己去应用它。所以这些只是心智挂钩，你知道也许会引起共鸣，也许不会。

Naval: Yeah. The general requires people to apply it themselves. So these are just mental hooks and you know may resonate, may not.

埃里克：我们谈过的一件事是：环境多少是在你念头的上游，环境决定了你想什么、你怎样想的那么多，但开悟的心多少可以选择它自己的环境，所以对那个拥有能动性。

Eric: One thing we talked about is the environment being sort of upstream of your thoughts, and environment determining so much of what you think, how you think, but that the enlightened mind sort of can choose its own environment, and so taking agency over that.

纳瓦尔：对。有一个反馈回路，对吧？作为活着的生物，我们注定要适应。我们适应我们的环境，并且我们在局部逆转熵，好让我们的环境更可控。但那的第一步是有意识地选择你的环境。所以这就是为什么在关系上我多少在说：不要依附于一段特定的关系，即便它并不适合你。你并没有某种义务去硬撑过去，除非你有孩子或类似的情况，那就不同了，对吧？你可以改变你的关系。你可以策划你的关系，然后一旦你处在正确的关系里，就会容易很多，因为你们彼此更契合。所以，我觉得这也多少进入我的推文：智力唯一真正的检验是你是否从生活中得到了你想要的，对吧？而那是我为自己最喜欢的句子之一，因为它是一个两部分的检验。一部分是选择想要什么，然后得到它。它不只是像：哦，我想成为一个身高六英尺八英寸的篮球运动员。那不会发生。所以你必须想要正确的东西，那些甚至可达成的东西，但又多少处在你可达成范围的边缘，并且达成它们的过程不会让你痛苦。所以你选择想要什么，然后你去得到你想要的，然后如果你选对了东西并且得到了它，那就是能够判定你是否聪明的唯一外部有效信号，对吧？所以我觉得相关的是：你选择你的环境，然后你改造你的环境。但不要只是困在你的环境里。不要只是去适应它。就像现代社会最伟大的礼物之一是，你知道，你拥有的第一项智商测试是：你是否留在你出生的地方，对吧？就像很多人最终困在他们出生的地方，也许那是对他们最优的地方，但通常我猜不是。通常我猜外面有某个地方对他们更好。它也许是隔壁那个镇子。它也许是另一个国家。它可以是任何东西。但人们想要更好的生活，而第一步是你为了那更好的生活去哪里。所以那是选择你的环境的一部分。但即便那时，我觉得你必须做点什么。

Naval: Yeah. There's a feedback loop, right? We are meant to adapt as living creatures. We adapt to our environment and we locally reverse entropy to make our environment more controllable. But the first step of that is choosing your environment consciously. And so that's why on relationships I was kind of saying don't get attached to a specific relationship even if it's not working for you. You don't have some obligation to struggle through unless you have kids or something, then it's different, right? You can change your relationship. You can curate your relationship, and then once you're in the right relationship, it's a lot easier because you're a better fit for each other. So, I think this kind of also goes into my tweet a little bit like the only true test of intelligence is if you get what you want out of life, right? And that was one of my favorites for myself because it's a two-part test. One is choosing what to want and then getting it. It's not just like, oh, I want to be a 6'8" tall basketball player. It's not going to happen. So you have to want the right things, things that are even achievable, but kind of at the edge of your range of achievability, and where the process of achieving them won't make you miserable. And so you choose what to want and then you go and get what you want, and then if you chose the right things and you got it, then that's the only external valid signal that can determine are you intelligent or not, right? So I think related, you choose your environment and then you adapt your environment. But don't just get stuck in your environment. Don't just adapt to it. Like one of the greatest gifts in modern society is that, you know, the first IQ test you have is do you stay where you were born, right? Like a lot of people end up stuck where they're born, and maybe it was the optimal place for them, but usually I would guess not. Usually I would guess there was some place out there that was better for them. It might be the next town over. It might be another country. It could be anything. But people want better lives and the first step is where do you go for that better life. So that is part of choosing your environment. But even then I feel like you have to do something.

我不认为你可以只作为一个建议给予者过完一生。你知道这对我感觉是假的。这对我感觉是虚假的。一种理想的生活是：你为自己和你的孩子写作，你做有用的、美的和真的事情。你为自己学习，而就你给予人们建议的程度而言，那是因为他们问了你，并且他们问了你某种具体的东西，并且它能有帮助。你不是为了成为一个建议给予者而给予建议。我最不喜欢的风投是那种像是说：我们是公司建造者。我们是团队背后的团队。就像，不，你们不是。创始人建造了公司。你知道，他们承担了全部风险。你在拿走一点点功劳。你拿别人的资本冒险，殷实的资本。而没错，你是一个好的挑选者。外面有少数风真的会介入，真的会卷起袖子，真的会把它当成自己的事。但那样的人很少。他们非常、非常少，并且他们不是你会想到的那些更出名的人。

I don't think you can go through life as just an advice giver. You know it feels fake to me. It feels false to me. An ideal life is one where you write for yourself and your children and you do things that are useful and beautiful and true. You learn for yourself, and to the extent that you're giving people advice, it's because they asked you and they asked you something specific and it can be helpful. You're not giving advice to become an advice giver. My least favorite VC is the type who like says, we're company builders. We're the team behind the team. It's like, no, you're not. The founder built the company. You know, they took all the risk. You're taking a little bit of credit. You risk somebody else's capital, healthy capital. And yes, you were a good picker. There are a few VCs out there who really do get involved and who really do roll up their sleeves and who really do take it personally. But there are few. They're very, very few, and they're not the more famous ones that you would think about.

埃里克：好，那就是你的 Twitter 独特的地方：你实际上没有花任何力气去积累追随者。你只是分享写给自己的笔记，而对，人们我多少觉得看见了其中的真实性以及它的价值。我看见你关于嫉妒的一些想法——那我觉得特别有用——和你所说的智力的真正游戏、或智力的真正检验是你是否从生活中得到你想要的，这两者之间有一种关联。我发现如果你通过了那个检验，如果你得到了你独特想要的东西，还有什么好嫉妒的？你为什么会羡慕任何别人？

Eric: Well, that's what's unique about your Twitter, is that you've exerted zero effort to actually accrue a following. 纳瓦尔：那是非常好的一点。它们确实会合到一起。话虽如此，就像要通过那个检验非常难。我们的欲望通常超过我们的能力，而我们有一种一得到想要的东西就制造出新欲望的方式。但对，我必须提醒自己选择激励而不是嫉妒。你知道当你看见某个人成功地做某件事，我试图不要成为的是：我试图不要嫉妒，意思是地球上没有一个人是我会拿我的生活去交换的，没有一个，即便这些开悟的存在也不是。没有任何人是我会做一次彻底的整体替换的，对吧？所以在那个意义上嫉妒并不真实。但当你看某个成功的人，你可以说，好我是说有那种评论，你可以像：好，他们不配，他们走运了。好吧当然，那里有那样的成分，而对有些人那种成分比别人多得多，或者他们在这件事上不道德，那很不幸。所以那些是真的，但固着于那些并不好，就像我们一开始讨论过的：如果你固着于生活中所有的不公，你永远做不成任何事。所以只是在非常实际的基础上，你想找到那有激励性的部分。所以比如说，我在做一家公司的原因之一是，你知道，我被像埃隆和彼得·蒂尔这样的人激励。而埃隆激励我，是因为他做这些公司，并且你知道，他猛冲，他承担巨大的项目，他做困难的事。彼得·蒂尔有点不同。就像蒂尔在谈你应当做重要的事，你知道，创造你想生活于其中的那个确定的未来。但我并没有看见他自己在做那件事，对吧？他是一个投资者和哲学家。所以我可以只做那种人。我可以只做一个投资者兼哲学家。但我选择被他所说所做里最好的那部分激励，并且我多少忽略其余的，那并不是说其余的有什么错，我只是喜欢激励那部分。

Naval: That's a very good point. They do coalesce together. That said, like it's very hard to pass that test. Our desires usually outstrip our capabilities and we have a way of manufacturing new ones as soon as we get what we want. But yeah, I have to remind myself to choose inspiration over envy. But if I had to look for like the pure inspiration, the guy that I never envied, I never swapped with him, but I was super inspired by Steve Jobs. Even when he was alive—it's easy to be inspired and not envious when people are dead, I swear, right? But even when he was alive, like I think somebody said this the other day. I know Elon's pretty reviled now because of the whole thing with the administration and politics. But there is no single cultural leader where somebody looks at that person and says, yeah, I want my life to be that, right? This person's building something incredible.
